UPSC PYQ's
Detailed Explantions

MODERN
HISTORY

From 1979-2025

UPSC PYQs - 1979
Q1. Who said first : “Swaraj is my birth right and I
shall have it”.
(a) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
(b) M.K. Gandhi
(c) Lala Lajpat Rai
(d) Sardar Patel
The correct option is (a).
• This iconic slogan was coined and popularized
by Bal Gangadhar Tilak, a prominent leader of
the Indian independence movement.
• Tilak was a key figure in the "extremist"
faction of the Indian National Congress and a
strong advocate for Swaraj, or self-rule.
• His declaration, "Swaraj is my birthright, and
I shall have it!," became a powerful rallying
cry for the nationalist movement, inspiring
millions of Indians to demand complete
independence from British rule.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q2. “India Wins Freedom” was written by
(a) Rajendra Prasad
(b) Maulana Abul Kalam Azad
(c) R.N. Tagore
(d) J.L. Nehru
The correct option is (b).
• India Wins Freedom is an autobiographical
account of the Indian independence
movement written by Maulana Abul Kalam
Azad.

•

•

•

Azad was a senior leader of the Indian
National Congress and the first Minister of
Education in independent India.
The book provides a firsthand perspective on
the political events leading up to India's
independence, including the negotiations for
partition.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q3. First Europeans, who started trade with India,
were
(a) The Portuguese
(b) The British
(c) The French
(d) The Dutch
The correct option is (a).
• The Portuguese were the first Europeans to
establish a direct sea route to India and
initiate trade in the modern colonial era.
• The Portuguese explorer Vasco da Gama
successfully reached Calicut on the
southwestern coast of India in 1498.
• Following this, the Portuguese established
several trading posts and colonies, such as
Goa, marking the beginning of European
commercial and political involvement in
India.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q4. The Home Rule movement was launched by
(a) Annie Besant
(b) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
(c) Mahatma Gandhi
(d) Lala Lajpat Rai
The correct option is (a).
• The Home Rule Movement in India was
organized on the lines of the Irish Home Rule
League and aimed to achieve selfgovernment for India within the British
Empire.
• The movement was led by two prominent
leaders: Annie Besant and Bal Gangadhar
Tilak.
• While both were central figures, Annie Besant
played a pivotal role in popularizing the idea

•

and establishing the All-India Home Rule
League in September 1916, following Tilak's
league which was set up in April 1916. The
question's options and historical context often
credit Besant as the primary launcher of the
broader, all-India movement.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q5. The cause for the immediate precipitation of the
Sepoy Mutiny was
(a) Use of cartridges greased with cow fat
(b) Doctrine of Lapse
(c) The disparity between salaries of Native Sepoys
and the British Soldiers
(d) The Spread of Christianity
The correct option is (a).
• While there were many underlying political,
economic, and social causes for the Revolt of
1857, the immediate trigger was the
introduction of the new Enfield rifle.
• A rumor spread among the sepoys (Indian
soldiers) that the paper cartridges for this
rifle were greased with a mixture of cow and
pig fat.
• To load the rifle, soldiers had to bite off the
end of the cartridge. This was deeply
offensive to both Hindu soldiers, who revere
cows, and Muslim soldiers, for whom pigs are
forbidden. This act of religious sacrilege was
the spark that ignited the widespread mutiny.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q6. Gandhiji started Dandi March in 1930
(a) Against imposition of salt tax laws
(b) Against the announcement of communal award
(c) Against atrocities committed on Harijans
(d) Against all of the above
The correct option is (a).
• The Dandi March, also known as the Salt
March, was a major act of nonviolent civil
disobedience led by Mahatma Gandhi.
• The march was a direct protest against the
British monopoly on salt production and the
imposition of a salt tax, which affected every
Indian, especially the poor.

•

•

By marching to the coastal village of Dandi
and making salt from seawater, Gandhi and
his followers symbolically and defiantly broke
the British salt laws, launching the
nationwide Civil Disobedience Movement.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q7. Gandhiji’s “Champaran Movement” was for
(a) The security of rights of Harijans
(b) Civil disobedience movement
(c) Maintaining the unity of Hindu society
(d) Solving the problem of the Indigo workers
The correct option is (d).
• The Champaran Satyagraha of 1917 was
Mahatma Gandhi's first major act of civil
disobedience in India.
• The movement was launched to address the
grievances of the tenant farmers and indigo
workers in the Champaran district of Bihar.
• These farmers were being forced by European
planters to cultivate indigo on a portion of
their land under the oppressive tinkathia
system and were paid extremely low prices,
leading to immense hardship. Gandhi's
intervention led to an inquiry and eventual
abolition of the system.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q8. Who commented “the Cripps Mission was a post
dated cheque on a crashing bank”?
(a) Mahatma Gandhi
(b) Jawaharlal Nehru
(c) Subhash Chandra Bose
(d) Sardar Patel
The correct option is (a).
• The Cripps Mission of 1942 offered India
Dominion Status after World War II in
exchange for support during the war.
• Mahatma Gandhi famously dismissed this
offer with the metaphor that it was a "postdated cheque on a crashing bank."
• The "post-dated" part referred to the promise
of freedom only after the war, and the
"crashing bank" referred to the declining
power and credibility of the British Empire,

•

which was suffering major defeats in the war
at that time.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q9. “Khilafat” movement subsided because of the
(a) Concessions given to Muslims by the British
(b) Amity achieved between Congress and Muslim
League
(c) Accession of Kamal Pasha on the throne of Turkey
(d) None of the above
The correct option is (c).
• The Khilafat Movement was launched by
Indian Muslims to protest the harsh
treatment of the Ottoman Sultan (Caliph) by
the British after World War I.
• The movement lost its primary reason for
existence when Turkish nationalists, led by
Mustafa Kemal Pasha (Atatürk), rose to
power in Turkey.
• In 1922, the Turkish nationalists abolished the
Ottoman sultanate and, in 1924, officially
abolished the institution of the Caliphate
itself. With the Caliphate gone, the central
issue of the Khilafat movement became
irrelevant, and it quickly subsided.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q10. The aim of the Cripps Mission to India was to
(a) Appease the Indian public opinion
(b) Appease the American people
(c) Decentralise the power to States
(d) None of the above
The correct option is (a).
• The Cripps Mission was sent to India in 1942
primarily to secure full Indian cooperation
and support for the British war effort in
World War II.
• To achieve this, the mission's aim was to
appease Indian public opinion by offering
constitutional proposals that included the
promise of Dominion Status and a
constituent assembly after the war.
• While there was pressure from allies like the
USA, the direct aim was to win over the
Indian political leadership and populace.

•

Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q11. The Lucknow Congress Session of 1916 refers to
(a) Concession of separate electorates for the Muslims
by the Congress Party
(b) Merger of Muslim League into Congress
(c) Selection of Muslim leader as the Congress
President
(d) None of the above
The correct option is (a).
• The Lucknow Session of 1916 is historic for
two main reasons: the reunion of the
moderate and extremist factions of the
Congress, and the signing of the Lucknow
Pact between the Congress and the Muslim
League.
• A key component of the Lucknow Pact was
the concession by the Congress Party to
accept the principle of separate electorates
for Muslims, which had been granted by the
British in the Morley-Minto Reforms of 1909.
• This was a major step towards Hindu-Muslim
political cooperation at the time, though it
had long-term implications.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q12. Rabindranath Tagore surrendered his title to
the British because of
(a) Civil Disobedience Movement
(b) Non-Cooperation Movement
(c) Jallianwalah Bagh massacre
(d) Partition of Bengal
The correct option is (c).

•
•

•

•

Rabindranath Tagore was awarded a
knighthood by the British Crown in 1915.
He was profoundly shocked and horrified by
the Jallianwala Bagh massacre in Amritsar on
April 13, 1919, where British troops fired on
an unarmed crowd.
In protest against this brutal act, Tagore
wrote a letter to the Viceroy, Lord
Chelmsford, renouncing his knighthood as a
symbolic gesture of solidarity with his
countrymen.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q13. The Interim Government at the Centre after
independence was formed
(a) After the visit of Cripps Mission
(b) Before the visit of Cripps Mission
(c) After Mountbatten submitted his plan
(d) Before the visit of the Cabinet Mission
The correct option is (a).
• The Cripps Mission came in 1942 and failed.
• The Cabinet Mission came to India in 1946
with a plan for the transfer of power, which
included the formation of a Constituent
Assembly and an Interim Government.
• Following the Cabinet Mission's proposals
and the subsequent elections to the
Constituent Assembly, the Interim
Government, headed by Jawaharlal Nehru,
was formed on September 2, 1946.
• This was well after the Cripps Mission and
before Mountbatten's plan of 1947. The most
logical sequence places it after the Cripps
Mission.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q14. Who was the Prime Minister of U.K. at the time
of India’s Independence?
(a) Lord Attlee
(b) Winston Churchill
(c) Lord Mountbatten
(d) Harold Wilson
The correct option is (a).
• India gained independence on August 15,
1947.

•

•

•

At that time, the Prime Minister of the United
Kingdom was Clement Attlee, the leader of
the Labour Party.
Attlee's government, which came to power in
July 1945 after defeating Winston Churchill's
Conservative Party, presided over the process
of granting independence to India. Lord
Mountbatten was the Viceroy of India, not
the Prime Minister of the U.K.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q15. Who led the extremists before the arrival of
Gandhiji on the political scene for freedom struggle?
(a) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
(b) Dadabhai Naoroji
(c) Gopal Krishna Gokhale
(d) Subhash Bose
The correct option is (a).
• Before Mahatma Gandhi became the
preeminent leader of the Indian National
Congress, the nationalist movement had two
main factions: the Moderates and the
Extremists.
• The Extremist faction, which advocated for
more assertive and radical methods to
achieve Swaraj, was led by the triumvirate of
"Lal-Bal-Pal" (Lala Lajpat Rai, Bal Gangadhar
Tilak, and Bipin Chandra Pal).
• Of these, Bal Gangadhar Tilak was the most
prominent and influential leader of the
Extremist group. Dadabhai Naoroji and Gopal
Krishna Gokhale were leading Moderates.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q16. The French supremacy in India came to an end
with the
(a) Battle of Wandiwash
(b) Battle of Plassey
(c) Battle of Buxar
(d) Battle of Buxar
The correct option is (a).
• The Anglo-French rivalry for control over
India culminated in the Third Carnatic War
(1756-1763).

•
•

•

The decisive conflict of this war was the
Battle of Wandiwash, fought in 1760.
In this battle, the British forces under Sir Eyre
Coote decisively defeated the French forces
under Comte de Lally. This victory shattered
French power in India and effectively ended
their ambitions of establishing an empire,
paving the way for British supremacy.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q17. Indian National Congress took the stand during
Second World War that
(a) It would support axis powers
(b) It would support allied powers
(c) It would cooperate with the British if India is
promised dominion status after the war
(d) It would cooperate with the British if India is
granted complete Independence
The correct option is (d).
• The Indian National Congress was opposed to
the fascist ideology of the Axis powers.
• However, it refused to unconditionally
support the British war effort, arguing that a
country fighting for freedom abroad could
not deny freedom to India itself.
• The Congress declared that it would
cooperate with the British in the war only if
India was immediately declared independent
and a provisional national government was
formed. The demand was for complete
independence, not just Dominion Status.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q18. “Inquilab Zindabad” slogan was given by
(a) Chandra Shekhar Azad
(b) Subhash Chandra Bose
(c) Bhagat Singh
(d) Iqbal
The correct option is (c).
• The slogan "Inquilab Zindabad," which
translates to "Long Live the Revolution," was
popularized by the revolutionary freedom
fighter Bhagat Singh.
• He and his associate, B. K. Dutt, shouted this
slogan after throwing bombs in the Central

•

•

Legislative Assembly in Delhi in 1929 to
protest against repressive British laws.
The slogan was originally coined by the Urdu
poet and freedom fighter Maulana Hasrat
Mohani, but it was Bhagat Singh who made it
a famous rallying cry of the independence
movement.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q19. Which of the following was not the outcome of
Jallianwalah Bagh massacre?
(a) Suspension of Gen. Dyer
(b) Change in Gandhiji’s outlook towards Britishers
(c) Temporary peace in Punjab
(d) Renunciation of British titles and positions by
many Indians
The correct option is (c).
• The Jallianwala Bagh massacre did not lead to
peace in Punjab; on the contrary, it
intensified nationalist anger and led to
further unrest and repression under martial
law.
• The other options were direct outcomes:
General Dyer was eventually removed from
active service (suspension) following the
Hunter Committee inquiry.
• The massacre shattered Gandhiji's faith in
British justice and fairness, pushing him
towards the Non-Cooperation Movement.
• Many Indians, most famously Rabindranath
Tagore, renounced their British titles in
protest.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q20. “Red Shirts” movement aimed at
(a) To throw out Britishers from India
(b) To promote Communist organizational activities
(c) To promote trade union activities
(d) All of the above
The correct option is (a).
• The "Red Shirts" movement was the popular
name for the Khudai Khidmatgar ("Servants
of God") movement.
• It was a non-violent resistance movement led
by Khan Abdul Ghaffar Khan among the

•

•

Pashtuns of the North-West Frontier
Province.
The primary aim of the movement was to
oppose British colonial rule and work for a
united, independent India. Its ultimate goal
was to throw the Britishers out of India
through non-violent means.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q21. Who evolved the national consciousness as a
formal concept?
(a) B.G. Tilak
(b) Mahatma Gandhi
(c) Jawaharlal Nehru
(d) Surendranath Bannerjee
The correct option is (d).
• Surendranath Bannerjee was one of the
earliest and most important pioneers of
Indian nationalism.
• He founded the Indian National Association
in 1876, a precursor to the Indian National
Congress, with the explicit aim of creating an
all-India political platform and fostering a
sense of shared national identity.
• Through his speeches, writings (including his
newspaper 'The Bengalee'), and
organizational efforts, he played a crucial role
in formally articulating and spreading the
concept of a unified national consciousness
among Indians. His autobiography is titled "A
Nation in the Making."
• Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q22. Why could British only succeed in trade and
commerce in India?
(a) Because of government backing
(b) Quality of merchandise

(c) Naval superiority
(d) All of the above
The correct option is (c).
• While government backing and trade policies
were factors, the single most decisive
advantage the British had over their
European rivals (like the Portuguese, Dutch,
and French) and Indian rulers was their naval
superiority.
• Control of the sea lanes allowed the British
East India Company to protect its trade
routes, transport troops and supplies
efficiently, blockade its enemies, and project
power along India's coastline.
• This naval dominance was the key that
unlocked their eventual commercial and
political success in India.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q23. Who participated the least in the Indian
National Movement?
(a) Capitalists
(b) Landlords and Merchants
(c) Princes of States
(d) Government officials
The correct option is (c).
• The Princes of the native States generally
participated the least in the Indian National
Movement and often actively opposed it.
• After the Revolt of 1857, the British adopted
a policy of protecting the princes and using
them as a bulwark against nationalism. The
princes' power and position were guaranteed
by the British Crown, so they had a vested
interest in maintaining the colonial status
quo.
• While some individual princes were
sympathetic, as a class, they remained aloof
from or hostile to the freedom struggle.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q24. The Prime Minister of Interim Government of
India after Independence was
(a) Gandhi
(b) Nehru

(c) Jinnah
(d) Rajgopalachari
The correct option is (b).
• The Interim Government was formed on
September 2, 1946, before independence, to
oversee the transition of power.
• Jawaharlal Nehru was the Vice President of
the Viceroy's Executive Council and held the
powers of the Prime Minister in this
government.
• After India became independent on August
15, 1947, he continued in this role and
officially became the first Prime Minister of
India.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q25. Swadeshi movement started during
(a) Anti-Bengal partition movement
(b) Non-Cooperation Movement
(c) Civil Disobedience Movement
(d) None of the above
The correct option is (a).
• The Swadeshi movement was formally
launched in 1905 as a direct response to the
British decision to partition Bengal.
• It was an integral part of the Anti-Bengal
Partition Movement.
• The movement called for the boycott of
British goods and the promotion and use of
"Swadeshi" or Indian-made goods to hurt
British economic interests and foster Indian
self-reliance.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q26. Aim of Swaraj Party was to
(a) Enter the Legislative Councils by contesting
elections in order to wreck the legislatures from
within
(b) Boycott the foreign goods
(c) Launch a non-cooperation movement against the
British
(d) Adopt Swadeshi in piece goods on a vast scale
The correct option is (a).
• The Swaraj Party was formed in 1923 by C.R.
Das and Motilal Nehru after a split in the

•

•

•

Congress following the suspension of the
Non-Cooperation Movement.
Their main political strategy, known as
"Council-entry," was to contest elections to
the legislative councils established by the
British.
Their stated aim was not to cooperate with
the government but to obstruct its
functioning from within, expose its
undemocratic nature, and use the councils as
a platform for nationalist propaganda,
thereby aiming to wreck the legislatures from
within.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q27. Swaraj Party was founded by
(a) Motilal Nehru
(b) Jawaharlal Nehru
(c) B.G. Tilak
(d) C. Rajagopalachari
The correct option is (a).
• The Swaraj Party was founded in January
1923 following the Gaya session of the
Congress.
• Its principal founders were Chittaranjan Das
(C.R. Das), who served as its president, and
Motilal Nehru, who served as its secretary.
• They were the leaders of the "pro-changer"
faction within the Congress who advocated
for entering the legislative councils.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q28. Which party was in power in U.K. when India
became independent?
(a) Labour
(b) Conservative

(c) Liberal
(d) None of these
The correct option is (a).
• In the UK general election of July 1945, the
Labour Party, led by Clement Attlee, won a
landslide victory, defeating Winston
Churchill's Conservative Party.
• It was this Labour government under Prime
Minister Clement Attlee that was in power
when India became independent on August
15, 1947.
• The Labour government initiated the process
of transferring power, leading to India's
independence.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.

(c) M. K. Gandhi
(d) B. G. Tilak
Answer: (a)
• The Servants of India Society was founded in
1905 in Pune, Maharashtra.
• Its founder was Gopal Krishna Gokhale, a
prominent leader of the moderate faction of
the Indian National Congress and a mentor to
Mahatma Gandhi.
• The society's aim was to train a dedicated
cadre of national missionaries for the service
of India, focusing on social welfare,
education, and the upliftment of the
underprivileged, without regard to caste or
creed.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 1980
Q1. The year 1919 is associated with
(a) Dandi March by Mahatma Gandhi
(b) Jallianwala Bagh Tragedy
(c) Chauri Chaura Incident
(d) Partition of Bengal
Answer: (b)
• The year 1919 is indelibly marked in Indian
history by the Jallianwala Bagh Tragedy
(Massacre), which occurred on April 13, 1919.
• British troops under the command of General
Dyer fired upon a large, unarmed crowd of
peaceful protestors and pilgrims in Amritsar,
Punjab.
• This event was a direct consequence of the
protests against the repressive Rowlatt Act,
also passed in 1919. The other events listed
occurred in different years: the Partition of
Bengal was in 1905, the Chauri Chaura
incident in 1922, and the Dandi March in
1930.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q2. Who was the founder of the Servants of India
Society?
(a) G. K. Gokhale
(b) K. M. Roy

Q3. Who introduced the ‘Civil Services’ in India?
(a) Lord Dalhousie
(b) Lord Curzon
(c) Lord Wellesley
(d) Lord Cornwallis
Answer: (d)
• Lord Cornwallis, Governor-General of India
from 1786 to 1793, is credited with organizing
and reforming the administration of the East
India Company, laying the foundation for the
Indian Civil Service (ICS).
• He separated the commercial and
administrative functions of the Company,
increased salaries of officials to curb
corruption, and established a system based
on merit.
• His reforms professionalized the
administration and created the structure that
evolved into the modern civil services in
India.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q4. Why did India industrialise only gradually in the
time of Britishers?
(a) Capitalists helped to set up new industries
(b) Many technicians came from different parts of the
world to set up new industries
(c) Britishers seized and handicapped Indian cottage

industries
(d) People were fond of new machine made goods
Answer: (c)
• The slow pace of industrialization in India
during British rule was a direct result of
colonial economic policies that prioritized the
interests of Britain.
• The British systematically handicapped Indian
cottage industries, especially the textile
industry, through discriminatory tariffs and
the flooding of the Indian market with cheap,
machine-made goods from Britain.
• This process, known as de-industrialization,
destroyed India's traditional manufacturing
base and transformed it into a supplier of raw
materials for British factories and a market
for their finished products, thereby stunting
indigenous industrial growth.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q5. Gandhiji opposed the untouchability and he
wanted
(a) The Harijans to revolt against it
(b) The people of India to give treatment of equality
to the untouchables
(c) Untouchability to be declared a crime under law
(d) A social revolution to create a society based on
equality
Answer: (b)
• Mahatma Gandhi's campaign against
untouchability was rooted in a deep moral
and spiritual conviction. He considered
untouchability a "sin" and a "blot" on
Hinduism.
• His primary approach was to appeal to the
conscience of caste Hindus, urging them to
reform their attitudes and behavior. He
wanted the people of India to give treatment
of equality to the untouchables, whom he
called "Harijans" or "children of God."
• While he also supported legal and social
reforms, his core message was one of moral
transformation and the acceptance of
Harijans as equal members of society.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q6. Which of the following was the main feature of
the policy of Dual Government of British rulers in
India?
(a) The English collected the revenues with the help of
Indian Officials and they became virtually the head of
the civil and military administration
(b) The general administrative body consisted of two
categories of rulers
(c) Main items of administration were in the hands of
local kings and rest were in the hands of British rulers
(d) Some portions of land were ruled by the local kings
and rest by the British rulers
Answer: (a)
• The Dual Government system was introduced
in Bengal by Robert Clive in 1765.
• Under this system, the East India Company
acquired the Diwani (right to collect revenue)
while the Nizamat (civil and criminal
administration) was nominally left in the
hands of the Nawab of Bengal.
• In practice, the Company controlled
everything. The English collected the
revenues (using Indian officials as
intermediaries) and held the real military and
administrative power, while the Nawab was a
figurehead with responsibility but no
authority. This allowed the British to wield
power without shouldering the direct
responsibility of governance.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q7. Which of the following is not a tenet of Gandhian
Socialism?
(a) Social justice
(b) Concern for the poor but not hatred for the rich
(c) Equality of opportunities of all
(d) Nationalisation of all means of production and
distribution
Answer: (d)
• Gandhian Socialism, often termed
"Sarvodaya" (welfare of all), is based on
principles of truth, non-violence, and social
justice.
• It emphasizes social justice, concern for the
poor, and equality of opportunity.

•

•

However, it is fundamentally different from
state socialism or communism. Gandhi did
not advocate for the nationalisation of all
means of production. Instead, he proposed
the concept of Trusteeship, where the
wealthy would hold their excess wealth in
trust for the benefit of society, acting as
stewards rather than absolute owners.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q8. The basic education advocated by Gandhiji in
Wardha Congress is related with
(a) Compulsory elementary education
(b) Social relevance of education to society
(c) Demand of separate institutions for minorities
(d) Compulsory military training for every student
Answer: (b)
• At the Wardha Conference in 1937, Mahatma
Gandhi proposed a new scheme of education
known as "Nai Talim" or Basic Education.
• A core principle of this scheme was to make
education self-supporting and deeply
connected to the student's environment. It
centered around learning a productive
manual craft (like spinning or carpentry).
• The goal was to integrate education with
daily life and work, thereby ensuring its social
relevance to society and preventing the
alienation of the educated from the masses.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q9. Which important event immediately preceded
Jallianwala Bagh massacre?
(a) Rowlatt Act enactment
(b) Communal award
(c) Coming of Simon Commission
(d) Quit India Movement
Answer: (a)
• The Jallianwala Bagh massacre of April 1919
was a direct consequence of the widespread
protests against the recently passed Rowlatt
Act.
• The Rowlatt Act (March 1919) was a
repressive law that allowed the British

•

•

government to imprison any person on
suspicion of terrorism without trial.
It was in protest against this "Black Act" and
the arrest of local leaders Dr. Satya Pal and
Dr. Saifuddin Kitchlew that the peaceful
gathering had assembled at Jallianwala Bagh.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q10. Non-Cooperation Movement was suspended in
1922 because
(a) Violence broke out at Chauri Chaura
(b) Gandhiji was arrested for five years
(c) Government accepted the demands of Indian
leaders
(d) People were not supporting this movement
Answer: (a)
• The Non-Cooperation Movement was in full
swing across India in early 1922.
• However, on February 5, 1922, an incident
occurred at Chauri Chaura in Uttar Pradesh,
where a mob of protestors, provoked by
police firing, attacked and set fire to a police
station, killing 22 policemen.
• Deeply disturbed by this act of violence,
Mahatma Gandhi, a staunch believer in nonviolence (Ahimsa), felt that the people were
not yet ready for a disciplined non-violent
struggle. He immediately decided to suspend
the entire movement.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q11. Who started Bhoodan Movement in India?
(a) Gandhiji
(b) Jayaprakash Narayan

(c) Vinoba Bhave
(d) Jawaharlal Nehru
Answer: (c)
• The Bhoodan Movement (Land Gift
Movement) was a voluntary land reform
movement initiated in 1951.
• It was started by Acharya Vinoba Bhave, a
prominent Gandhian and spiritual leader.
• The movement's mission was to persuade
wealthy landowners to voluntarily donate a
portion of their land ("Bhoodan") to be
redistributed among landless laborers,
aiming to solve the problem of land
inequality through non-violent means.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q12. Gandhiji was of the view that
(a) All old traditions should be followed
(b) All old traditions should be discarded
(c) Only scientifically based traditions should be
followed
(d) Those traditions should not be followed which are
against our moral values
Answer: (d)
• Mahatma Gandhi was not a blind follower of
tradition, nor did he advocate for discarding
all old customs.
• His approach was to evaluate traditions
based on the core principles of truth, nonviolence, and human dignity.
• He believed that any tradition, no matter how
ancient, should be rejected if it was found to
be harmful, unjust, or against fundamental
moral values. His campaign against
untouchability is a prime example of this
view.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q13. According to Gandhiji non-violence is
(a) A way to attain truth
(b) A way to win political freedom
(c) The only way to realise God
(d) An end in itself
Answer: (a)

•

•

•

•

For Mahatma Gandhi, Truth (Satya) and Nonviolence (Ahimsa) were inextricably linked.
He often stated, "Truth is God."
He believed that Truth was the ultimate goal
or end, and Non-violence was the means to
achieve it.
In his philosophy, one could not be separated
from the other. Therefore, he saw nonviolence as the path or the way to attain
truth. While it was also a method for winning
political freedom, its philosophical foundation
was its role as the means to realize the
ultimate Truth.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q14. The DYARCHY as introduced by the Government
of India Act, 1919 postulated which of the following?
(a) A system of dual government in Bengal
(b) Backward classes were entitled to vote
(c) A few subjects were transferred to the Provincial
Ministries and the rest retained by the Executive
Council
(d) Hindus and Muslims could vote separately
Answer: (c)
• Dyarchy, meaning "rule of two," was a
system of dual governance introduced in the
provinces by the Government of India Act,
1919.
• Under this system, the subjects of provincial
administration were divided into two
categories: "Transferred" and "Reserved."
• Transferred subjects (like education and
public health) were administered by Indian
ministers responsible to the provincial
legislative council.
• Reserved subjects (like finance, law and
order) were retained by the British governor
and his executive council, who were not
responsible to the legislature. This created a
division of power within the provincial
government.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q15. What is true of Indian National Congress during
World War II?

(a) It demanded for its co-operation with the British
that a provincial national government may be set up
at the centre
(b) It extended full support to the Indian National
Army to oust the British from India
(c) It decided to extend full support to the allied
nations
(d) It worked for the defeat of the allied nations
Answer: (a)
• During World War II, the Indian National
Congress was sympathetic to the Allied cause
against fascism but refused to offer
unconditional support to the British.
• The Congress's consistent stand was that it
would only cooperate in the war effort if the
British met its key demands.
• The primary demand was the immediate
formation of a provisional national
government at the centre with real power
and a promise of complete independence
after the war. The British refusal to meet this
demand led to the resignation of Congress
ministries in 1939 and later the Quit India
Movement in 1942.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1981
Q1. What is the correct order of happenings of the
following three events in the British rule in India?
1. Rowlatt Act
2. Hunter Report
3. Jallianwalla Bagh Tragedy
Select the correct answer from the codes given below:
(a) 1, 3, 2
(b) 2, 1, 3
(c) 3, 2, 1
(d) 2, 3, 1
The correct option is (a).
• The events occurred in a clear cause-andeffect sequence.
• First, the repressive Rowlatt Act was passed
in March 1919, which led to widespread
protests across India.

•

•

•

•

Second, in response to these protests, the
Jallianwala Bagh Tragedy (or massacre) took
place in Amritsar on April 13, 1919, where
British troops fired on a peaceful gathering.
Third, following the massacre and the ensuing
national outrage, the British government
appointed the Hunter Commission (or Hunter
Report) in October 1919 to investigate the
incident.
Therefore, the correct chronological order is
Rowlatt Act (1), Jallianwala Bagh Tragedy (3),
and Hunter Report (2).
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q2. Who was the Viceroy when Attlee declared
India’s Independence?
(a) Lord Wavell
(b) Lord Mountbatten
(c) Lord William Bentinck
(d) Lord Cornwallis
The correct option is (b).
• In February 1947, British Prime Minister
Clement Attlee made the historic declaration
that Britain would grant India full selfgovernance by June 1948 at the latest.
• To oversee this transfer of power, he
appointed Lord Mountbatten as the new and
last Viceroy of India, replacing Lord Wavell.
• Therefore, Lord Mountbatten was the Viceroy
at the time of Attlee's declaration and
subsequently administered the partition and
independence of India.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q3. Under whose leadership, the Indian National
Army was formed?
(a) Mahatma Gandhi
(b) Subhash Chandra Bose
(c) Jawaharlal Nehru
(d) Gopal Krishna Gokhale
The correct option is (b).
• The Indian National Army (INA), or Azad Hind
Fauj, was initially formed in 1942 by Indian
prisoners of war captured by the Japanese.

•

•

•

However, the organization was revived,
revitalized, and transformed into a formidable
force under the dynamic leadership of
Subhash Chandra Bose after his arrival in
Southeast Asia in 1943.
Bose became the Supreme Commander of
the INA and head of the Provisional
Government of Free India (Arzi Hukumat-eAzad Hind), aiming to liberate India from
British rule with Japanese assistance.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q4. Who said these words at the time of Quit India
Movement—’Do or die’?
(a) Motilal Nehru
(b) Mahatma Gandhi
(c) Sardar Patel
(d) Subhash Chandra Bose
The correct option is (b).
• The famous call to “Do or Die” (Karo ya Maro)
was given by Mahatma Gandhi.
• He delivered this powerful message in a
speech in Bombay on August 8, 1942, on the
eve of launching the Quit India Movement.
• The slogan was meant to inspire the Indian
people to make a final, determined push for
freedom, signifying that they should either
free India or die in the attempt, but not live to
see the perpetuation of their slavery.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q5. Why did Gandhiji stop the Non- Cooperation
Movement?
(a) Because of Gandhi-Irwin Pact

(b) Because the mob turned violent
(c) Because he was ordered to do so by the Britishers
(d) Because he did not like to continue the movement
The correct option is (b).
• The Non-Cooperation Movement was
founded on the principle of non-violence
(Ahimsa).
• Mahatma Gandhi abruptly suspended the
movement in February 1922 after an incident
at Chauri Chaura in Uttar Pradesh.
• At Chauri Chaura, a mob of protestors turned
violent and set fire to a police station, killing
22 policemen.
• This act of violence went against the
fundamental tenets of the movement, leading
Gandhi to call it off as he felt the people were
not yet ready for a purely non-violent
struggle.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q6. The Indian National Movement refers to the
(a) Movement started by Subhash Chandra Bose
through I.N.A.
(b) Struggle by the Indians to gain freedom from
British colonial rule in India
(c) Struggle by Indians to gain dominion status under
British rule
(d) Struggle by Indians to stop British trade in India
The correct option is (b).
• The Indian National Movement is a broad
term that encompasses the long and
multifaceted struggle by the Indians to gain
freedom from British colonial rule in India.
• It includes a wide array of activities,
ideologies, and phases, from the early
moderate petitions to the extremist
agitations, the Gandhian mass movements,
and the actions of revolutionaries.
• While gaining dominion status was an early
goal for some, the ultimate aim of the
movement evolved into achieving complete
independence.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q7. Which of the following represents the
integration of Hindus and Muslims for a common
cause?
(a) Khilafat Movement
(b) Quit India Movement
(c) Civil Disobedience Movement
(d) None of the above
The correct option is (a).
• The Khilafat Movement (1919–1924) saw an
unprecedented level of Hindu–Muslim unity.
• Indian Muslims launched the movement to
protest the dismemberment of the Ottoman
Empire and the abolition of the Caliphate by
the British after World War I.
• Mahatma Gandhi saw this as a golden
opportunity to unite Hindus and Muslims
against British rule.
• He merged the Non-Cooperation Movement
with the Khilafat Movement, creating a
unified, mass-based struggle that
represented a high point of integration of
Hindus and Muslims for a common cause.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q8. Purna Swaraj Day was observed on
(a) 26th November, 1947
(b) 26th October, 1949
(c) 26th January, 1950
(d) 26th January, 1930
The correct option is (d).
• At the Lahore Congress session in December
1929, the Indian National Congress passed
the historic Purna Swaraj (Complete
Independence) resolution.
• It was decided that January 26, 1930, would
be observed as the first Purna Swaraj Day, or
Independence Day, across the country.
• On this day, people gathered to take a pledge
of independence.
• To commemorate this historic day, the
Constitution of India was formally adopted
on January 26, 1950, which is now celebrated
as India's Republic Day.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q9. Whom did Gandhiji consider his political Guru?
(a) Vinoba Bhave
(b) B. G. Tilak
(c) Gopal Krishna Gokhale
(d) Dadabhai Naoroji
The correct option is (c).
• Upon his return to India from South Africa in
1915, Mahatma Gandhi was advised by Gopal
Krishna Gokhale to spend a year traveling
across India to understand the country and its
people before entering politics.
• Gandhi held Gokhale in very high esteem for
his wisdom, moderation, and selfless service
to the nation.
• He looked up to Gokhale for guidance and
mentorship, and in his autobiography, Gandhi
explicitly refers to Gokhale as his political
Guru.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q10. Which of the following periods can be
considered as the era of mass revolution?
(a) 1856–1885 A.D.
(b) 1757–1857 A.D.
(c) 1920–1947 A.D.
(d) 1901–1919 A.D.
The correct option is (c).
• The period from 1920 to 1947 is known as the
Gandhian Era in the Indian freedom struggle.
• This era was marked by the transformation of
the nationalist movement from an elite-led
agitation into a true mass revolution.
• Under Mahatma Gandhi's leadership, three
major pan-India movements were launched:
o The Non-Cooperation Movement
(1920–22)

o

•
•

The Civil Disobedience Movement
(1930–34)
o The Quit India Movement (1942)
These saw the participation of millions of
ordinary Indians from all walks of life.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q11. The Indian patriot, who died in jail due to
hunger strike, was
(a) S. C. Bose
(b) Bhagat Singh
(c) Jatin Das
(d) Bipin Chandra Pal
The correct option is (c).
• Jatindra Nath Das, popularly known as Jatin
Das, was an Indian revolutionary and
freedom fighter.
• He was arrested in 1929 in connection with
the Lahore Conspiracy Case and imprisoned in
Lahore jail along with Bhagat Singh and
others.
• In jail, he began a hunger strike to protest the
inhumane conditions and demand equal
rights for Indian political prisoners.
• He passed away on the 63rd day of his fast,
on September 13, 1929, becoming a
celebrated martyr of the freedom struggle.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q12. Who among the following were associated with
the birth of Swarajya Party?
(a) C. R. Das and Motilal Nehru
(b) C. R. Das and Sardar Patel
(c) Motilal Nehru and Rajendra Prasad
(d) None of the above
The correct option is (a).

•

•

•

•

The Swarajya Party (or Swaraj Party) was
formed in January 1923 after a split within the
Congress following the suspension of the
Non-Cooperation Movement.
The party was founded by the “pro-changer”
faction of the Congress who advocated for
entering the legislative councils to obstruct
the government from within.
Its principal founders and leaders were
Chittaranjan Das (C. R. Das), who served as its
president, and Motilal Nehru, who was its
secretary.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q13. Who among the following was responsible for
the revival of Hinduism in the 19th century?
(a) Swami Dayanand
(b) Swami Vivekanand
(c) Guru Shankaracharya
(d) Raja Rammohan Roy
The correct option is (a).
• Swami Dayanand Saraswati was a major
Hindu religious scholar and reformer who
founded the Arya Samaj in 1875.
• His mission was to reform and revive
Hinduism by rejecting what he saw as later,
corrupting additions (like idol worship, the
rigid caste system, and certain rituals) and
calling for a return to the pristine teachings of
the Vedas.
• His slogan “Back to the Vedas” and his efforts
to purify and defend Hinduism against
criticism played a significant role in the Hindu
revivalism of the 19th century.
• While Swami Vivekananda also played a
massive role, Swami Dayanand's work was
more focused on internal reform and revival.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1982
Q1. The personality who is not connected with 1857
mutiny is
(a) Bhagat Singh
(b) Lakshmibai

(c) Tantia Tope
(d) Nana Saheb
The correct option is (a).
• The Revolt of 1857, also known as the Sepoy
Mutiny, was a major uprising against the
British East India Company.
• Key figures who led this revolt include Rani
Lakshmibai of Jhansi, Nana Saheb, and his
general Tantia Tope.
• These individuals were contemporaries and
central figures in the 1857 struggle.
• Bhagat Singh, in contrast, was a revolutionary
of a later period, born in 1907, and active
during the 1920s–1930s.
• He is famous for his role in the Hindustan
Socialist Republican Association and his
martyrdom in 1931.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q2. “Once again India leads the world” are the words
of
(a) S. N. Bannerjee
(b) Mahatma Gandhi
(c) Gopal Krishna Gokhale
(d) None of these
The correct option is (d).
• This quote is not attributed to Surendranath
Bannerjee, Mahatma Gandhi, or Gopal
Krishna Gokhale.
• While these leaders often spoke of India’s
spiritual leadership, this exact phrasing is not
part of their recognized writings or speeches.
• Swami Vivekananda spoke similarly, but not
in these exact words.
• Therefore, it cannot be correctly ascribed to
any of the given options.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q3. Who among the following founded Ramakrishna
Mission?
(a) Swami Dayanand Saraswati
(b) Raja Rammohan Roy
(c) Swami Vivekananda
(d) None of these
The correct option is (c).

•

•

•

•
•

The Ramakrishna Mission is a Hindu religious
and spiritual organization, founded on May 1,
1897.
It was established by Swami Vivekananda, the
foremost disciple of Sri Ramakrishna
Paramahamsa.
Its motto is “Atmano Mokshartham Jagat
Hitaya Cha” (For one’s own salvation and for
the welfare of the world).
It focuses on Karma Yoga, education,
healthcare, social service, and disaster relief.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q4. Who among the following was not a moderate?
(a) Mahatma Gandhi
(b) W. C. Bannerjee
(c) G. K. Gokhale
(d) B. G. Tilak
The correct option is (d).
• The early Indian National Congress had
Moderates and Extremists.
• Moderates like W. C. Bannerjee and G. K.
Gokhale believed in constitutional reforms
and petition-based politics.
• B. G. Tilak was the most prominent Extremist,
advocating assertive methods, mass
agitation, and Swaraj.
• He famously declared “Swaraj is my
birthright, and I shall have it!”
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q5. Dandi March was organised by Gandhiji to
(a) Break the salt laws
(b) Protest against atrocities on Harijans
(c) Oppose Simon Commission
(d) Ask for Purna Swaraj
The correct option is (a).
• The Dandi March (1930), or Salt March, was
led by Mahatma Gandhi.
• Its purpose was to protest and defy the
British monopoly on salt production and the
salt tax.
• Gandhi marched to Dandi and made salt
illegally, initiating the Civil Disobedience
Movement.

•

Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q6. Non-Cooperation Movement was called off by
Gandhiji because
(a) Of Chauri Chaura incident
(b) Government accepted the demands of Indian
leaders
(c) Of repressive measures adopted by the
government
(d) None of these
The correct option is (a).
• The Non-Cooperation Movement (1920–22)
was suspended after the Chauri Chaura
incident.
• In February 1922, protestors attacked and
burned a police station, killing 22 policemen.
• Gandhi, disturbed by the violence, felt the
people were not ready for non-violent
struggle and called off the movement.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q7. Civil Disobedience Movement was started in
India in 1930 for
(a) Home rule
(b) Purna Swaraj
(c) Non-cooperation with British government
(d) Hindu-Muslim unity
The correct option is (b).
• At the Lahore Session (1929), Congress under
Jawaharlal Nehru passed the Purna Swaraj
resolution.
• The Civil Disobedience Movement, initiated
with the Dandi March, was launched to
achieve complete independence.
• It officially replaced Dominion Status with the
demand for Purna Swaraj.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q8. Doctrine of Lapse was adopted as a measure to
annex Indian states by
(a) Lord Cornwallis
(b) Lord Clive
(c) Lord Dalhousie
(d) Lord Wellesley
The correct option is (c).

•
•
•
•
•

The Doctrine of Lapse was implemented by
Lord Dalhousie (1848–1856).
It allowed annexation of states if a ruler died
without a natural male heir.
The British did not recognize adopted heirs
for succession.
States like Satara, Nagpur, and Jhansi were
annexed through this doctrine.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q9. Raja Rammohan Roy was supporter of
(a) Sati
(b) Widow re-marriage
(c) Child marriage
(d) Learning of Sanskrit
The correct option is (b).
• Raja Rammohan Roy was a pioneering social
reformer of the Bengal Renaissance.
• He opposed Sati, child marriage, and
polygamy, and advocated for women’s rights.
• He was a strong supporter of widow remarriage, arguing for humane treatment of
widows.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 1983
Q1. Who represented India at the Chicago
Conference of World Religions in 1893?
(a) Swami Vivekananda
(b) Swami Dayananda
(c) Swami Abhedananda
(d) Ramakrishna Paramhansa
The correct option is (a).

•

•

•

•

•

Swami Vivekananda, a Hindu monk and the
chief disciple of the mystic Sri Ramakrishna
Paramhamsa, represented Hinduism and
India at the Parliament of the World's
Religions held in Chicago in 1893.
His opening address, beginning with the
famous words, "Sisters and brothers of
America," received a two-minute standing
ovation and captivated the audience.
His speeches at the conference were
groundbreaking, introducing the core tenets
of Hindu philosophy, particularly Vedanta
and Yoga, to the Western world and
presenting Hinduism as a major global
religion.
This event marked a significant moment in
the global understanding of Eastern
spirituality.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q2. Some persons who were close associates of
Gandhiji died recently. They were
(a) Anandmayi Ma, Pyarelal, Vinoba Bhave
(b) Mira Behn, J.P. Narayan, Lord Mountbatten,
Manibhai Desai
(c) J. B. Kripalani, Mahadev-Desai, Pyarelal
(d) Sarla Behn, J. B. Kripalani, Mira Behn
The correct option is (a).
• This question, from a 1983 exam, asks to
identify a group of people associated with
Mahatma Gandhi who had passed away in
the years immediately preceding the exam.
• Anandamayi Ma, a spiritual leader, passed
away on August 27, 1982.
• Pyarelal Nayyar, who served as Gandhi's
personal secretary in his later years, also
passed away in 1982.
• Vinoba Bhave, a prominent Gandhian and
spiritual successor known for the Bhoodan
Movement, passed away on November 15,
1982.
• The deaths of these three notable figures, all
connected to Gandhi's legacy, occurred in
close succession in 1982, making this the
correct group for the context of the question.

•

Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q3. The theory of economic drain from India to
England was propounded by
(a) Dadabhai Naoroji
(b) R. C. Dutt
(c) B. G. Tilak
(d) L. K. Jha
The correct option is (a).
• The "Drain Theory" was a cornerstone of the
early Indian nationalist critique of British
colonial rule.
• It argued that Britain was systematically
draining wealth from India, contributing to its
poverty.
• This theory was most famously and rigorously
articulated by Dadabhai Naoroji, known as
the "Grand Old Man of India."
• In his seminal work, Poverty and Un-British
Rule in India (1901), Naoroji detailed how
resources were transferred from India to
Britain through salaries and pensions of
British officials, military expenditure, and
profits from trade, all without any equivalent
economic return for India.
• This "drain of wealth" was presented as a
major cause of India's economic
underdevelopment.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q4. The capital of India was shifted from Calcutta to
Delhi in the year
(a) 1905
(b) 1911
(c) 1912
(d) 1916
The correct option is (b).
• The decision to shift the capital of British
India from Calcutta (now Kolkata) to Delhi
was announced on December 12, 1911.
• The announcement was made by King George
V at the grand Delhi Durbar.
• The move was seen as both a strategic
decision to place the capital in a more central
and historically significant location and a

•

•

political move to appease Bengali sentiment
after the controversial Partition of Bengal was
annulled at the same time.
While the formal inauguration of the new
capital, New Delhi, took place much later in
1931, the year of the official announcement
and the beginning of the shift was 1911.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q5. Who can be called the father of local selfgovernment in India?
(a) Lord Ripon
(b) Lord Minto
(c) Lord Curzon
(d) Lord Dufferin
The correct option is (a).
• Lord Ripon, who served as the Viceroy of
India from 1880 to 1884, is widely regarded as
the "father of local self-government in India."
• In 1882, he introduced a series of reforms
aimed at developing local bodies like
municipal committees and district boards.
• His resolution advocated for these bodies to
have a majority of elected non-official
members and to be entrusted with real
financial and administrative powers.
• Ripon's intention was not just to improve
administration but to provide political
education and training in self-governance for
Indians, laying a crucial foundation for
democratic institutions in the country.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q6. Indian National Congress was founded by
(a) W. C. Bannerjee
(b) A. O. Hume
(c) Mahatma Gandhi
(d) Annie Besant
The correct option is (b).
• The Indian National Congress (INC) was
founded in December 1885.
• The initiative for its formation was taken by
Allan Octavian Hume (A. O. Hume), a retired
British civil servant.

•

•

•

He envisioned an all-India organization that
would serve as a platform for educated
Indians to voice their opinions and
grievances, acting as a "safety valve" to
prevent popular discontent from turning into
another major revolt.
While W. C. Bannerjee was the first president
of the INC, A. O. Hume was the principal
founder and served as its first General
Secretary.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q7. The Indian national movement became
organised for the first time in
(a) 1885
(b) 1857
(c) 1905-06
(d) 1916-17
The correct option is (a).
• While there were many regional political
associations before, the Indian national
movement became organized on a panIndian scale for the first time with the
establishment of the Indian National
Congress (INC) in 1885.
• The INC provided a unified, national platform
where leaders from different parts of the
country could meet annually, discuss
common problems, formulate demands, and
develop a cohesive strategy to engage with
the British government.

•

•

This marked the beginning of an organized,
institutionalized phase of the freedom
struggle.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

•

•

Q8. The first great experiment of Gandhiji in
‘Satyagraha’ took place in
(a) Dandi
(b) Champaran
(c) South Africa
(d) Bardoli
The correct option is (c).
• Mahatma Gandhi developed and first
implemented his philosophy and technique
of non-violent resistance, which he termed
Satyagraha (truth-force), during his time in
South Africa.
• From 1893 to 1914, he led the Indian
community in South Africa in a series of
campaigns against discriminatory and unjust
laws imposed by the government.
• These campaigns involved peaceful protests,
civil disobedience, and courting arrest,
serving as the testing ground for the methods
he would later use on a much larger scale in
India.
• His first major Satyagraha in India was at
Champaran in 1917, but his very first
experiments were in South Africa.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q9. After Chauri Chaura incident in which 22
policemen were killed, the result was that
(a) Gandhiji started his Dandi March
(b) Gandhiji called off the no tax campaign
(c) Gandhiji withdrew his movement of non
cooperation
(d) Gandhiji went to-talk with the Viceroy, Lord Irwin
The correct option is (c).
• The Chauri Chaura incident, which occurred
on February 5, 1922, was a turning point in
the Non-Cooperation Movement.
• A mob of protestors attacked and burned a
police station, killing 22 policemen.

•

As the movement was founded on the
principle of non-violence (Ahimsa), Mahatma
Gandhi was deeply shocked and pained by
this act of violence.
Believing that the people were not yet ready
for a disciplined non-violent struggle, he
made the unilateral decision to withdraw the
Non-Cooperation Movement entirely, a move
that was met with disappointment by many
other nationalist leaders.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q10. The first among the following to establish trade
links with India were
(a) Dutch
(b) French
(c) Portuguese
(d) English
The correct option is (c).
• The Portuguese were the pioneers among
European powers in establishing a direct sea
route and trade links with India in the
modern era.
• The explorer Vasco da Gama successfully
navigated around Africa's Cape of Good Hope
and reached the port of Calicut in India in
1498.
• This voyage opened the way for the
Portuguese to establish a lucrative spice
trade and set up trading posts and colonies
along the Indian coast, long before the arrival
of the Dutch, English, and French.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q11. Match the following :
A. Lord Dalhousie : 1. Prohibition of Sati
B. Lord William Bentinck : 2. Local SelfGovernment
C. Lord Ripon : 3. Division of Bengal
D. Lord Curzon : 4. Doctrine of Lapse
Select the correct answer using the codes given
below:
ABCD
(a) 4 1 2 3
(b) 4 2 1 3

(c) 1 2 3 4
(d) 3 1 2 4
The correct option is (a).
• Lord Dalhousie (Governor-General 1848–
1856) is most famously associated with the
Doctrine of Lapse, an aggressive annexation
policy. (A–4)
• Lord William Bentinck (Governor-General
1828–1835) was a social reformer who, with
the help of Raja Rammohan Roy, enacted the
law for the Prohibition of Sati in 1829. (B–1)
• Lord Ripon (Viceroy 1880–1884) is known as
the “father of Local Self-Government” in
India for his 1882 reforms that aimed to
establish elected local bodies. (C–2)
• Lord Curzon (Viceroy 1899–1905) is most
notoriously remembered for overseeing the
Division of Bengal (Partition of Bengal) in
1905. (D–3)
• The correct matching is A–4, B–1, C–2, D–3.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q12. The demand for Pakistan was made by the
Muslim League for the first time in
(a) 1938
(b) 1940
(c) 1941
(d) 1946
The correct option is (b).
• The All-India Muslim League formally put
forward the demand for a separate Muslim
state for the first time at its annual session in
Lahore in March 1940.
• The resolution passed at this session, which
came to be known as the Lahore Resolution
or the Pakistan Resolution, stated that the
geographically contiguous areas in the
northwestern and eastern zones of India,
where Muslims were in a majority, should be
grouped to constitute “Independent States.”
• This marked a pivotal moment in the
subcontinent's history, formalizing the twonation theory and the demand for Pakistan.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q13. Which of the following events is not correctly
matched?
(a) Liberation of Goa — 1961
(b) Death of Jawaharlal Nehru — 1966
(c) Indo-China War — 1962
(d) First General Elections — 1951-52
The correct option is (b).
• This pair is not correctly matched. Jawaharlal
Nehru, the first Prime Minister of India,
passed away while in office on May 27, 1964,
not 1966.
• The other pairs are correct:
o Goa was liberated from Portuguese
rule through Indian military action
(Operation Vijay) in 1961.
o The Sino-Indian War (Indo-China
War) took place in 1962.
o The first General Elections in
independent India were held over
several months from late 1951 to
early 1952.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q14. The wrongly matched pair among the following
is
(a) Gandhiji — Dandi March
(b) Mohammed Ali Jinnah — Khilafat movement
(c) Sir Syed Ahmed Khan — Aligarh
(d) Subhash Chandra Bose — Forward Bloc
The correct option is (b).
• This pair is wrongly matched. While
Mohammed Ali Jinnah was a prominent
Muslim leader, he was not a central figure in
the Khilafat Movement.
• In fact, he was critical of its methods and its
mixing of religion with politics.

•

•

•

The Khilafat Movement was primarily led by
the Ali Brothers (Shaukat Ali and Muhammad
Ali) and Maulana Abul Kalam Azad, with
Mahatma Gandhi’s support.
The other pairs are correct:
o Gandhiji led the Dandi March.
o Sir Syed Ahmed Khan founded the
Muhammadan Anglo-Oriental
College, which later became the
Aligarh Muslim University.
o Subhash Chandra Bose founded the
Forward Bloc in 1939 after resigning
from the Congress presidency.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q15. The following are the events in national
movement
1. Champaran Satyagraha
2. Non-Cooperation Movement
3. Quit India Movement
4. Dandi March
The correct chronological order is
(a) 1, 2, 4, 3
(b) 2, 1, 3, 4
(c) 3, 2, 1, 4
(d) 2, 3, 1, 4
The correct option is (a).
• To arrange these events in chronological
order:
o Champaran Satyagraha was
Mahatma Gandhi's first major
satyagraha in India, which took place
in 1917.
o The Non-Cooperation Movement was
a nationwide mass movement
launched in 1920.
o The Dandi March (Salt March)
initiated the Civil Disobedience
Movement in 1930.
o The Quit India Movement was the
final major mass struggle for
independence, launched in 1942.
• Therefore, the correct sequence is 1, 2, 4, 3.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q16. Match the following correctly :
A. Ramakrishna Mission : 1. Swami Dayanand
Saraswati
B. Arya Samaj : 2. Raja Rammohan Roy

• Arya Samaj was founded by Swami Dayanand
Saraswati in 1875. (B–1)
• Brahmo Samaj was founded by Raja
Rammohan Roy in 1828. (C–2)
• Prarthana Samaj was founded by Atmaram
Pandurang in 1867, with significant influence
and help from Keshab Chandra Sen, a leader
of the Brahmo Samaj. (D–3)
• The correct matching is A–4, B–1, C–2, D–3.

Q17. Which of the following is/are correct ?
Newspaper : Publisher/Editor

and Bhupendranath Dutt, not C. Y.
