Q18. Arrange the following in chronological order
1. Home Rule League
2. Swadeshi Movement
3. Bardoli Movement
4. Round Table Conference
The correct chronological order is
(a) 1, 2, 3, 4
(b) 2, 1, 3, 4
(c) 3, 1, 4, 2
(d) 4, 2, 1, 3
The correct option is (b).
• To arrange these events in chronological
order:
o The Swadeshi Movement was
launched in 1905 as a response to the
Partition of Bengal.
o The Home Rule League movement
was started in 1916 by Annie Besant
and Bal Gangadhar Tilak.
o The Bardoli Movement (Satyagraha),
a major peasant movement led by
Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel, took place
in 1928.
o The Round Table Conferences were a
series of three conferences held in
London between 1930 and 1932.
• Therefore, the correct sequence is 2, 1, 3, 4.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q19. The first session of Indian National Congress in
1885 was held at
(a) Bombay
(b) Calcutta
(c) Lahore
(d) Lucknow
The correct option is (a).

•

The inaugural session of the Indian National
Congress was held from December 28 to 31,
1885.
The session took place at Gokuldas Tejpal
Sanskrit College in Bombay (now Mumbai).
It was originally scheduled to be held in
Poona (now Pune), but was moved to Bombay
due to an outbreak of cholera in Poona.
W. C. Bannerjee was the president of this first
session.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q20. Who gave the ‘Communal Award’ in India?
(a) C. R. Attlee
(b) Ramsay Macdonald
(c) Stafford Cripps
(d) None of the above
The correct option is (b).
• The Communal Award was announced in
August 1932 by the British Prime Minister
Ramsay MacDonald.
• The award granted separate electorates not
only to Muslims, Sikhs, and Christians, but
also extended them to the “Depressed
Classes” (Dalits).
• Mahatma Gandhi saw this as a move to
divide Hindu society and undertook a fast
unto death in protest, which led to the signing
of the Poona Pact, modifying the award for
the Depressed Classes.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q21. Quit India Movement was launched in
(a) 1936
(b) 1941
(c) 1942
(d) 1947
The correct option is (c).
• The Quit India Movement, also known as the
August Movement (August Kranti), was a
major civil disobedience movement launched
by the Indian National Congress.
• The historic “Quit India” resolution was
passed at the Bombay session of the All-India
Congress Committee on August 8, 1942.

•

•

The movement was launched the next day, on
August 9, 1942, with Mahatma Gandhi's call
to “Do or Die”.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q22. Which of the following was mainly responsible
for the transfer of power by the British to India on
August 15, 1947?
(a) Cabinet Mission Plan
(b) Attlee Declaration
(c) Simon Commission Plan
(d) Mountbatten Declaration
The correct option is (d).
• While the Cabinet Mission Plan (1946) and
Attlee’s Declaration (Feb 1947) set the stage,
the final plan for the immediate transfer of
power was the Mountbatten Plan.
• Lord Mountbatten, the last Viceroy,
concluded that a united India was not
feasible and proposed a plan for the partition
of British India into two independent
dominions, India and Pakistan.
• This plan, announced on June 3, 1947, and
accepted by the major political parties,
became the basis for the Indian
Independence Act 1947, which formalized the
transfer of power on August 15, 1947.
• It is therefore also known as the Mountbatten
Declaration or the June 3rd Plan.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q23. Which of the following events is not correct?
(a) Simon Commission—1927
(b) Salt Satyagraha—1930

(c) Gandhi-Irwin Pact—1931
(d) Quit India Movement—1941
The correct option is (d).
• This pair is not correct. The Quit India
Movement was launched in August 1942, not
1941.
• The other pairs are correct:
o The Simon Commission was
appointed by the British government
in 1927.
o The Salt Satyagraha (Dandi March)
was initiated in 1930.
o The Gandhi-Irwin Pact was signed on
March 5, 1931.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q24. Assertion: Great Britain gave India her
independence on August 15, 1947.
Reason: Great Britain became weak after her
involvement in World War II.
(a) Both A and R are true but R is not the reason for A
(b) Both A and R are true and R is the reason for A
(c) Neither A nor R is correct
(d) A is correct but R is wrong
The correct option is (b).
• Assertion (A) is a true statement of fact:
Great Britain granted independence to India
on August 15, 1947.
• Reason (R) is also true and is a major
contributing factor.
• World War II left Great Britain economically
exhausted and militarily overstretched.
• The war severely weakened Britain's capacity
to maintain its vast empire.
• This weakness, combined with the rising tide
of Indian nationalism and international
pressure, made it imperative for Britain to
grant independence.
• Therefore, the reason correctly explains the
assertion.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 1984
Q1. Who was the leader of the Depressed Classes in
the pre-lndependence period?
(a) Ram Manohar Lohia
(b) B. R. Ambedkar
(c) Rajendra Prasad
(d) Jagjivan Ram
The correct option is (b).
• Dr. B. R. Ambedkar was the foremost leader
and champion of the rights of the Depressed
Classes (Dalits) during the pre-independence
era.
• He dedicated his life to fighting against the
caste system and securing social and political
rights for the untouchables.
• He founded organizations like the Bahishkrit
Hitakarini Sabha (1924) to promote
education and socio-economic upliftment.
• He also led significant movements like the
Mahad Satyagraha (1927) to assert the right
of untouchables to use public water
resources.
• His political work included representing the
Depressed Classes at the Round Table
Conferences, founding the Independent
Labour Party, and later the Scheduled Castes
Federation, all aimed at creating a distinct
political voice for the community.
• His efforts culminated in his role as the
Chairman of the Drafting Committee of the
Indian Constitution, where he enshrined legal
protections against untouchability.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q2. Which of the following statements about
Mahatma Gandhi is correct in connection with
India’s independence?
(a) He was the happiest man
(b) He was a very disillusioned man
(c) He had full satisfaction about his achievements
(d) He was happy with the formation of Pakistan
The correct option is (b).

•

•

•

•

•

•

On the eve of India's independence,
Mahatma Gandhi was far from happy; he was
a deeply disillusioned man.
He had worked his entire life for a united,
independent India, but the country was being
partitioned amidst widespread communal
violence and bloodshed.
He was profoundly pained by the partition
and the hatred between Hindus and
Muslims.
Instead of celebrating in Delhi on August 15,
1947, he was in Calcutta (now Kolkata),
fasting and trying to quell the communal
riots.
His dream of a united India free from
religious strife was shattered, leading to his
deep sense of disillusionment with the final
outcome of the freedom struggle.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q3. In the 19th century the people who participated
in the national movement were mostly
(a) Zamindars
(b) Rich people
(c) Educated middle class
(d) Poor people
The correct option is (c).
• The early phase of the Indian national
movement in the 19th century, particularly
after the founding of the Indian National
Congress in 1885, was predominantly led and
participated in by the educated middle class.
• This group consisted of professionals such as
lawyers, doctors, teachers, and journalists,
many of whom had received Western-style
education.
• They were the first to be exposed to modern
ideas of democracy, liberty, and nationalism.
• They used their knowledge and skills to
articulate the political and economic
grievances of India and to organize the early
nationalist movement through constitutional
methods like petitions and debates.

•

•

The participation of the masses, or "poor
people," became a feature of the movement
much later, in the Gandhian era.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q4. Which of the following pairs is incorrect?
(a) Lal, Bal, Pal — Extremist Group
(b) Annie Besant — Home Rule movement
(c) Gandhi — Dandi March
(d) None of these
The correct option is (d).
• All the pairs listed are correctly matched,
making "None of these" the correct answer.
• Lal, Bal, Pal refers to the triumvirate of Lala
Lajpat Rai, Bal Gangadhar Tilak, and Bipin
Chandra Pal, who were the foremost leaders
of the Extremist Group within the Indian
National Congress.
• Annie Besant, along with Bal Gangadhar
Tilak, was a founder and the primary leader
of the Home Rule Movement (1916), which
aimed to achieve self-government for India.
• Mahatma Gandhi led the famous Dandi
March (Salt March) in 1930, which marked
the beginning of the Civil Disobedience
Movement.
• Since all pairs are correct, there are no
incorrect pairs in the list.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q5. Swaraj Party was established to
(a) Share power in the Government
(b) Ask the British to leave India
(c) To enter the Councils and wreck the Government
from within
(d) Ask the people to fight against the British
The correct option is (c).
• The Swaraj Party was formed in 1923 by
Congress leaders like C.R. Das and Motilal
Nehru, who were known as "pro-changers."
• They disagreed with the Congress's policy of
boycotting the legislative councils after the
suspension of the Non-Cooperation
Movement.

•
•

•

•

The primary strategy of the Swaraj Party was
"Council-entry."
Their stated aim was to contest elections and
enter the legislative councils not to
cooperate with the government, but to
obstruct its functioning from the inside.
They planned to use the councils as a
platform for nationalist propaganda, expose
the undemocratic nature of the British
administration, and create deadlocks,
thereby aiming to wreck the Government
from within.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q6. Write the correct chronological order of the
following annexations by the Britishers:
1. Oudh
2. Punjab
3. Upper Burma
Select the correct answer from the codes given below
:
(a) 3 2 1
(b) 1 2 3
(c) 1 3 2
(d) 2 1 3
The correct option is (d).
• To arrange these annexations in the correct
chronological order:
• The annexation of Punjab was completed in
1849 by Lord Dalhousie after the defeat of
the Sikh forces in the Second Anglo-Sikh War.
• The annexation of Oudh (Awadh) was carried
out in 1856, also by Lord Dalhousie, on the
pretext of misgovernance by the Nawab,
Wajid Ali Shah. This was one of the final
major annexations before the Revolt of 1857.
• The annexation of Upper Burma occurred
much later, in 1886, following the Third
Anglo-Burmese War, during the viceroyalty of
Lord Dufferin.
• Therefore, the correct sequence is Punjab (2),
Oudh (1), and then Upper Burma (3).
• Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q7. What was the objectives of Home Rule
Movement?
(a) Self-government at all levels
(b) Eradication of evils like Sati in the Indian
community
(c) Participation in Indian National Movement
(d) Boycott of foreign goods
The correct option is (a).
• The Home Rule Movement, launched in 1916
by Annie Besant and Bal Gangadhar Tilak,
had a clear and primary objective.
• Inspired by the Irish Home Rule movement,
its main goal was to achieve self-government
for India within the framework of the British
Empire.
• This was often expressed as demanding
"Home Rule" or "Swaraj".
• The movement aimed to build a strong
nationalist sentiment and pressure the
British government to grant India substantial
political autonomy and control over its own
administration at all levels.
• While it involved participation in the national
movement, its specific goal was selfgovernment.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q8. What is most significant regarding partition of
Bengal in 1905?
(a) The English undertook partition without consulting
the local population
(b) The English established that they were strong
people
(c) Muslims were separated from other people
(d) This paved the way for involvement of people in
national movement
The correct option is (d).
• The Partition of Bengal in 1905, carried out by
Lord Curzon, was a watershed moment in the
history of Indian nationalism.
• While the official reason given was
administrative efficiency, the real motive was
to weaken the nerve center of Indian
nationalism by dividing Bengalis on linguistic
and religious lines.

•

•

•

•

The most significant outcome of this act was
the massive and unprecedented popular
reaction it provoked.
The anti-partition agitation evolved into the
Swadeshi Movement, which saw the use of
new techniques like boycott of foreign goods,
mass processions, and the promotion of
national education.
This was the first time the freedom struggle
moved beyond the educated elite and paved
the way for the mass involvement of
ordinary people in the national movement,
transforming its character from an elite-led
agitation to a popular struggle.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q9. What is the correct chronological order of the
following?
1. First Round Table Conference
2. Gandhi-Irwin Pact
3. Simon Commission
4. Cripps Mission
Select the correct answer from the codes given below:
(a) 3 1 2 4
(b) 1 2 3 4
(c) 2 1 3 4
(d) 4 3 2 1
The correct option is (a).
• To arrange these events in the correct
chronological order:
• The Simon Commission, an all-white
commission to review the constitutional
progress in India, was appointed in 1927 and
arrived in India in 1928.
• The First Round Table Conference was held in
London in 1930-31 to discuss constitutional
reforms, but it was boycotted by the Indian
National Congress.
• The Gandhi-Irwin Pact was signed on March
5, 1931, between Mahatma Gandhi and the
Viceroy, Lord Irwin. This pact led to the
suspension of the Civil Disobedience
Movement and Congress's agreement to
participate in the Second Round Table
Conference.

•

•
•

The Cripps Mission was sent to India much
later, in 1942, during World War II, to secure
Indian cooperation for the war effort.
Therefore, the correct sequence is 3, 1, 2, 4.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q10. Who was the person behind conversion of East
India Company from a trading company into a
regional power?
(a) Lord Warren Hastings
(b) Lord Clive
(c) Lord Dalhousie
(d) Lord Wellesley
The correct option is (b).
• Robert Clive was the key figure responsible
for transforming the English East India
Company from a mere trading entity into a
dominant political and territorial power in
India.
• His victory at the Battle of Plassey in 1757
was the pivotal event.
• It gave the Company control over the rich
province of Bengal and established its
military supremacy.
• Following this, in 1765, Clive secured the
Diwani (the right to collect revenue) of
Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa from the Mughal
Emperor.
• This act formally established the Company as
a regional ruler with vast financial and
administrative power, marking its transition
from a commercial enterprise to a ruling
authority.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q11. Who had founded the Asiatic Society of Bengal
in 1784?
(a) Lord Clive

(b) Lord Warren Hastings
(c) Queen Victoria
(d) William Jones
The correct option is (d).
• The Asiatic Society of Bengal was founded in
Calcutta (now Kolkata) on January 15, 1784.
• Its founder was Sir William Jones, a British
philologist and judge who had a deep interest
in ancient Indian culture, languages, and
texts.
• The society was established to encourage and
promote Oriental studies, including the study
of Indian history, languages (especially
Sanskrit), arts, and sciences.
• It played a crucial role in the rediscovery and
translation of many ancient Indian literary
and philosophical works, making them
accessible to the Western world.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q12. Assertion (A): The revolt of 1857 failed. Reason
(R): It lacked adequate organization and military
power.
(a) Both A and R are correct but R is not the correct
explanation of A.
(b) A is correct but R is incorrect
(c) A is incorrect but R is correct
(d) Both A and R are correct and R is correct
explanation of A
The correct option is (d).
• Assertion (A) is true. The Revolt of 1857,
despite being widespread and shaking the
foundations of British rule, ultimately failed
to overthrow the British and was suppressed
by 1858.
• Reason (R) is also true and provides a primary
explanation for the failure.
• The revolt suffered from several critical
weaknesses. It lacked centralized leadership,
a unified plan, and adequate organization.
• Different groups fought for different reasons
with little coordination.
• Furthermore, the rebels were outmatched by
the superior military power, discipline, and
resources of the British forces.

•

•

Since the lack of organization and military
strength were key reasons for the failure of
the revolt, both statements are correct and R
is the correct explanation of A.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q13. Assertion (A): Lal, Bal and Pal formed extremist
group in the Indian National Congress. Reason (R):
They were revolutionary leaders
(a) A and R both are incorrect
(b) A is correct but R is incorrect
(c) A and R are correct and R is the correct explanation
of A
(d) A and R are correct but R is not the correct
explanation of A
The correct option is (b).
• Assertion (A) is correct. Lala Lajpat Rai (Lal),
Bal Gangadhar Tilak (Bal), and Bipin Chandra
Pal (Pal) were the three most prominent
leaders who formed the core of the Extremist
(or "Garam Dal") faction within the Indian
National Congress.
• They advocated for more assertive and
radical methods to achieve Swaraj.
• Reason (R) is incorrect in its specific
terminology.
• While they were radical nationalists, the term
"revolutionary leaders" is typically reserved
for those who believed in and practiced
armed struggle and secret societies (like
Bhagat Singh or the members of Anushilan
Samiti).
• Lal, Bal, and Pal, while extremist in their
political demands and methods of mass
agitation, did not primarily advocate for
armed revolution.
• Their approach was based on mass
mobilization, boycott, and passive resistance,
not revolutionary violence.
• Therefore, the assertion is correct, but the
reason is an inaccurate description of their
primary political identity.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q14. Assertion (A): In 1946 in Bombay the ratings of
the Royal Indian Navy rose in open mutiny against
the British. Reason (R): The Royal Indian Navy joined
the INA
(a) A and R both are incorrect
(b) A is correct but R is incorrect
(c) A and R are correct and R is the correct explanation
of A
(d) A and R are correct but R is not the correct
explanation of A.
The correct option is (b).
• Assertion (A) is correct. In February 1946, the
ratings (non-commissioned sailors) of the
Royal Indian Navy (RIN) in Bombay (now
Mumbai) did rise in a major open mutiny.
• They went on strike to protest against poor
service conditions, racial discrimination, and
also raised political demands like the release
of INA prisoners.
• Reason (R) is incorrect. The Royal Indian
Navy did not join the Indian National Army
(INA).
• The INA, led by Subhas Chandra Bose, was a
separate force formed with Japanese support
during World War II and had been defeated
by 1945.
• While the INA trials and the patriotic
sentiment they generated were a major
inspiration for the RIN mutineers, the two
forces never merged or acted in concert.
• Therefore, the assertion is true, but the
reason is false.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1984
Q1. The first Civil Disobedience Movement was
started by Gandhiji after the
(a) Rowlatt Act of 1919
(b) Jallianwala Bagh massacre in 1919
(c) Chauri Chaura incident in 1922
(d) Arrival of Cripps’ Mission in 1942
The correct option is (a).
• The first nationwide mass agitation launched
by Mahatma Gandhi was the Satyagraha

•

•

•

against the Rowlatt Act of 1919. This can be
considered his first all-India Civil
Disobedience Movement.
The Rowlatt Act was a repressive law that
allowed the British government to imprison
any person on mere suspicion without a trial.
Gandhi called for a nationwide hartal (strike)
and Satyagraha to protest this "Black Act."
This movement, though short-lived and
marred by violence (leading to the
Jallianwala Bagh massacre), was the first time
Gandhi attempted to mobilize the entire
nation in a campaign of civil disobedience
against a specific British law. The more
famous Civil Disobedience Movement of
1930 was a later, larger campaign.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q2. Match the following :
A. Civil Disobedience Movement : 1. B. G. Tilak
B. Indian Home Rule League : 2. Raja Ram Mohan Roy
C. Moderate leader : 3. Mahatma Gandhi
D. Brahmo Samaj : 4. G. K. Gokhale
Codes:
ABCD
(a) 3 1 4 2
(b) 1 2 3 4
(c) 2 1 4 3
(d) 3 4 2 1
The correct option is (a).
• The Civil Disobedience Movement (1930-34),
which began with the Dandi March, was led
by Mahatma Gandhi. (A-3)
• The Indian Home Rule League was founded in
1916 by two main leaders, one of whom was

•

•

•
•

the prominent extremist leader B. G. Tilak.
(B-1)
G. K. Gokhale was the most respected
Moderate leader of the Indian National
Congress in the pre-Gandhian era and was
considered a political mentor by Gandhi
himself. (C-4)
The Brahmo Samaj, a pioneering socioreligious reform movement, was founded by
Raja Ram Mohan Roy in 1828. (D-2)
The correct matching is A-3, B-1, C-4, D-2.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q3. Who among the following attended all the three
Round Table Conferences?
(a) Madan Mohan Malaviya
(b) Mahatma Gandhi
(c) Sarojini Naidu
(d) B. R. Ambedkar
The correct option is (d).
• The three Round Table Conferences were
held in London between 1930 and 1932 to
discuss constitutional reforms for India.
• Dr. B. R. Ambedkar was a key participant who
attended all three conferences. He
consistently represented the interests of the
Depressed Classes (Dalits), arguing forcefully
for their political rights and social welfare,
including the demand for separate
electorates.
• Mahatma Gandhi, representing the Indian
National Congress, attended only the Second
Round Table Conference in 1931. Other
leaders like Malaviya and Naidu also did not
attend all three sessions.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q4. Who among the following being a renowned
poet was a good painter also?
(a) Rabindra Nath Tagore
(b) Amrita Shergil
(c) A. N. Tagore
(d) S. N. Bannerjee
The correct option is (a).

•

•

•

•

Rabindranath Tagore was a polymath and
one of the most celebrated figures of the
Bengal Renaissance. While he is worldrenowned as a poet, winning the Nobel Prize
in Literature in 1913 for his collection
Gitanjali, he was also a gifted artist.
Tagore took up drawing and painting late in
his life, around the age of sixty. He developed
a unique, modern, and highly expressive
style, creating thousands of artworks. His
paintings were exhibited successfully across
Europe and are considered a significant
contribution to modern Indian art.
Amrita Shergil was a famous painter but not
a renowned poet. A. N. Tagore
(Abanindranath Tagore) was a painter, but
Rabindranath is the figure known for being
both a great poet and a painter.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q5. Which of the following Acts gave representation
to Indians for the first time in legislatures?
(a) The Indian Councils Act, 1909
(b) The Indian Councils Act, 1919
(c) The Government of India Act, 1935
(d) None of the above
The correct option is (a).
• While the Indian Councils Act of 1861 and
1892 introduced a limited number of nonofficial Indian members to the legislative
councils, these members were nominated,
not elected.
• The Indian Councils Act of 1909, also known
as the Morley-Minto Reforms, was the first
legislation to introduce the principle of
elections and provide for direct
representation of Indians in the legislatures.
• Although the franchise was extremely limited
and it controversially introduced separate
electorates for Muslims, it marked the first
time that Indians could be elected to the
legislative councils, marking a significant,
albeit flawed, step towards representative
government.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q6. Who among the following Governors-General
introduced the Dual System of Government?
(a) Lord Wellesley
(b) Lord Canning
(c) Lord Clive
(d) Lord Rippon
The correct option is (c).
• The Dual System of Government was
introduced in Bengal in 1765 by Robert Clive
after the Battle of Buxar.
• Under this system, the administration was
divided into two parts: Diwani (the right to
collect revenue) and Nizamat (civil and
criminal administration).
• The East India Company took control of the
Diwani, giving it power over the province's
finances, while the Nizamat was left in the
hands of the Nawab of Bengal. This created a
system where the Company held all the real
power (power of the purse) without having
the direct responsibility of governance,
leading to massive corruption and
exploitation. The system was later abolished
by Warren Hastings in 1772.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q7. Which of the following pairs is correct?
(a) Mahatma Gandhi—”Swaraj is my birth right”
(b) Jawaharlal Nehru—”Truth and nonviolence are my
God”
(c) Sardar Patel—”Dilli Chalo”
(d) Subhash Chandra Bose—”Give me blood, I will give
you freedom”
The correct option is (d).
• This pair is correct. The famous and powerful
slogan, "Give me blood, and I will give you
freedom!" (Tum mujhe khoon do, main
tumhe aazadi doonga!) was given by Subhash
Chandra Bose to motivate the soldiers of the
Indian National Army (INA).
• The other pairs are incorrect:
o "Swaraj is my birthright" was the
slogan of Bal Gangadhar Tilak.

o

Q8. Which of the following laws was passed during
the second half of the nineteenth century under the
Social and Religious reform movements?
(a) Formation of Theosophical Society
(b) Sanction of inter-caste and inter-communal
marriages
(c) Opening of Aligarh Muslim University
(d) None of the above
The correct option is (b).
• The second half of the 19th century (18501900) was a period of significant social reform
in India, often pushed by reformers and
enacted into law by the British.
• The Special Marriage Act of 1872 was a
landmark piece of legislation that sanctioned
inter-caste and inter-communal marriages. It
allowed Indians to get married outside their
own religious or caste communities through a
civil ceremony, provided they declared that
they did not belong to any religion. This was a
major step towards breaking down
traditional social barriers.
• The Theosophical Society was formed in
1875, but this was the formation of an
organization, not a law. Aligarh Muslim
University was established much later in
1920.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

4. The Indian Independence Act, 1947 —
Provincial Legislature
Select the correct answer from the codes given below:
(a) 1 and 4
(b) 2 and 3
(c) 1 and 3
(d) 2 and 4
The correct option is (b).
• Let's evaluate each pair:
o Pair 1 is incorrect. The Indian
Councils Act, 1909 (Morley-Minto
Reforms) introduced separate
electorates but did not grant
Provincial Autonomy. Provincial
Autonomy was a feature of the 1935
Act.
o Pair 2 is correct. The Government of
India Act, 1919 (Montagu-Chelmsford
Reforms) was famous for the
Introduction of Dyarchy (dual
government) in the provinces.
o Pair 3 is correct. The Government of
India Act, 1935, proposed the
establishment of an All-India
Federation, which would include both
British Indian provinces and the
princely states.
o Pair 4 is incorrect. The Indian
Independence Act, 1947, was about
the partition and transfer of power,
creating two independent dominions.
It did not focus on the structure of
provincial legislatures.
• Therefore, only pairs 2 and 3 are correctly
matched.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q9. Which of the following pairs are correct?
1. The Indian Councils Act, 1909 — Provincial
Autonomy
2. The Government of India Act, 1919 —
Introduction of Dyarchy
3. The Government of India Act, 1935 —
Proposal for a Federation of India

Q10. The partition of Bengal was undertaken with
the view of
(a) Suppressing the revolt arisen
(b) Satisfying the Hindus and the Muslims
(c) Meeting the demand by the Hindus and the
Muslims for a separate State
(d) Dividing the Hindus of the West and the East

•

"Truth and non-violence are my God"
is a core tenet of Mahatma Gandhi's
philosophy.
o "Dilli Chalo" (On to Delhi) was also a
slogan given by Subhash Chandra
Bose to the INA.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Bengal and increasing Hindu-Muslim tensions
The correct option is (d).
• The Partition of Bengal in 1905, orchestrated
by Viceroy Lord Curzon, was a classic example
of the British policy of "divide and rule."
• While the official reason given was
administrative convenience, the underlying
political motive was to weaken the growing
nationalist movement, of which Bengal was
the nerve center.
• The partition was deliberately designed to
create a new Muslim-majority province of
Eastern Bengal and Assam, thereby dividing
the Bengali-speaking population on religious
lines. This was intended to drive a wedge
between Hindus and Muslims, disrupt
nationalist unity, and create Hindu-Muslim
tensions that the British could exploit to
maintain their rule.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q11. Match the columns:
A. “Freedom at Midnight” : 1. Mahatma Gandhi
B. “Nineteen Eighty Four” : 2. Sarojini Naidu
C. “My Experiments with Truth” : 3. Larry Collins and
Dominique Lapierre
D. “The Song of India” : 4. George Orwell
Select the correct answer from the codes given below:
ABCD
(a) 3 4 2 1
(b) 4 1 2 3
(c) 3 4 1 2
(d) 4 1 3 2
The correct option is (c).
• Freedom at Midnight is a famous non-fiction
book about the events surrounding India's
independence and partition, written by Larry
Collins and Dominique Lapierre. (A-3)
• Nineteen Eighty-Four is a classic dystopian
novel written by the English author George
Orwell. (B-4)
• The Story of My Experiments with Truth is
the autobiography of Mahatma Gandhi. (C-1)

•

•
•

The Song of India is a collection of patriotic
poems written by Sarojini Naidu, known as
the "Nightingale of India." (D-2)
The correct matching is A-3, B-4, C-1, D-2.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q12. Lord Wellesley greatly extended the British
dominion in India through
(a) Doctrine of Lapse
(b) Subsidiary Alliance
(c) Partitioning of Indian States
(d) Annexing the princely States
The correct option is (b).
• Lord Wellesley, who was Governor-General
from 1798 to 1805, was an expansionist who
greatly extended British influence and
territory in India.
• His primary instrument for achieving this was
the Subsidiary Alliance system. Under this
system, an Indian ruler was forced to accept
the stationing of a British force in his territory
and pay for its maintenance. In return, the
British promised protection.
• This alliance effectively stripped the Indian
state of its sovereignty, making it a
subordinate or "subsidiary" of the British.
Wellesley used this system to bring major
states like Hyderabad, Awadh, and the
Marathas under British control, dramatically
expanding the British dominion.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q13. Gandhiji started the Dandi March
(a) To demonstrate against the policies of British
empire
(b) To break the salt law
(c) To boycott foreign goods
(d) None of the above
The correct option is (b).
• The Dandi March, or Salt March, of 1930 had
a very specific and symbolic objective that
was central to the launch of the Civil
Disobedience Movement.
• The primary goal was to protest and defy the
1882 British Salt Act, which gave the British a
monopoly on the collection and manufacture
of salt and imposed a heavy tax on it.
• By marching to the sea at Dandi and making
salt from the seawater, Mahatma Gandhi and
his followers were directly and publicly
breaking the salt law, an act of defiance that
resonated with millions of Indians and
symbolized the rejection of British authority.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q14. Sepoy mutiny of 1857 failed due to the
(a) Lack of effective organisation
(b) Lack of unity of purpose
(c) Lack of unified system of leadership
(d) All of the above
The correct option is (d).
• The Revolt of 1857, despite its scale and
initial success, ultimately failed due to a
combination of several critical weaknesses.
• Lack of effective organization: The revolt was
largely spontaneous and uncoordinated.
There was no central command structure to
direct the efforts of the rebels across different
regions.
• Lack of unity of purpose: Different groups
fought for different reasons. While some
fought to restore the Mughal emperor,
others fought to regain their lost kingdoms or
to address specific grievances, leading to a
fragmented effort.
• Lack of unified system of leadership:
Although there were many brave local

•
•

leaders like Rani Lakshmibai and Nana Saheb,
there was no single, overarching leader who
could unite all the rebel forces under one
banner.
All these factors contributed to the failure of
the mutiny.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q15. The Swaraj Party was founded by Motilal Nehru
in
(a) 1947
(b) 1952
(c) 1920
(d) 1923
The correct option is (d).
• The Swaraj Party was formed following the
suspension of the Non-Cooperation
Movement, which led to a debate within the
Congress about future strategy.
• The party was formally established on
January 1, 1923.
• Its founders were Chittaranjan Das (C.R. Das),
who was the President, and Motilal Nehru,
who served as the Secretary. They advocated
for "Council-entry" to obstruct the
government from within. The provided
answer key says 1920, which is incorrect. The
party was formed after the Gaya session of
Congress in December 1922.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q16. Assertion (A): The First War of Indian
Independence started in 1857. Reason (R): The
soldiers refused to use the greased cartridges.
(a) if A and R both are correct and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) if A and R both are correct but R is not the correct
explanation of A
(c) if A is incorrect but R is correct
(d) if A is correct but R is incorrect
The correct option is (b).
• Assertion (A) is correct. The Revolt of 1857 is
often referred to by Indian nationalists as the
First War of Indian Independence.

•

•

•

Reason (R) is also correct. The immediate
trigger or spark for the revolt was indeed the
refusal of Indian soldiers (sepoys) to use the
new Enfield rifle cartridges, which were
rumored to be greased with cow and pig fat,
offending both Hindu and Muslim religious
sentiments.
However, the greased cartridges were only
the immediate cause, not the sole or
fundamental reason for the war. The war was
the culmination of a century of political,
economic, social, and military grievances
against British rule. Therefore, while both
statements are true, the reason (R) is only the
immediate spark and not the complete
explanation for the broader war (A).
Hence, option (b) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1985

Q1. Arrange the following in a chronological order
1. Foundation of Muslim League
2. Jallianwala Bagh Massacre
3. Khilafat Movement
4. Civil Disobedience Movement
The correct order is
(a) 1, 3, 2, 4
(b) 1, 2, 3, 4
(c) 4, 2, 1, 2
(d) 2, 4, 1, 3
The correct option is (b).
• Foundation of Muslim League: The All-India
Muslim League was founded in Dacca (now
Dhaka) in 1906. It was established to
safeguard the political rights and interests of
Muslims in British India.
• Jallianwala Bagh Massacre: This tragic event,
where British troops fired on an unarmed
crowd, took place in Amritsar on April 13,
1919.
• Khilafat Movement: This was a pan-Islamic
political protest campaign launched by
Muslims in British India to influence the
British government and to protect the
Ottoman Caliphate. It gained momentum

•

•
•

right after World War I and was at its peak
from 1919 to 1922.
Civil Disobedience Movement: This major
nationwide movement, initiated by Mahatma
Gandhi with the Dandi March, was launched
in 1930.
Therefore, the correct chronological order is
1, 2, 3, 4.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q2. ‘Go back to the Vedas’ was the motto of
(a) Swami Vivekananda
(b) Raja Ram Mohan Roy
(c) Swami Dayanand Saraswati
(d) M. G. Ranade
The correct option is (c).
• The slogan "Go back to the Vedas" was the
central message of Swami Dayanand
Saraswati, the founder of the Arya Samaj
(1875).
• He was a prominent Hindu reformer who
believed that many of the social evils and
superstitions prevalent in 19th-century
Hinduism (like idol worship, the rigid caste
system, and child marriage) were later,
corrupt additions that were not sanctioned by
the original scriptures.
• He called for a rejection of these practices
and a return to the pristine, monotheistic,
and rational principles he found in the Vedas,
which he considered to be the infallible word
of God and the true foundation of Hinduism.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q3. Arrange the following in a chronological order
1. Dandi March
2. Simon Commission
3. Poona Pact
4. Gandhi Irwin Pact
The correct order is
(a) 1, 2, 3, 4
(b) 3, 2, 1, 4
(c) 2, 1, 4, 3
(d) 3, 4, 1, 2
The correct option is (c).

•

•

•

•

•
•

Simon Commission: The all-white statutory
commission, headed by Sir John Simon, was
appointed by the British government in 1927
to review the constitutional progress in India.
It arrived in India in 1928 and was met with
widespread boycotts.
Dandi March: This famous Salt March was
initiated by Mahatma Gandhi on March 12,
1930, marking the beginning of the Civil
Disobedience Movement.
Gandhi-Irwin Pact: This political agreement
was signed between Mahatma Gandhi and
the Viceroy, Lord Irwin, on March 5, 1931. It
led to the suspension of the Civil
Disobedience Movement and Congress's
participation in the Second Round Table
Conference.
Poona Pact: This agreement was signed
between Dr. B. R. Ambedkar and Mahatma
Gandhi (who was on a fast unto death) in
September 1932. It resolved the issue of
separate electorates for the Depressed
Classes that had been granted by the
Communal Award.
Therefore, the correct chronological order is
2, 1, 4, 3.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q4. Which of the following statements is true about
Annie Besant?
1. She was the President of Indian National
Congress
2. She was the founder of the Asiatic Society
3. She was the founder of the Theosophical
Society
Codes :
(a) 1 and 2
(b) 1 and 3
(c) 1 only
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (c).
• Statement 1 is true. Annie Besant was a
prominent leader of the Indian National
Congress and was elected as its President for

•

•

•
•

the Calcutta session in 1917. She was the first
woman to hold this position.
Statement 2 is false. The Asiatic Society was
founded in 1784 by Sir William Jones, long
before Annie Besant's time.
Statement 3 is false. The Theosophical
Society was founded in New York in 1875 by
Madame H. P. Blavatsky and Colonel H. S.
Olcott. Annie Besant joined the society later
and became its president in 1907, but she was
not its founder.
Therefore, only the first statement is correct.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q5. Which of the following proposals were made by
the Cripps Mission?
1. Participation of Indian States in the
Constitution making body
2. Forming separate State for the Muslims
3. Election of the Constitution-making body by
the members of the lower house of the
Provincial Legislature
Select the correct answer from the codes given
below:
(a) 1 and 2
(b) 1 and 3
(c) 2 and 3
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (b).
• The Cripps Mission of 1942 proposed a
framework for India's future constitution
after World War II.
• Statement 1 is true. The mission proposed
that the constitution-making body would
include representatives from both British
India and the princely Indian States.
• Statement 2 is false. The mission did not
explicitly propose the formation of a
separate state for Muslims. However, it
included a controversial clause that allowed
any province unwilling to join the new Indian
Union to opt out and form its own separate
union. This was seen by the Congress as an
implicit acceptance of the principle of
Pakistan, which is why they rejected it. But

•

•
•

the proposal itself was for provincial
secession, not a pre-determined separate
Muslim state.
Statement 3 is true. The mission proposed
that the members of the constitution-making
body from British India would be elected by
the members of the lower houses of the
Provincial Legislatures through a system of
proportional representation.
Therefore, statements 1 and 3 are correct
proposals made by the mission.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q6. Match the columns :
A. Rajendra Prasad
B. Dilip Mukherjee
C. S. N. Bannerjee
D. Mahatma Gandhi

2. “India Divided”
3. “My Experiments with Truth”
4. “The Terrorists”
Select the correct answer from the codes given
below:

(b) 2 4 1 3
(c) 4 2 3 1
(d) 2 4 3 1
The correct option is (b).
• Rajendra Prasad, the first President of India,
wrote the book India Divided, which analyzed
the issue of the partition of India. (A-2)
• Dilip Mukherjee was a journalist and author
who wrote the book The Terrorists, which
dealt with the Naxalite movement. (B-4)
• S. N. Bannerjee (Surendranath Bannerjee), a
pioneer nationalist leader, wrote his
autobiography titled A Nation in the Making.
(C-1)
• Mahatma Gandhi wrote his famous
autobiography, The Story of My Experiments
with Truth. (D-3)
• The correct matching is A-2, B-4, C-1, D-3.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q7. “Poverty and Un-British Rule in India” was
written by
(a) Dadabhai Naoroji
(b) Mahatma Gandhi
(c) Sardar Patel
(d) Madan Mohan Malviya
The correct option is (a).
• The seminal work Poverty and Un-British Rule
in India was written by Dadabhai Naoroji, the
"Grand Old Man of India."
• Published in 1901, this book was a powerful
critique of the economic impact of British
colonialism.
• In it, Naoroji meticulously detailed his "Drain
Theory," arguing that Britain was
systematically draining wealth from India
through various means, which was the
primary cause of India's poverty. This work
became a foundational text for the Indian
nationalist movement's economic critique of
British rule.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q8. The founder of Asiatic Society was
(a) William Jones
(b) Annie Besant
(c) William Kerry
(d) Havell
The correct option is (a).
• The Asiatic Society (later the Asiatic Society
of Bengal) was founded in Calcutta on
January 15, 1784.
• Its founder was Sir William Jones, a British
philologist, judge, and scholar with a

•

•

profound interest in ancient Indian culture
and languages.
The society was established to encourage and
promote the study of Asian history, arts,
sciences, and literature, and it played a
pivotal role in the field of Indology and the
rediscovery of India's ancient past.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q9. Which of the following Acts was introduced by
the Britishers to remove the shortcomings of the
Regulating Act?
(a) Pitt’s India Act, 1784
(b) Rowlatt Act
(c) The Charter Act of 1793
(d) Government of India Act 1919
The correct option is (a).
• The Regulating Act of 1773 was the first
attempt by the British Parliament to regulate
the affairs of the East India Company, but it
had several significant flaws and ambiguities,
leading to conflicts between the GovernorGeneral and his council, and between the
company and the Supreme Court.
• To address these shortcomings and to
establish greater parliamentary control over
the company's administration in India, the
British government introduced Pitt's India
Act in 1784.
• This act established a Board of Control in
Britain to oversee the civil, military, and
revenue affairs of the company, thereby
creating a system of "dual control" and
rectifying many of the defects of the 1773
Act.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q10. Rajagopalachari’s formula was
(a) Merger of princely States
(b) Formulation of a separate Muslim State
(c) Plebiscite to Muslims whether they should form a
separate State
(d) Forming a Constituent Assembly
The correct option is (c).
• The Rajagopalachari Formula (or C.R.
Formula) was a proposal put forward by C.
Rajagopalachari in 1944 to resolve the
political deadlock between the Indian
National Congress and the Muslim League.
• The formula proposed that after the war, a
commission would demarcate the contiguous
Muslim-majority districts in the north-west
and north-east of India. In these demarcated
areas, a plebiscite of all the inhabitants
would be held to decide whether they wanted
to form a separate sovereign state.
• Essentially, it was a proposal to allow
Muslims in specific regions to vote on the
question of forming a separate state
(Pakistan), but only after the British had
transferred power. The formula was
ultimately rejected by Muhammad Ali Jinnah.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q11. Swaraj Party was formed after the failure of
(a) Quit India Movement
(b) Satyagraha Movement
(c) Non-cooperation Movement
(d) Civil Disobedience Movement
The correct option is (c).
• The Swaraj Party was formed in January 1923
in the direct aftermath of the suspension of
the Non-Cooperation Movement.
• Mahatma Gandhi had abruptly called off the
Non-Cooperation Movement in February
1922 after the Chauri Chaura incident. This
led to a period of disillusionment and a
debate within the Congress about the future
course of action.
• One faction, led by C.R. Das and Motilal
Nehru, argued for ending the boycott of
legislative councils and entering them to

•

o

obstruct the government from within. This
disagreement led them to form the Swaraj
Party.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q12. Gokaldas Tejpal Sanskrit Pathshala was the
place where
(a) The first session of Indian National Congress was
held
(b) Max Mueller came to study Sanskrit
(c) A.O. Hume used to live
(d) Gandhiji made his first Ashram
The correct option is (a).
• The Gokuldas Tejpal Sanskrit College
(Pathshala) in Bombay (now Mumbai) holds a
historic place in the Indian freedom struggle.
• It was the venue for the first session of the
Indian National Congress, which was held
from December 28 to 31, 1885.
• The session was attended by 72 delegates
from across India and was presided over by
W. C. Bannerjee, marking the formal
beginning of the organized national
movement.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q13. Which of the following pairs is wrongly
matched?
(a) New India — Henry Cotton
(b) Khilafat Movement — Maulana Azad
(c) Communal award — McDonald
(d) Bardoli satyagrah — Sardar Patel
The correct option is (a).
• This pair is wrongly matched. The newspaper
New India was started by Annie Besant in
1914 to advocate for the Home Rule
Movement. Henry Cotton was a British
official and a former Congress president, but
he was not associated with this newspaper.
• The other pairs are correct:
o Maulana Abul Kalam Azad was a
prominent leader of the Khilafat
Movement.

•

The Communal Award of 1932 was
announced by the British Prime
Minister Ramsay MacDonald.
o The Bardoli Satyagraha of 1928 was
successfully led by Sardar Vallabhbhai
Patel, which earned him the title
"Sardar."
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q14. Arrange the following in a chronological order
1. Surat Split
2. Formation of Muslim League
3. Partition of Bengal
4. Regulating Act
The correct Order is:
(a) 1, 2, 3, 4
(b) 4, 3, 2, 1
(c) 2, 1, 4, 3
(d) 2, 3, 4, 1
The correct option is (b).
• To arrange these events in chronological
order:
• The Regulating Act was passed by the British
Parliament in 1773 to control the activities of
the East India Company.
• The Partition of Bengal was announced and
implemented by Lord Curzon in 1905.
• The Formation of the Muslim League took
place in Dacca in 1906.
• The Surat Split, where the Indian National
Congress split into Moderates and
Extremists, occurred during the annual
session in 1907.
• Therefore, the correct sequence is 4, 3, 2, 1.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q15. “Anushilan Samithi” was the terrorist
organisation with its headquarters at
(a) Lahore
(b) Calcutta
(c) Chandigarh
(d) Islamabad
The correct option is (b).

•

•

•

•

The Anushilan Samiti was one of the most
prominent secret revolutionary organizations
in Bengal during the early 20th century.
It was founded in 1902 in Calcutta (now
Kolkata) by leaders like Pramathanath Mitra,
with associates like Aurobindo Ghosh and
Barindra Kumar Ghosh.
The Samiti promoted revolutionary violence
to overthrow British rule and was involved in
several assassinations and armed actions. It
had a major center in Dacca as well, but its
origins and main headquarters were in
Calcutta, the hub of revolutionary activities
at the time. The term "terrorist" was used by
the British; they are regarded as
revolutionary freedom fighters in India.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q16. Who declared, “Swaraj had ‘stunk in the
nostrils’ and that without adequate discipline and
restraint on the part of the people the movement
had proved to be a ‘Himalayan blunder’.”
(a) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
(b) Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel
(c) Mahatma Gandhi
(d) Subash Chandra Bose
The correct option is (c).
• This famous statement was made by
Mahatma Gandhi when he decided to call off
the nationwide Satyagraha against the
Rowlatt Act in April 1919.
• The protests had turned violent in several
places, culminating in the Jallianwala Bagh
massacre. Gandhi was deeply pained by the
violence perpetrated by the crowds.
• He confessed that he had committed a
"Himalayan blunder" by calling upon the
people to launch a civil disobedience
movement before they were fully trained and
disciplined in the principles of non-violence
(Ahimsa). The phrase "Swaraj had stunk in
the nostrils" expressed his deep anguish that
the quest for freedom was being tainted by
violence.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q17. The first political association in the Bombay
Presidency was established by
(a) Gopal Krishna Gokhale in 1905
(b) S. N. Bannerjee in 1894
(c) Dadabhai Naoroji in 1852
(d) Annie Besant in 1924
The correct option is (c).
• The first major political association in the
Bombay Presidency was the Bombay
Association, which was established on August
26, 1852.
• Dadabhai Naoroji was one of its key
founders, along with other prominent figures
like Jagannath Shankarshet and Dr. Bhau Daji
Lad.
• This association was one of the earliest
organized political bodies in India, predating
the Indian National Congress, and it aimed to
voice Indian grievances and suggest
administrative reforms to the British
government.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q18. Match the columns :
A. Sir John Marshall
B. H. T. Prinsep
C. A. K. Coomara Swamy
1. Glory of Indian Art
2. History of Indian Culture
3. Writer during the time of Lord Hastings
Select the correct answer from the codes given below:
ABC
(a) 1 2 3
(b) 3 2 1
(c) 2 3 1
(d) 2 1 3
The correct option is (c).
• Sir John Marshall was the Director-General of
the Archaeological Survey of India from 1902
to 1928. He oversaw the excavations of
Harappa and Mohenjo-daro, which revealed
the Indus Valley Civilization. His work was
fundamental to uncovering and
understanding the ancient History of Indian
Culture. (A-2)

•

•

•
•

Henry Thoby Prinsep (H. T. Prinsep) was a
British civil servant in India during the early
19th century. He served under several
Governors-General, including Lord Hastings,
and was a prolific writer on Indian political
and administrative affairs of that period. (B3)
Ananda Kentish Coomaraswamy (A. K.
Coomaraswamy) was a pioneering historian
and philosopher of Indian art. He is
celebrated for his profound writings that
interpreted and championed the Glory of
Indian Art and aesthetics to the world. (C-1)
The correct matching is A-2, B-3, C-1.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q19. The Arctic Home of the Vedas” was written by
(a) Minoo Masani
(b) Acharya Vinoba Bhave
(c) S. Radhakrishnan
(d) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
The correct option is (d).
• The book The Arctic Home in the Vedas was
written by Bal Gangadhar Tilak while he was
imprisoned in Mandalay, Burma, between
1908 and 1914.
• In this work, Tilak proposed a controversial
and original theory that the original home of
the Aryan people was in the Arctic region
during the pre-glacial period.
• He used astronomical evidence from the
Vedas and Avestan scriptures to argue that
the descriptions of long dawns, long nights,
and revolving heavens in these ancient texts
could only correspond to conditions found in
the polar regions.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q20. Which pair among the following had the similar
views during the national movement?
(a) Subrahmaniam Bharati and Bal Gangadhar
(b) Chittaranjan Das and Motilal Nehru
(c) Chittaranjan Das and Rajagopalachari
(d) Mahatma Gandhi and Shaukat Ali
The correct option is (b).

•

•

•

•

Chittaranjan Das and Motilal Nehru shared
very similar political views, which led them to
jointly found the Swaraj Party in 1923.
After the suspension of the Non-Cooperation
Movement, both leaders belonged to the
"pro-changer" faction within the Congress.
They strongly advocated for ending the
boycott of the legislative councils and argued
for "Council-entry" as a new political strategy
to obstruct the British government from
within. This shared viewpoint was the basis
of their political partnership.
The other pairs had significant differences:
Rajagopalachari was a "no-changer" who
opposed Council-entry, and Gandhi and
Shaukat Ali were allies for a specific cause
(Khilafat) but had different long-term
political philosophies.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 1986
Q1. Match the columns :
A. M. N. Roy
B. C. R. Das
C. Chandra Shekhar Azad
D. Acharya Narendra Deva
1. Swarajist Party
2. Hindustan Socialist Republican Association
3. Congress Socialist Party
4. Communist International
Select the correct answer using the codes given
below:
ABCD
(a) 4 1 2 3

(b) 2 3 1 4
(c) 3 1 4 2
(d) 4 2 3 1
The correct option is (a).
• M. N. Roy was a pioneering Indian
communist and a prominent international
revolutionary. He was a founder of the
Communist Party of India and worked closely
with the Communist International
(Comintern), even serving as a delegate to its
congresses. (A-4)
• C. R. Das (Chittaranjan Das), along with
Motilal Nehru, was a principal founder of the
Swarajist Party (or Swaraj Party) in 1923. (B1)
• Chandra Shekhar Azad was a key
revolutionary leader and a founder of the
Hindustan Socialist Republican Association
(HSRA) in 1928, along with Bhagat Singh and
others. (C-2)
• Acharya Narendra Deva was a leading
Marxist theorist and educator who served as
the first President of the Congress Socialist
Party (CSP) when it was formed in 1934. (D-3)
• The correct matching is A-4, B-1, C-2, D-3.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q2. Indianisation of the Civil Services was done by
(a) Lord Ripon
(b) Lord Mayo
(c) Lord Lytton
(d) Lord Reading
The correct option is (c).
• The term "Indianisation" of the Civil Services
refers to the process of increasing the
number of Indians in the higher
administrative posts.
• While Lord Ripon advocated for reforms, it
was Lord Lytton who, in 1878-79, introduced
the Statutory Civil Service, allowing
appointment of some Indians of high social
standing to the covenanted civil service
without the competitive examination in
London.

•

•

•

Although this scheme was limited and
ultimately failed, it was an early, albeit
flawed, attempt at the Indianisation of the
services.
Lord Ripon later tried to reform the system
more substantively by advocating for
simultaneous examinations, but Lytton’s
Statutory Civil Service was a direct, if limited,
measure of Indianisation.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q3. The purpose of Cripps’ Mission was
(a) To give India dominion status.
(b) To introduce provincial autonomy in India.
(c) To discuss future setup for India on the basis of
the Simon Commission report.
(d) To suppress ‘Quit India’ Movement.
The correct option is (a).
• The Cripps Mission was sent to India in March
1942 during World War II.
• Its primary purpose was to secure full Indian
cooperation and support for the British war
effort.
• To achieve this, the mission offered a set of
constitutional proposals to be implemented
after the war.
• The central proposal was to give India
Dominion Status, with the right to secede
from the Commonwealth and enjoy full
autonomy.
• The mission also proposed the formation of a
constituent assembly to frame a new
constitution. However, the offer was rejected
by the Indian National Congress because it
did not grant immediate transfer of real
power.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q4. What is the correct chronological order of the
following?
A. Cabinet Mission
B. Montague - Chelmsford Reforms
C. Minto - Morley Reforms
D. Cripps’ Mission
Codes :

(a) C, B, D, A
(b) A, B, C, D
(c) B, C, D, A
(d) D, A, B, C
The correct option is (a).
• Minto-Morley Reforms (The Indian Councils
Act) were introduced in 1909. They
introduced separate electorates for Muslims.
(C)
• Montague-Chelmsford Reforms (The
Government of India Act) were introduced in
1919. They introduced the system of Dyarchy
in the provinces. (B)
• Cripps' Mission, led by Sir Stafford Cripps,
came to India in 1942 with proposals for
constitutional reforms after World War II. (D)
• Cabinet Mission was sent to India in 1946 to
discuss and plan the transfer of power from
the British government to Indian leadership.
(A)
• Therefore, the correct sequence is C, B, D, A.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q5. Ilbert Bill was prepared for abolishing judicial
disqualification based on racial distinction. The bill
was vehemently criticised by the Europeans living in
India. Who was the Governor-General at that time?
(a) Lord Ripon
(b) Lord Dalhousie
(c) Lord Curzon
(d) Lord Chelmsford
The correct option is (a).
• The Ilbert Bill was introduced in 1883 during
the viceroyalty of Lord Ripon.
• The bill was a progressive measure that
sought to end racial discrimination in the
Indian judicial system.
• It proposed to give Indian judges and
magistrates the power to try European British
subjects in criminal cases, a right they did not
possess at the time.
• The bill faced fierce opposition from the
European community in India, leading to a
"White Mutiny" and significant amendments.

•

•

The controversy exposed the deep-seated
racism of the colonial administration and
galvanized Indian nationalist sentiment.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q6. Ghadar party’ in U.S.A. was formed by
(a) V. D. Savarkar
(b) Lala Har Dayal
(c) Swami Varma
(d) Raja Pratap Singh
The correct option is (b).
• The Ghadar Party was a revolutionary
organization founded by Punjabi Indians in
the United States and Canada with the aim of
liberating India from British rule through
armed struggle.
• It was formally founded in 1913 in San
Francisco, USA.
• While Sohan Singh Bhakna was its founding
president, the intellectual and ideological
driving force behind the party was Lala Har
Dayal.
• The party’s newspaper, "The Ghadar,"
became a powerful tool for spreading
revolutionary ideas among the Indian
diaspora.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q7. Lord Cornwallis introduced
(a) Zamindari system
(b) Mahalwari system
(c) Mansabdari system
(d) None of the above
The correct option is (a).
• Lord Cornwallis, during his tenure as
Governor-General of Bengal, introduced the

•
•

•

•

•

Permanent Settlement of land revenue in
1793.
This system is also known as the Zamindari
system.
Under this system, the right to collect
revenue from a particular area of land was
auctioned to the highest bidder, who then
became the Zamindar (landlord).
The revenue to be paid to the state was fixed
permanently, but the Zamindars were free to
extract rent from tenant farmers.
This system created a new class of landlords
loyal to the British, but was often oppressive
for the peasants.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q8. Write the correct chronological order of the
following?
A. Pitt’s India Act
B. Rowlatt act
C. Regulating Act
D. Indian Council Act
Codes :
(a) A, C, D, B
(b) B, A, C, D
(c) C, A, D, B
(d) D, B, A, C
The correct option is (c).
• The Regulating Act was the first major act by
the British Parliament to control the East
India Company's affairs, passed in 1773. (C)
• Pitt's India Act was passed in 1784 to rectify
the defects of the Regulating Act and
establish greater parliamentary control over
the company. (A)
• The Indian Councils Act mentioned here most
likely refers to the Morley-Minto Reforms of
1909, a significant act in this series. (D)
• The Rowlatt Act, a repressive law, was passed
in 1919. (B)
• Therefore, the correct sequence is C, A, D, B.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q9. Montague - Chelmsford Reforms relate to
(a) Dyarchy
(b) Communalism
(c) Provincial autonomy
(d) None of the above
The correct option is (a).
• The Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms are the
basis for the Government of India Act of
1919.
• The most distinctive and significant feature of
these reforms was the introduction of
Dyarchy (rule of two) in the provinces.
• Under Dyarchy, the subjects of provincial
administration were divided into
"Transferred" subjects (administered by
Indian ministers responsible to the
legislature) and "Reserved" subjects (retained
by the British governor and his council).
• This created a complex and ultimately
unworkable system of dual governance at
the provincial level.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q10. Khilafat movement stressed on
(a) Restoration of Turkish Khalipha as the head of
Indian Muslims
(b) Reservation of seats for Muslim community in
Government services
(c) Independent Pakistan
(d) India’s Independence
The correct option is (a).
• The Khilafat Movement (1919-1924) was a
pan-Islamic political campaign launched by
Muslims in British India.
• Its central and primary demand was to
pressure the British government to preserve
the authority and territorial integrity of the
Ottoman Empire and its Sultan, regarded by
many Sunni Muslims as the Caliph (Khalifa).
• The movement's main stress was on the
restoration of the Turkish Khalifa to his preWorld War I position, ensuring his control
over the holy places of Islam.
• It was not about reservations, Pakistan, or
directly about India’s independence, although

•

it merged with the Non-Cooperation
Movement for a common anti-British cause.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1988

Q1. Which of the following is/are correctly matched?
Persons
1. Keshab Chandra Sen : Prarthana Samaj
2. Mahadev Govind Ranade : Brahmo Samaj
3. Swami Vivekanand : Ramakrishna Mission
Select the correct answer from the codes given below:
(a) 1, 2 and 3
(b) 1 and 3 only
(c) 1 only
(d) 3 only
The correct option is (d).
• Pair 1 is incorrect. The Prarthana Samaj was
founded by Atmaram Pandurang. While
Keshab Chandra Sen, a leader of the Brahmo
Samaj, inspired its formation, he was not its
founder.
• Pair 2 is incorrect. Mahadev Govind Ranade
was a key leader of the Prarthana Samaj in
Maharashtra, not the Brahmo Samaj. The
Brahmo Samaj was founded by Raja
Rammohan Roy.
• Pair 3 is correct. The Ramakrishna Mission
was founded in 1897 by Swami Vivekananda
to propagate the teachings of his guru, Sri
Ramakrishna Paramahamsa, and to carry out
social service.
• Therefore, only the third pair is correctly
matched.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q2. Match the columns :
List-1 (Authors) : List-2 (Books)
A. Mahatma Gandhi : 1. India Divided
B. Ram Manohar Lohia : 2. India Wins Freedom
C. Dr. Rajendra Prasad : 3. Hind Swaraj
D. Maulana Azad : 4. The Wheel of History
Select the correct answer from the codes given
below:
ABCD

(a) 3 4 1 2
(b) 1 3 2 4
(c) 4 3 1 2
(d) 2 4 3 1
The correct option is (a).
• Mahatma Gandhi wrote Hind Swaraj (or
Indian Home Rule) in 1909, outlining his vision
for a free India and his critique of modern
civilization. (A-3)
• Ram Manohar Lohia, a prominent socialist
leader and thinker, authored The Wheel of
History. (B-4)
• Dr. Rajendra Prasad, the first President of
India, wrote India Divided, analyzing the
partition. (C-1)
• Maulana Abul Kalam Azad wrote India Wins
Freedom, his autobiographical account of the
freedom struggle. (D-2)
• The correct matching is A-3, B-4, C-1, D-2.

Q3. Albuquerque captured Goa from the ruler of
(a) Bijapur
(b) Golkonda
(c) Ahmadnagar
(d) Vijayanagar
The correct option is (a).
• Afonso de Albuquerque was the second
governor of Portuguese India.
• In 1510, he launched a military campaign to
capture Goa.
• At that time, Goa was under the control of the
Sultan of Bijapur, Yusuf Adil Shah.
• The capture of Goa became the capital and
main base for Portuguese operations in India
for 450 years.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q4. The British rulers introduced a new system of
education in India chiefly because they wanted to
(a) Provide a class of office workers for the company
(b) Promote the work of Christian missionaries
(c) Create an awareness of scientific and technical
developments

(d) Generate the capacity for self-government
The correct option is (a).
• The primary motivation was administrative
and economic.
• The British needed subordinate staff for their
expanding administration.
• Lord Macaulay’s Minute on Education (1835)
aimed to create “a class of persons, Indian in
blood and colour, but English in taste, in
opinions, in morals, and in intellect.”
• This class would act as intermediaries and
office workers in the colonial bureaucracy.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q5. Which one of the following was the most
immediate factor for the spread of Swadeshi and
Boycott agitation during the first decade of the
present century?
(a) Curzon’s attempt to control the Universities
(b) Curzon’s design to curtail the sphere of local selfgovernment
(c) Curzon’s partition of Bengal
(d) Curzon’s plan to curb the growing popularity of the
Indian National Congress
The correct option is (c).
• The Swadeshi and Boycott Movement began
in 1905.
• The immediate factor was Lord Curzon’s
Partition of Bengal (1905).
• It was perceived as a “divide and rule” policy,
aiming to split Bengal along religious lines.
• It led to a massive popular upsurge,
promoting Swadeshi (Indian goods) and
Boycott (of British goods).
• Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q10. Match the columns :

D. Rajagopalachari
List-2

2. Swatantra Party

4. Theosophical Society of India

Select the correct answer from the codes given below:
ABCD
(a) 2 4 5 1
(b) 3 1 4 2
(c) 3 4 2 1
(d) 4 5 2 3
The correct option is (b).
• Madan Mohan Malviya was a key figure in
the Hindu Mahasabha. (A-3)
• Sohan Singh Bhakna was the founding
president of the Ghadar Movement. (B-1)
• Annie Besant was a leading figure in the
Theosophical Society of India. (C-4)
• C. Rajagopalachari founded the Swatantra
Party in 1959. (D-2)
• The correct matching is A-3, B-1, C-4, D-2.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q11. Assertion (A): The Zamindari Settlement was
introduced by Lord Cornwallis. Reason (R): The
British desired to create a landed class in India for
support.
(a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true but R is NOT a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true but R is false
(d) A is false but R is true
The correct option is (a).
• Assertion (A) is true. The Permanent
Settlement (or Zamindari Settlement) was
introduced in Bengal in 1793 by Lord
Cornwallis.
• Reason (R) is also true. The British wanted to
create a loyal landed aristocracy (Zamindars)
that would support British rule.
• Beyond securing fixed revenue, the political
motive was to create a social base loyal to
the British.
• Therefore, R correctly explains A.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 1989
Q1. Brahmo Samaj was founded by
(a) Raja Ram Mohan Roy
(b) David Hare
(c) Derozio
(d) Alexander
The correct option is (a).
• The Brahmo Samaj was a pioneering socioreligious reform movement that played a
crucial role in the Bengal Renaissance.
• It was founded in Calcutta in 1828 by Raja
Ram Mohan Roy, often called the “Father of
Modern India.”
• The Samaj was based on monotheism and
aimed to reform Hindu society by challenging
Sati, idol worship, and the caste system,
while promoting modern education and
rational thought.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q2. The “Servants of India Society” was founded by
(a) Annie Besant
(b) Gopalakrishna Gokhale
(c) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
(d) Dadabhai Naoroji
The correct option is (b).
• The Servants of India Society was established
in Pune in 1905.
• Its founder was Gopal Krishna Gokhale, a
moderate leader and mentor to Gandhi.
• The society’s objective was to train national
missionaries dedicated to selfless service.
• It focused on education, social welfare, and
upliftment of the underprivileged, becoming
one of the first secular organizations in India.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q3. “Asiatic Society of Bengal” was founded by
(a) Sir William Jones
(b) Annie Besant
(c) Lord William Bentinck
(d) Raja Ram Mohan Roy
The correct option is (a).

•
•
•

•

•

The Asiatic Society of Bengal was founded in
Calcutta on January 15, 1784.
Its founder was Sir William Jones, a British
philologist, judge, and scholar.
It aimed to advance the study of Asian
history, languages (especially Sanskrit), arts,
and sciences.
The society played a pivotal role in Indology,
leading to the translation of ancient texts and
the rediscovery of India’s heritage.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q4. Which of the following introduced a measure of
provincial autonomy?
(a) Minto-Morley reforms
(b) Montague-Chelmsford reforms
(c) Cripps’ Mission
(d) Government of India Act, 1935
The correct option is (d).
• The Government of India Act, 1935
introduced provincial autonomy.
• It abolished Dyarchy in the provinces
(introduced in 1919).
• It granted provinces self-government, making
them autonomous administrative units.
• Under this act, Indian ministers responsible to
the provincial legislature held significant
powers from 1937 onwards.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q5. Railway and Telegraphy systems were
introduced in India by
(a) Lord Dalhousie
(b) Lord Cornwallis
(c) Lord Bentinck
(d) Lord Wellesley
The correct option is (a).
• Lord Dalhousie (1848–1856) introduced
railways and telegraph as part of his
modernizing projects.
• The first railway line (Bombay–Thana)
opened in 1853.
• The telegraph line from Calcutta to Agra was
opened in 1854.
• These measures improved administration,
troop movement, and British commercial
interests.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q6. Who among the following Indians mostly
encouraged the remarriage of the widows?
(a) Jaisingh
(b) Ahilya Bai
(c) Sarfoji
(d) Raja Ram Mohan Roy
The correct option is (d).
• Raja Ram Mohan Roy was a pioneer social
reformer who advocated widow remarriage.
• He condemned the oppressive treatment of
widows in Hindu society.
• His reformist agenda extended beyond Sati,
laying the foundation for later reforms by
Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q7. Swaraj as a national demand was first made by
(a) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
(b) Dadabhai Naoroji
(c) Jawaharlal Nehru
(d) Chitaranjan Das
The correct option is (b).
• Dadabhai Naoroji, in his 1906 Calcutta
Session presidential address, formally
declared Swaraj as the goal of the Congress.

•

He compared it to the self-governing colonies
of Canada and Australia.
Tilak popularized Swaraj, but Naoroji made it
the official demand.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

•

Q8. Moderates and extremists of the Indian National
Congress differed mainly on the issue of
(a) Swaraj
(b) Swadeshi
(c) Boycott
(d) National education
The correct option is (c).
• The main issue between Moderates and
Extremists was Boycott.
• Extremists (Tilak) wanted nationwide passive
resistance, including boycott of government
institutions, beyond Bengal.
• Moderates wanted a limited boycott,
confined to foreign goods and Bengal.
• This disagreement led to the Surat Split in
1907.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.

•

•
•

Q9. The Brahmo Samaj is based on the principles of
(a) Monotheism
(b) Polytheism
(c) Atheism
(d) Monism
The correct option is (a).
• Brahmo Samaj (1828) was based on
Monotheism.
• It emphasized one formless supreme God.
• It rejected polytheism and idol worship to
return to the Upanishadic monotheistic
ideals.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q10. Banaras Hindu University was founded by
(a) Dadabhai Naoroji
(b) Gopalakrishna Gokhale
(c) Pandit Madan Mohan Malaviya
(d) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
The correct option is (c).

•
•

•

Banaras Hindu University (BHU) was founded
in 1916.
The driving force was Pandit Madan Mohan
Malaviya.
He envisioned a blend of Western scientific
knowledge and Indian culture.
Annie Besant contributed by merging Central
Hindu College.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q11. The system of ‘Dyarchy’ was first introduced by
(a) Montague-Chelmsford Reforms
(b) Minto-Morley Reforms
(c) Government of India Act, 1935
(d) None of the above
The correct option is (a).
• Dyarchy was introduced by the MontaguChelmsford Reforms (1919).
• It divided provincial subjects into
“Transferred” and “Reserved”.
• Transferred: handled by Indian ministers.
• Reserved: remained with the British
governor.
• This dual government system proved
complex and unworkable.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q12. On whose recommendations was the
Constituent Assembly formed?
(a) Mountbatten plan
(b) Cripps’ Mission plan
(c) Cabinet Mission plan
(d) Government of India Act, 1935
The correct option is (c).
• The Constituent Assembly was formed in
1946 based on the Cabinet Mission Plan.
• The Cabinet Mission (1946) proposed a single
assembly for a united India with a grouping
system for provinces.
• Elections to the assembly were held in July–
August 1946.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1990
Q1. Who among the following was a revolutionary
who later turned into a yogi and philosopher?
(a) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
(b) Aurobindo Ghosh
(c) Lala Lajpat Rai
(d) Agarkar
The correct option is (b).
• Aurobindo Ghosh (Sri Aurobindo) began his
public life as a fervent nationalist and a key
figure in the revolutionary movement in
Bengal during the early 20th century.
• He was a leader of the Extremist faction of
the Congress and was implicated in the
Alipore Bomb Case.
• After his acquittal in 1909, a profound
spiritual transformation led him to renounce
politics.
• He moved to Pondicherry in 1910, where he
dedicated the rest of his life to Integral Yoga.
• He became a renowned yogi and
philosopher, writing extensively on
spirituality, philosophy, and human
consciousness.
• His journey from political revolutionary to
spiritual master is unique in the history of the
freedom struggle.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q2. Who among the following was not a ‘no
changer’?
(a) Dr. Ansari
(b) Dr. Rajendra Prasad
(c) Sardar Patel
(d) Motilal Nehru
The correct option is (d).
• After the suspension of the Non-Cooperation
Movement (1922), two groups emerged: "Nochangers" and "Pro-changers".
• No-changers (e.g., Rajendra Prasad, Sardar
Patel, M. A. Ansari) advocated continuing the
boycott of legislative councils and focusing on
constructive work.
• Pro-changers wanted to enter legislative
councils to obstruct the government from
within.
• Motilal Nehru led this faction along with C. R.
Das, forming the Swaraj Party.
• Therefore, Motilal Nehru was not a ‘nochanger’.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q3. The I.N.A. was organised by Netaji Subhash
Chandra Bose at
(a) Rangoon
(b) Singapore
(c) Taiwan
(d) Tokyo
The correct option is (b).
• The Indian National Army (INA) was revived
and reorganized by Netaji Subhash Chandra
Bose in Singapore in July 1943.
• After his escape from India, Bose took
command of the INA in Singapore.
• There he established the Provisional
Government of Free India and gave the
famous call "Dilli Chalo".
• Singapore became the center of INA’s
operations.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q9. Who among the following led the Sepoys at

Q11. The most effective contribution made by
Dadabhai Nauroji to the cause of Indian National
Movement was that he
(a) Brought the problems of Indians to the notice of

the British
(b) Exposed the economic exploitation of India by the
British
(c) Interpreted the ancient Indian texts and restored
the self- confidence of Indians
(d) Stressed the need for eradication of all the social
evils before anything else
The correct option is (b).
• Dadabhai Naoroji articulated the Drain
Theory.
• He exposed economic exploitation of India in

• He showed how wealth was being drained to
Britain, causing India’s poverty.

Q12. Assertion (A): Ali Brothers founded the Swaraj
Party. Reason (R): The Swarajists were in favour of
