(a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct
explanation of A.
(b) Both A and R are true but R is not a correct
explanation of A.
(c) A is true but R is false.
(d) A is false but R is true.
The correct option is (d).
• Assertion (A) is false — The Swaraj Party was
founded by C. R. Das and Motilal Nehru, not
the Ali Brothers.
• Reason (R) is true — The Swarajists aimed to
enter councils and obstruct government from
within.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1991
Q1. When the Portuguese arrived in India, the
foreigners whom they encountered as trade rivals
were the
(a) Persians
(b) Arabs
(c) Dutch
(d) English
The correct option is (b).

•

•

•

•

•

Before the arrival of the Portuguese in 1498,
the lucrative sea-borne trade between India
and Europe was largely controlled by Arab
merchants.
The Arabs dominated the trade routes across
the Indian Ocean and the Red Sea. They
transported spices and other valuable goods
from Indian ports to the Levant, from where
Venetian and Genoese merchants distributed
them throughout Europe.
The arrival of the Portuguese, who
established a direct all-sea route around
Africa, challenged this Arab monopoly.
Using their superior naval power, the
Portuguese disrupted and displaced Arab
traders to control the spice trade for
themselves.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q2. The intensity of famine increased in India, under
the British rule because
(a) The failure of monsoons became frequent
(b) The drain of wealth from India was uncontrolled
(c) Of the poor distribution of food grains
(d) Discriminatory protection was extended, to food
processing industries
The correct option is (b).
• While monsoon failures were a natural cause
of famines, their devastating intensity under
British rule was due to colonial economic
policies.
• The most significant factor was the
uncontrolled drain of wealth from India.
• British policies extracted maximum revenue
and resources; high land revenue demands
impoverished the peasantry.
• This economic drain transferred wealth to
Britain, destroying the traditional economic
fabric and rural resilience, turning food
scarcities into catastrophic famines.
• Poor distribution and ineffective relief were
symptoms of this economic exploitation.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q3. The Congress Ministries resigned in November
1939 because
(a) The Provincial Autonomy proved to be a farce
(b) The Governors interfered too much in the day-today administration of the provinces
(c) India was dragged into the Second World War
without the consent of her people
(d) The British followed the policy of divide and rule
The correct option is (c).
• In 1937, the Indian National Congress formed
ministries in several provinces after elections
under the Government of India Act, 1935.
• When World War II broke out in September
1939, Lord Linlithgow declared that India was
at war with Germany without consulting
Indian representatives.
• The Congress protested this unilateral
decision, refusing to support a war effort
while India was under colonial rule.
• When the British refused to promise
independence, the Congress ministries
resigned in October–November 1939.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q4. Consider the following two statements called
Assertion (A) and Reason (R) and answer by using the
codes given below the statements.
Assertion (A): The Quit India Movement of 1942
marked the culmination of the Indian Freedom
Movement.
Reason (R): After the Quit India Movement it was only
a Question of time and determining the actual
mechanics of the transfer of power.
(a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true but R is not an explanation
of A
(c) A is true but R is false
(d) A is false but R is true
The correct option is (a).
• Assertion (A) is true: The Quit India
Movement (1942) was the final and most
intense mass struggle for independence,
marking the culmination of the movement.

•

•
•
•
•

Reason (R) is also true: The scale of the
movement convinced the British their rule
was untenable.
After 1942, the issue was not if, but when and
how power would be transferred.
The Cabinet Mission and other negotiations
were about the mechanics of transfer.
R correctly explains A.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q5. Which one of the following is the most
significant contribution of Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel
to modern India?
(a) His participation in the Quit India Movement
(b) His role in the Non-Cooperation Movement
(c) His achievement of the integration of princely
states
(d) His Presidentship of the Indian National Congress
The correct option is (c).
• Sardar Patel’s most enduring contribution
was the integration of princely states.
• At independence, there were 560+ princely
states with the option to join India, Pakistan,
or remain independent.
• As Deputy Prime Minister and Home
Minister, Patel used diplomacy, persuasion,
and firm action (e.g., Hyderabad) to secure
their accession.
• This political consolidation earned him the
title "Iron Man of India".
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q6. Federation, Indian responsibility, reservation and
safeguards were the three lynchpins of
(a) Gandhi-Irwin Pact
(b) Macdonald Award
(c) Poona Pact
(d) Radcliffe Award
The correct option is (a).
• The Gandhi–Irwin Pact (March 5, 1931) was
between Mahatma Gandhi and Lord Irwin.
• It outlined the framework for the Second
Round Table Conference.
• Key principles:

o

•
•

Federation – All-India Federation as
the future structure.
o Indian Responsibility – Responsible
government at the center.
o Reservations and Safeguards – For
defense, external affairs, minorities.
These were the three lynchpins of the pact.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q7. On whose recommendation was the Constituent
Assembly formed?
(a) Mountbatten plan
(b) Cripp’s Mission
(c) Cabinet Mission Plan
(d) Government of India Act, 1935
The correct option is (c).
• The Constituent Assembly was formed in
November 1946.
• It was based on the Cabinet Mission Plan
(1946).
• The Cabinet Mission proposed an indirect
election by provincial legislatures.
• Its plan was accepted, and elections were
held in July–August 1946.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q8. “English men are more efficient partly by
upbringing, partly by race and partly by education”.
Who said this?
(a) Rippon
(b) Lord Curzon
(c) Minto

(d) Dufferin
The correct option is (b).
• This reflects the colonial mindset of racial
superiority.
• It is attributed to Lord Curzon (Viceroy 1899–
1905).
• In 1904, his government justified keeping top
ICS posts for Englishmen, citing upbringing,
race, and education.
• This reveals the racist ideology legitimizing
colonial domination.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q9. “I have nothing to offer but blood, toil, tears and
sweat.” Who said this?
(a) Winston Churchill
(b) Jawaharlal Nehru
(c) Mahatma Gandhi
(d) Mao Tse-Tung
The correct option is (a).
• This line was delivered by Winston Churchill
in his first speech as PM to the House of
Commons on May 13, 1940.
• It was during the early days of World War II.
• The speech rallied the British for the struggle
against Nazi Germany, emphasizing sacrifice
and determination.
• It is one of the greatest political orations in
history.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 1992
Q1. Which of the following are associated with Lord
Lytton?
1. Rowlatt Act
2. The Arms Act
3. The Vernacular Press Act
4. Abolition of duties on the import of coarse
cotton goods
Select the correct answer by using the codes given
below :
(a) 1, 2 and 3
(b) 2, 3 and 4
(c) 1, 3 and 4
(d) 1, 2 and 4
The correct option is (b).
• Lord Lytton's viceroyalty (1876–1880) was
marked by several reactionary and
controversial measures.
• The Arms Act (1878): Made it a criminal
offense for Indians to keep, bear, or traffic in
arms without a license, while Europeans were
exempted. (Statement 2 is correct)
• The Vernacular Press Act (1878): Known as
the “Gagging Act”, it gave the government
powers to censor vernacular newspapers.
(Statement 3 is correct)

•

•

•
•

Abolition of duties on the import of coarse
cotton goods (1879): This benefited British
textile manufacturers and harmed the Indian
textile industry. (Statement 4 is correct)
The Rowlatt Act was passed in 1919, not
during Lytton’s period. (Statement 1 is
incorrect)
Therefore, statements 2, 3, and 4 are
associated with Lord Lytton.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q2. Which one of the following was given to King
Charles II as part of his dowry on the occasion of his
marriage with Queen Catherina of Braganz?
(a) Bombay
(b) Chandranagore
(c) Cochin
(d) Tranquebar
The correct option is (a).
• In 1661, King Charles II of England married
Catherine of Braganza, a Portuguese
princess.
• As part of her dowry, Portugal transferred
Bombay to the British Crown.
• In 1668, the Crown leased it to the East India
Company for £10 annually.
• This acquisition proved immensely valuable,
as Bombay became a major trading hub and
headquarters in western India.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q3. Which one of the following was the state that
was not annexed by Lord Dalhousie by using the
Doctrine of Lapse?
(a) Nagpur
(b) Udaipur
(c) Mysore
(d) Satara
The correct option is (c).
• The Doctrine of Lapse annexed states if rulers
died without male heirs.
• Satara (1848), Udaipur (1852), and Nagpur
(1854) were annexed under it.
• Mysore was not annexed under the Doctrine.

•

•

It came under British administration (1831)
due to misgovernance, and was restored to
its rulers in 1881 by Lord Ripon.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q4. The Indian National Congress was founded in
1885 by
(a) Dadabhai Naoroji
(b) Henry Cotton
(c) Badruddin Tyabji
(d) A.O. Hume
The correct option is (d).
• The Indian National Congress (INC) was
founded in December 1885.
• The initiative came from Allan Octavian Hume
(A.O. Hume), a retired British civil servant.
• He envisioned an all-India platform for
educated Indians to voice grievances
constitutionally.
• A.O. Hume is universally recognized as the
founder and first General Secretary.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q5. The All India Muslim League was formed in 1906
at
(a) Aligarh
(b) Dhaka
(c) Delhi
(d) Karachi
The correct option is (b).
• The All-India Muslim League was founded on
December 30, 1906.
• The meeting was held at Dhaka, hosted by
Nawab Sir Salimullah Khan.
• It was formed by Muslim nobles and
landlords to promote loyalty to the British
and safeguard Muslim political rights.
• This marked a major step in communal
politics in India.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q6. The objective of the Morley-Minto Reforms was
to
(a) Establish dyarchy in Provinces
(b) Abolish the post-of Secretary of State for India
(c) Extension of Provincial Assemblies
(d) Give more powers to local government
The correct option is (c).
• The Morley-Minto Reforms (Indian Councils
Act, 1909) aimed to placate moderate
nationalists and divide politically.
• A key feature was the extension of Provincial
Assemblies, increasing their size and elected
members.
• Separate electorates for Muslims were also
introduced.
• Dyarchy came later in 1919, and the Secretary
of State post remained.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q7. Who out of the following was not a moderate?
(a) Gopal Krishna Gokhale
(b) Dadabhai Naoroji
(c) Ferozshah Mehta
(d) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
The correct option is (d).
• Gokhale, Naoroji, and Mehta were key
Moderate leaders, advocating constitutional
methods.
• Bal Gangadhar Tilak led the Extremists,
advocating mass agitation, boycott, and
Swaraj through assertive methods.
• His slogan: “Swaraj is my birthright, and I
shall have it!” became iconic.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q8. Which of the following political parties/
organisations were established by Dr. B.R.
Ambedkar?
1. Swaraj Party
2. Samaj Samata Sangh
3. All India Scheduled Castes Federation
4. The Independent Labour Party
Select the correct answer using the codes given
below:
(a) 1, 2 and 4

(b) 1, 3 and 4
(c) 2, 3 and 4
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (c).
• Samaj Samata Sangh (1927) – Founded to
promote social equality. (Statement 2 –
correct)
• All India Scheduled Castes Federation (1942)
– Political platform for Scheduled Castes.
(Statement 3 – correct)
• Independent Labour Party (1936) – To
contest elections and fight for laboring
classes. (Statement 4 – correct)
• Swaraj Party (1923) – Founded by C.R. Das
and Motilal Nehru, not Ambedkar.
(Statement 1 – incorrect)
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q9. In 1921 the Session of the Indian National
Congress was held when its President was in prison
and with some other leader acting as President. Who
was the Congress President in prison?
(a) Mahatma Gandhi
(b) Mohammad Ali
(c) C.R. Das
(d) Abul Kalam Azad
The correct option is (c).
• The 1921 session of the INC was held in
Ahmedabad.
• C.R. Das was elected President but was in
prison for Non-Cooperation activities.
• Hakim Ajmal Khan acted as President during
the session.
• This highlighted the repressive British
measures during the movement.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 1993

•

Q1. The title given by British Government to
Mahatma Gandhi which, he surrendered during the
Non-Cooperation Movement, was
(a) Hind Kesari
(b) Kaiser-e-Hind
(c) Rai Bahadur
(d) Rt. Honorable
The correct option is (b).
• Mahatma Gandhi was awarded the Kaiser-eHind (Emperor of India) gold medal in 1915
by the British government for his
humanitarian work and ambulance services
during the Boer War in South Africa.
• However, as a mark of protest against the
British government's actions, particularly the
Jallianwala Bagh massacre of 1919 and the
injustices of the Khilafat issue, he
surrendered this prestigious title in August
1920.
• This act of returning the medal was a
powerful symbolic gesture that marked the
formal launch of the Non-Cooperation
Movement, signaling his complete
withdrawal of cooperation from the "satanic"
British government.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

•

Q2. Which of the following were the main objectives
of the Khilafat movement?
1. To rouse anti-British feelings among the
Muslims of India
2. To reform the Muslim society
3. To demand separate electorates and preserve
the Khilafat
4. To save the Ottoman empire and preserve the
Khilafat
Choose the correct answer from the codes given
below:
(a) 1 and 2
(b) 2 and 3
(c) 3 and 4
(d) 1 and 4
The correct option is (d).

•

•

•

The Khilafat Movement (1919-1924) was a
pan-Islamic political campaign in British India.
Objective 4 is correct: Its primary and most
central goal was to pressure the British
government to save the Ottoman Empire and
preserve the institution of the Khilafat. After
Turkey's defeat in World War I, Indian
Muslims feared that the Caliph (Khalifa), the
Sultan of Turkey and the spiritual head of the
global Muslim community, would be deposed
and his empire dismembered.
Objective 1 is correct: As the British were the
main power dictating the harsh peace terms
on Turkey, the movement naturally aimed to
rouse anti-British feelings among the
Muslims of India and mobilize them against
the colonial government.
The movement was not focused on internal
social reform (Objective 2) or the demand for
separate electorates (Objective 3), which was
a separate political issue.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q3. Which one of the following statements does
correctly define the term ‘drain theory’ as
propounded by Dadabhai Naoroji?
(a) That the resources of the country were being
utilised in the interest of Britain
(b) That a part of India’s national wealth or total
annual product was being exported to Britain for
which India got no material return
(c) That the British Industrialists were being given an
opportunity to invest in India under the protection of
the imperial power
(d) That the British goods were being imported to the

country making it poorer day by day
The correct option is (b).
• Dadabhai Naoroji's "Drain Theory" was a
specific and powerful economic critique of
British rule.
• The theory's core argument, as defined by
Naoroji, was that a significant part of India's
national wealth or total annual product was
being exported to Britain for which India got
no material return.
• This "unrequited export" or "drain" consisted
of payments for Britain's administrative and
military costs in India, profits sent home by
British officials and businessmen, and
interest on loans. Unlike normal trade where
imports balance exports, this was a one-way
flow of resources that impoverished India
while enriching Britain. The other options
describe aspects of colonial exploitation but
do not define the specific mechanism of the
"drain."
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q4. The first political organisation established in
India in 1838 was known as
(a) British India Society
(b) Bengal British India Society
(c) Settlers Association
(d) Zamindary Association
The correct option is (d).
• The Zamindary Association, more popularly
known as the Landholders' Society, is widely
regarded as the first organized political
association in British India.
• It was established in Calcutta in 1838.
• As its name suggests, its primary objective
was to protect the interests of the landlords
(Zamindars) of Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa.
Although its focus was narrow and classbased, it marked the beginning of organized
political activity in India, using constitutional
methods like petitions and lobbying to
influence government policy.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q5. “Indian cotton merchant; banker; Congressman
and a close associate of Mahatma Gandhi.” This
description fits
(a) G.D. Birla
(b) M.R. Jayakar
(c) Jamnalal Bajaj
(d) V.S. Srinivasa Sastri
The correct option is (a).
• This description perfectly fits Ghanshyam Das
Birla (G.D. Birla).
• He was a pioneering Indian industrialist who
built a vast business empire, starting from
cotton and jute trading, and was a prominent
Indian cotton merchant and banker.
• He was also deeply involved in the nationalist
movement as a Congressman and was a very
close associate and financial supporter of
Mahatma Gandhi. Despite sometimes
disagreeing with Gandhi's methods, he
remained a lifelong confidant and supporter,
and Gandhi was staying at Birla House in
Delhi when he was assassinated.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q6. Mahatma Gandhi was referred to as the ‘Father
of the nation’ first by
(a) Jawaharlal Nehru
(b) Vallabhbhai Patel
(c) C. Rajagopalachari
(d) Subhash Chandra Bose
The correct option is (d).
• The title "Father of the Nation" for Mahatma
Gandhi was first used by Subhash Chandra
Bose.

•

•

•

On July 6, 1944, in a broadcast on Azad Hind
Radio from Singapore, Netaji Subhash
Chandra Bose addressed Mahatma Gandhi
and sought his blessings for the "holy war" of
India's liberation. In this address, he referred
to Gandhi as the "Father of our Nation."
This title was later officially recognized by the
Indian government, but its first prominent
use is credited to Netaji.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q7. What is the correct chronological sequence of
the following stages in the political life of Mahatama
Gandhi?
1. Champaran
2. Ahmedabad Mill Strike
3. Kheda
4. Non-cooperation Movement
Choose the correct option from the codes given
below:
(a) 2, 4, 3, 1
(b) 1, 2, 3, 4
(c) 4, 3, 2, 1
(d) 3, 4, 2, 1
The correct option is (b).
• To arrange these early movements led by
Mahatma Gandhi in India in chronological
order:
• 1. Champaran Satyagraha: This was Gandhi's
first major satyagraha in India, launched in
1917 to address the grievances of indigo
farmers in Bihar.
• 2. Ahmedabad Mill Strike: Gandhi intervened
in a dispute between textile mill owners and
workers in Ahmedabad in February-March
1918. It was during this struggle that he
undertook his first hunger strike in India.
• 3. Kheda Satyagraha: Immediately following
the Ahmedabad strike, Gandhi led the
peasants of Kheda district in Gujarat in a notax campaign in March 1918 due to crop
failure.
• 4. Non-cooperation Movement: This was a
much larger, nationwide mass movement
launched formally in 1920.

•
•

Therefore, the correct sequence is 1, 2, 3, 4.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q8. The Ryotwari Settlement was introduced by the
British in the
(a) Bengal Presidency
(b) Madras Presidency
(c) Bombay Presidency
(d) Madras and Bombay Presidencies
The correct option is (d).
• The Ryotwari Settlement was one of the
three main land revenue systems introduced
by the British in India.
• Under this system, the government collected
revenue directly from the individual
cultivators (ryots), who were recognized as
the proprietors of the land they cultivated.
• This system was primarily introduced in large
parts of the Madras and Bombay
Presidencies. It was first introduced in the
Baramahal district of Madras by Alexander
Reed and Thomas Munro, and later extended
to the Bombay Presidency under the
supervision of Mountstuart Elphinstone.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q9. The foundation of modern educational system in
India was laid by
(a) The Charter Act of 1813
(b) Macaulay’s Minutes of 1835
(c) The Hunter Commission of 1882

(d) Wood’s Despatch of 1854
The correct option is (b).
• While the Charter Act of 1813 first allocated
funds for education, the definitive policy that
laid the foundation for the modern
educational system in India was based on
Macaulay's Minute on Education (1835).
• Thomas Babington Macaulay, as the Law
Member of the Governor-General's Council,
famously argued in favor of promoting
Western sciences and literature through the
medium of the English language.
• His minute was accepted by GovernorGeneral Lord William Bentinck, and it
established the policy of creating a class of
English-educated Indians to serve the
colonial administration. This decision
fundamentally shaped the direction of higher
education in India for the next century.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q10. Uplift of the backward classes was the main
programme of the
(a) Prathana Samaj
(b) Satyashodhak Samaj
(c) Arya Samaj
(d) Ramakrishna Mission
The correct option is (b).
• The Satyashodhak Samaj (Truth-Seekers'
Society) was founded by Jyotiba Phule in
Maharashtra in 1873.
• The primary and central programme of this
society was the social upliftment of the
backward classes, particularly the Shudras
and Ati-Shudras (Dalits).
• Phule launched a powerful critique of the
Brahmanical caste system, which he saw as
the root cause of oppression. The Samaj
worked to liberate the lower castes from the
hegemony of the Brahmins by promoting
education, social equality, and rational
thought, and by challenging religious rituals
and priestly dominance.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 1994
Q1. Who among the following was the leader of a
number of anti-British revolts in Sambalpur?
(a) Utirat Singh
(b) Surendra Sai
(c) Kattabomman
(d) Sayyid Ahmad Barelvi
The correct option is (b).
• Surendra Sai was a prominent freedom
fighter from Sambalpur in Odisha who led a
prolonged and powerful rebellion against
British rule.
• He belonged to the ruling Chauhan dynasty of
Sambalpur and began his resistance in the
1830s when the British refused to recognize
his claim to the throne.
• His struggle intensified significantly during the
Revolt of 1857, when he escaped from
Hazaribagh jail and returned to Sambalpur to
lead a widespread guerrilla war against the
British.
• He mobilized the tribal communities and local
zamindars, continuing his fight for many years
even after the 1857 revolt was suppressed
elsewhere, making him a legendary figure of
resistance in the region.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

•

Q2. Those who joined Mahatma Gandhi during the
Champaran struggle included
(a) Vallabhbhai Patel and Vinobha Bhave
(b) Jawaharlal Nehru and Rajendra Prasad
(c) Rajendra Prasad and Anugraha Narayan Sinha
(d) Mahadev Desai and Maniben Patel
The correct option is (c).
• The Champaran Satyagraha of 1917 was
Mahatma Gandhi's first major satyagraha in
India. He went to Champaran, Bihar, at the
request of a local peasant, Rajkumar Shukla,
to investigate the grievances of indigo
cultivators.
• During this struggle, Gandhi was joined by a
team of eminent and dedicated lawyers from
Bihar who assisted him in recording the
statements of thousands of peasants.
• The most prominent among these associates
were Dr. Rajendra Prasad (who would later
become the first President of India) and
Anugraha Narayan Sinha, another leading
nationalist from Bihar. Other key figures who
joined him included Brajkishore Prasad,
Acharya Kripalani, and Mazhar-ul-Haq.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.

Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q4. The first Indian ruler who joined the Subsidiary
Alliance was
(a) the Nawab of Oudh
(b) the Nizam of Hyderabad
(c) Peshwa Baji Rao II
(d) the King of Travancore
The correct option is (b).
• The Subsidiary Alliance was a system devised
by the British, most famously used by Lord
Wellesley, to bring Indian princely states
under their control without outright
annexation.
• The first Indian ruler to accept a formal
Subsidiary Alliance was the Nizam of
Hyderabad.
• He signed the treaty in 1798. Under the terms
of the alliance, the Nizam had to disband his
own army, accept a contingent of British
troops stationed in his territory, and pay for
their maintenance. This treaty marked the
beginning of the expansion of British
paramountcy through this diplomatic tool.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q3. The capital of the kingdom of Maharaja Ranjit
Singh was
(a) Amritsar
(b) Patiala
(c) Lahore
(d) Kapurthala
The correct option is (c).
• Maharaja Ranjit Singh was the founder of the
Sikh Empire, which ruled the Punjab region in
the early 19th century.
• After consolidating his power and uniting the
various Sikh misls (confederacies), he
captured the city of Lahore from the Bhangi
Misl in 1799.
• He established Lahore as the political capital
of his vast empire in 1802. While Amritsar
was the spiritual and cultural center of the
Sikhs, Lahore served as the administrative
and military headquarters of his kingdom.

Q5. Consider the map given below: The map shows
the sea voyage route followed by
(a) Thomas Cook
(b) Ferdinand Magellan
(c) John Cabot
(d) Vasco da Gama
The correct option is (b).
• The map depicts a historic sea voyage that
was the first to circumnavigate the globe.
• This expedition was led by the Portuguese
explorer Ferdinand Magellan, who sailed
under the Spanish flag.
• The voyage began in 1519, sailing west from
Spain, across the Atlantic, around the
southern tip of South America (through the
strait that now bears his name), across the
vast Pacific Ocean, and reaching the
Philippines, where Magellan was killed. One
of his ships, the Victoria, under the command

•

of Juan Sebastián Elcano, continued the
journey westward, completing the first-ever
circumnavigation of the Earth in 1522. The
route shown on the map is characteristic of
this epic voyage.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q6. Which one of the following upheavals took place
in Bengal immediately after the Revolt of 1857?
(a) Sanyasi Rebellion
(b) Santhal Rebellion
(c) Indigo Disturbances
(d) Pabna Disturbances
The correct option is (c).
• The event that took place in Bengal
immediately following the Revolt of 1857 was
the Indigo Disturbances or the Indigo Revolt.
• This major peasant uprising began in 1859. It
was a revolt by the ryots (peasants) of Bengal
against the European indigo planters who
forced them to grow indigo under an
oppressive system that was highly
unprofitable for the cultivators.
• The peasants refused to grow indigo, resisted
the planters' coercion, and organized social
boycotts. The revolt gained significant
support from the Bengali intelligentsia and
press, and it ultimately led to the collapse of
the indigo plantation system in Bengal. The
Sanyasi and Santhal rebellions occurred
before 1857, and the Pabna disturbances
occurred later in the 1870s.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q7. The significance of the Bengal Regulation of 1793
lies in the fact that
(a) it provided for the establishment of the Supreme
Court
(b) it restricted the application of English law to
Englishmen only
(c) it accommodated the personal laws of Hindus and
Muslims
(d) it provided for the appointment of the Indian Law
Commission
The correct option is (c).

•

•

•

•

The Bengal Regulation of 1793, also known as
the Cornwallis Code, was a comprehensive
set of laws that reformed the civil and
criminal justice system in British India.
A key significance of this code was that it
formally accommodated the personal laws of
Hindus and Muslims within the colonial
judicial framework.
The regulation stipulated that in matters of
inheritance, marriage, caste, and other
religious usages, the courts would apply
Hindu law for Hindus and Muslim law for
Muslims. This principle of governing personal
matters through religious laws became a
foundational aspect of the Indian legal
system.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q8. What is common among Madam Bhikaji Cama,
M. Barkatulla, V.V.S. Iyer and M.N. Roy?
(a) All of them were leading members of the
International Communist Movement
(b) M. Barkatulla was the Prime Minister and the rest
were Ministers in the Provisional Government of Free
India established by a group of revolutionaries at
Kabul
(c) All of them were among the prominent
revolutionaries outside India operating in different
countries during the Freedom Movement
(d) All of them were accused in the case relating to
the throwing of a bomb on Lord Hardinge
The correct option is (c).
• The common thread connecting Madam
Bhikaji Cama, M. Barkatulla, V.V.S. Iyer, and
M.N. Roy is that they were all prominent
Indian revolutionaries who operated from
outside India during the freedom struggle.
• Madam Cama was active in Paris and
Germany, famously unfurling a flag of Indian
independence in Stuttgart.
• M. Barkatulla was a key figure in the Ghadar
Party and the Provisional Government of
India in Kabul.
• V.V.S. Iyer was a revolutionary associate of
V.D. Savarkar at India House in London.

•

•

•

M.N. Roy was a revolutionary who traveled
to the USA, Mexico (where he co-founded the
Mexican Communist Party), and Russia,
seeking international support for India's
independence.
They all worked from foreign soil to organize,
gather support, and agitate for the
overthrow of British rule in India.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q9. Which of the following international events
influenced the course of the national movement in
India before the advent of Mahatma Gandhi?
1. Italian-Abyssinian War, 1898
2. Boxer Movement in China
3. Revolutionary Movement in Ireland
4. Victory of Japan in the Russo-Japanese War
Choose the correct answer from the code given
below:
(a) 1, 2 and 3 only
(b) 1, 2 and 4 only
(c) 3 and 4 only
(d) 1, 2, 3 and 4
The correct option is (d).
• The Indian national movement in the late
19th and early 20th centuries was
significantly influenced by contemporary
international events that challenged the
notion of European invincibility and inspired
nationalist sentiments.
• Italian-Abyssinian War (1895-96): The defeat
of a modern European army (Italy) by an
African nation (Abyssinia/Ethiopia) was a
major morale booster for anti-colonial
movements worldwide, including in India.
(Statement 1 is correct)
• Boxer Movement in China (1899-1901): This
anti-foreign, anti-imperialist uprising in China
resonated with Indian nationalists who were
also struggling against foreign domination.
(Statement 2 is correct)
• Revolutionary Movement in Ireland: The Irish
struggle for Home Rule and independence
from Britain, both through constitutional and
revolutionary means, served as a direct

•

•

•

inspiration for Indian nationalists, including
the founders of the Home Rule League.
(Statement 3 is correct)
Victory of Japan in the Russo-Japanese War
(1904-05): The victory of an Asian power
(Japan) over a major European power
(Russia) shattered the myth of European
racial and military superiority and had a
profound psychological impact, inspiring
great confidence among Indian nationalists.
(Statement 4 is correct)
All these events contributed to the rise of a
more assertive and radical phase of
nationalism in India.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Court
(b) it restricted the application of English law to
Englishmen only
(c) it accommodated the personal laws of Hindus and
Muslims
(d) it provided for the appointment of the Indian Law

Commission
Correct Answer: (c)
• The Bengal Regulation of 1793, also called the
Cornwallis Code, was a comprehensive legal
reform.
• Its key significance was that it accommodated
the personal laws of Hindus and Muslims
within the colonial judicial framework.
• It stated that in matters of inheritance,
marriage, caste, and religious usages, Hindu
law would apply to Hindus and Muslim law to
Muslims.
• This principle of personal laws became a
foundational feature of the Indian legal
system.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q8. What is common among Madam Bhikaji Cama,
M. Barkatulla, V.V.S. Iyer and M.N. Roy?
(a) All of them were leading members of the
International Communist Movement
(b) M. Barkatulla was the Prime Minister and the rest
were Ministers in the Provisional Government of Free
India established by a group of revolutionaries at
Kabul
(c) All of them were among the prominent
revolutionaries outside India operating in different
countries during the Freedom Movement
(d) All of them were accused in the case relating to
the throwing of a bomb on Lord Hardinge
Correct Answer: (c)
• Madam Bhikaji Cama, M. Barkatulla, V.V.S.
Iyer, and M.N. Roy were all prominent
revolutionaries operating outside India
during the freedom movement.
• Madam Cama worked from Paris and
Germany, famously unfurling a flag of Indian
independence in Stuttgart.
• M. Barkatulla was a key figure in the Ghadar
Party and part of the Provisional Government
of India in Kabul.
• V.V.S. Iyer was associated with India House in
London with V.D. Savarkar.

•

•
•

M.N. Roy traveled to the USA, Mexico, and
Russia, co-founding the Mexican Communist
Party and seeking international support.
All four worked to mobilize global support for
India’s independence.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q9. Which of the following international events
influenced the course of the national movement in
India before the advent of Mahatma Gandhi?
1. Italian-Abyssinian War, 1898
2. Boxer Movement in China
3. Revolutionary Movement in Ireland
4. Victory of Japan in the Russo-Japanese War
Choose the correct answer from the code given
below:
(a) 1, 2 and 3 only
(b) 1, 2 and 4 only
(c) 3 and 4 only
(d) 1, 2, 3 and 4
Correct Answer: (d)
• Italian-Abyssinian War (1895–96): The defeat
of Italy by Abyssinia inspired anti-colonial
movements globally, including in India. (1 is
correct)
• Boxer Movement (1899–1901) in China was
anti-imperialist, inspiring Indian nationalists.
(2 is correct)
• Revolutionary Movement in Ireland
motivated Indian leaders through its Home
Rule struggle and revolutionary methods. (3
is correct)
• Victory of Japan in the Russo-Japanese War
(1904–05) shattered the myth of European
racial superiority, inspiring Indian
nationalism. (4 is correct)
• All these events shaped Indian nationalist
thought before Gandhi’s arrival.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1996
Q1. Who among the following was a prominent
leader of the Congress Socialist Party?
(a) M. N. Roy

(b) Ganesh Shankar Vidyarthi
(c) Pattam Thanu Pillai
(d) Acharya Narendra Dev
Correct Answer: (d)
• The Congress Socialist Party (CSP) was
formed in 1934 as a socialist caucus within
the Indian National Congress.
• It aimed to steer the nationalist movement
towards socialist ideals.
• Acharya Narendra Dev, a respected scholar
and Marxist theorist, was a key founder of
the CSP.
• He served as its first President.
• Along with Jayaprakash Narayan (General
Secretary), he provided intellectual and
organizational leadership to the party.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q2. The meeting of Indian and British political
leaders during 1930-32 in London has often been
referred to as the First, Second and Third Round
Table Conferences. It would be incorrect to refer to
them as such because
(a) the Indian National Congress did not take part in
two of them
(b) Indian parties other than the Indian National
Congress participating in the Conference represented
sectional interests and not the whole of India
(c) the British Labour Party had withdrawn from the
Conference, thereby making the proceedings of the
Conference partisan
(d) it was an instance of a Conference held in three
sessions and not that of three separate conferences
Correct Answer: (d)
• The term “First, Second, and Third Round
Table Conferences” can be misleading.

•

•
•

•

•

The British government officially viewed the
entire process as a single, continuous
conference held in three successive sessions.
The single agenda was the future constitution
of India.
The process was not concluded in the first
session, so it continued into the second and
third.
Referring to them as three separate
conferences is technically incorrect from the
British official perspective.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q3. Who among the following suggested the winding
up of the Indian National Congress after India
attained independence?
(a) C. Rajagopalachari
(b) Acharya Kripalani
(c) Mahatma Gandhi
(d) Jayaprakash Narain
Correct Answer: (c)
• Shortly before his assassination in 1948,
Mahatma Gandhi expressed his view that the
Indian National Congress should be
disbanded after achieving independence.
• He drafted a new constitution proposing that
the Congress should dissolve and be reborn as
a Lok Sevak Sangh.
• He wanted members to focus on social work
and rural upliftment instead of political
power.
• This suggestion was not accepted by the
Congress leadership.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q4. Consider the following landmarks in Indian
education:
1. Hindu College, Calcutta
2. University of Calcutta
3. Adam’s Report
4. Wood’s Despatch
The correct chronological order of these landmarks is
(a) 1, 3, 4, 2
(b) 1, 4, 3, 2
(c) 3, 1, 4, 2

(d) 3, 2, 4, 1
Correct Answer: (a)
• Hindu College, Calcutta (1817): Established by
Raja Ram Mohan Roy and David Hare. (1)
• Adam’s Report (1835–38): Commissioned by
William Bentinck to survey indigenous
education. (3)
• Wood’s Despatch (1854): Issued by Sir
Charles Wood, called the “Magna Carta of
English Education in India”. (4)
• University of Calcutta (1857): Established
following Wood’s Despatch, along with
Bombay and Madras Universities. (2)
• Therefore, the correct sequence is 1, 3, 4, 2.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q5. Which one of the following is an important
historical novel written during the latter half of the
nineteenth century?
(a) Rast Goftar
(b) Durgesh Nandini
(c) Maharatha
(d) Nibandhamala
Correct Answer: (b)
• Durgeshnandini is a landmark Bengali
historical romance novel written by Bankim
Chandra Chattopadhyay.
• It was published in 1865, in the latter half of
the nineteenth century.
• Set against Mughal-Pathan conflicts, it was
the first major novel in modern Bengali
literature.
• It influenced literary and nationalist
consciousness.
• Rast Goftar and Mahratta were newspapers,
and Nibandhamala was a collection of essays.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q6. Consider the following statements : The NonCooperation Movement led to the
1. Congress becoming a mass movement for the
first time.
2. growth of Hindu-Muslim unity.
3. removal of fear of the British ‘might’ from the
minds of the people.

4. British government’s willingness to grant
political concessions to Indians.
Of these statements :
(a) 1, 2, 3 and 4 are correct
(b) 1, 2 and 3 are correct
(c) 1 and 3 are correct
(d) 3 and 4 are correct
The correct option is (b).
• Statement 1 is correct: The Non-Cooperation
Movement (1920–22) was the first instance
where the Indian National Congress was
transformed from an elite organization into a
genuine mass movement, with participation
from peasants, workers, students, and
women across the country.
• Statement 2 is correct: The movement was
launched in conjunction with the Khilafat
Movement, which led to an unprecedented
period of Hindu–Muslim unity in the fight
against the British.
• Statement 3 is correct: The widespread
defiance of British laws and the willingness of
thousands to court arrest and face repression
helped to significantly reduce the fear of
British 'might' from the minds of the common
people, empowering them psychologically.
• Statement 4 is incorrect: The movement did
not immediately lead to the British
government's willingness to grant political
concessions. In fact, it was met with severe
repression, and no major concessions were
offered until much later.
Therefore, statements 1, 2, and 3 are correct.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

2. Formation of the All-India State Peoples
Conference
3. Foundation of Muslim League at Dacca
4. First session of National Conference at
Calcutta
Codes :
ABCD
(a) 4 3 1 2
(b) 3 4 1 2
(c) 4 3 2 1
(d) 3 4 2 1
The correct option is (c).
• A. 1883: The first session of the Indian
National Conference was held in Calcutta in
December 1883. It was convened by
Surendranath Bannerjee and was a precursor
to the Indian National Congress. (A-4)
• B. 1906: The All-India Muslim League was
founded at Dacca (now Dhaka) on December
30, 1906, to protect the political interests of
Muslims in British India. (B-3)
• C. 1927: The All-India State Peoples'
Conference was formed in 1927 to coordinate
political activities and demand democratic
rights for the people living in the princely
states. (C-2)
• D. 1932: The Communal Award was
announced by the British Prime Minister,
Ramsay MacDonald, in August 1932, granting
separate electorates to various minority
communities, including the Depressed
Classes. (D-1)
The correct matching is A-4, B-3, C-2, D-1.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q8. Match List I with List II and select the correct

• A. Abhinav Bharat Society: This secret society
of revolutionaries was founded in 1904 by V.

• B. Anushilan Samiti: This was a prominent

Q9. Which one of the following is not correct about
the Cabinet Mission Plan?
(a) Provincial grouping
(b) Interim Cabinet of Indians
(c) Acceptance of Pakistan
(d) Constitution framing right
The correct option is (c).
• The Cabinet Mission Plan of 1946 had several
key proposals, but the explicit acceptance of
Pakistan was not one of them.
• In fact, the Mission explicitly rejected the
demand for a sovereign, independent
Pakistan.
• Instead, it proposed a three-tiered federal
structure with a weak central government
and strong provinces.

•

It did propose provincial grouping, the
formation of an Interim Cabinet of Indians,
and granting Indians the right to frame their
own constitution through a Constituent
Assembly.
• The rejection of a sovereign Pakistan was a
key reason why the plan ultimately failed to
secure a long-term agreement.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q10. Match List I with List II and select the correct
answer by using the codes given below the lists:
List-I
A. Surendranath Bannerjee
B. M. K. Gandhi
C. Subhash Chandra Bose
D. Lajpat Rai

The correct option is (c).
• A. Surendranath Bannerjee: A pioneer
nationalist leader, he wrote his important
autobiography titled A Nation in the Making.
(A-4)
• B. M. K. Gandhi: He authored the seminal
work Hind Swaraj (or Indian Home Rule) in
1909, which outlines his fundamental
philosophical and political ideas. (B-1)
• C. Subhash Chandra Bose: He wrote The

Q11. Consider the following statements about
Jawaharlal Nehru:
1. He was the President of the Congress Party in
1947
2. He presided over the Constituent Assembly.
3. He formed the first Congress Ministry in
United Province before India’s independence.
Of these statements
(a) 1, 2 and 3 are correct
(b) 1 and 3 are correct
(c) 1 and 2 are correct
(d) None is correct
The correct option is (d).
• Statement 1 is incorrect: The President of the
Indian National Congress during the transfer
of power in 1947 was Acharya J.B. Kripalani,
not Jawaharlal Nehru.
• Statement 2 is incorrect: The President of the
Constituent Assembly was Dr. Rajendra
Prasad. Jawaharlal Nehru was the chairman
of key committees like the Union Powers
Committee, but he did not preside over the
Assembly itself.
• Statement 3 is incorrect: The first Congress
Ministry in the United Provinces (now Uttar
Pradesh) after the 1937 elections was formed
under the leadership of Govind Ballabh Pant,
who became the Premier.
Since all three statements are incorrect, the correct
option is (d).
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q12. The Sarabandi (no tax) campaign of 1922 was
led by
(a) Bhagat Singh
(b) Chittaranjan Das
(c) Rajaguru
(d) Vallabhbhai Patel
The correct option is (d).
• The Sarabandi (no-tax) campaign mentioned
here refers to the Bardoli Satyagraha.

•

While the groundwork for peasant
organization in Bardoli was laid from 1922
onwards, the major no-tax campaign itself
took place in 1928.
• This powerful peasant movement was
launched in the Bardoli taluka of Gujarat to
protest against an arbitrary and unjust 22%
hike in land revenue by the Bombay
government.
• The campaign was masterfully organized and
led by Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel.
• His leadership was so effective that the
peasants remained united and defiant
despite severe government repression,
ultimately forcing the government to
concede.
• It was the women of Bardoli who gave him
the title “Sardar” for his leadership during this
struggle.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q13. Which one of the following first mooted the
idea of a constituent assembly to frame a
constitution for India?
(a) Swaraj Party in 1934
(b) Congress Party in 1936
(c) Muslim League in 1942
(d) All Parties Conference in 1946
The correct option is (a).
• The idea of a Constituent Assembly elected
by the people to frame a constitution for
India was first formally put forward by the
Swaraj Party.
• In a conference held in Ranchi in May 1934,
the Swaraj Party, which was then functioning

as the parliamentary wing of the Congress,
passed a resolution demanding that a
Constituent Assembly be convened to draft a
constitution for a free India.
• This demand was soon adopted by the Indian
National Congress as its official policy, but the
Swaraj Party was the first political party to
articulate it.
• The idea itself had been floated earlier by
individuals like M.N. Roy.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q14. Assertion (A): The British sovereignty continued
to exist in free India.
Reason (R): The British sovereign appointed the last
Governor-General of free India.
In the context of the above two statements, which
one of the following is correct?
(a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true but R is not a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true but R is false
(d) A is false but R is true
The correct option is (d).
• Assertion (A) is false: With the passing of the
Indian Independence Act of 1947, British rule
ended, and India became a fully independent
and sovereign nation on August 15, 1947.
• British sovereignty over India ceased to exist.
India became a dominion, but this was a
transitional status implying self-governance,
not continued British sovereignty.
• Reason (R) is true: Lord Mountbatten, the
last Viceroy, was indeed appointed as the
first Governor-General of the Dominion of
India.
• This appointment was formally made by the
British sovereign, King George VI, but it was
done on the advice of the Indian leadership,
specifically Jawaharlal Nehru.
• The Governor-General acted as a
constitutional head of state, not as an agent
of British sovereignty.

Therefore, the assertion is false, but the reason is
true.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q15. His ‘principal forte was social and religious
reform. He relied upon legislation to do away with
social ills and worked unceasingly for the eradication
of child marriage, the purdah system…….To
encourage consideration of social problems on a
national scale, he inaugurated the Indian National
Social Conference, which for many years met for its
annual sessions alongside the Indian National
Congress.’ The reference in this passage is to
(a) Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar
(b) Behramji Merwanji Malabari
(c) Mahadev Govind Ranade
(d) B. R. Ambedkar
The correct option is (c).
• This passage accurately describes the work
and contributions of Mahadev Govind
Ranade.
• Ranade was a distinguished judge, scholar,
and a key figure in the social reform
movement in Western India.
• His “principal forte” was indeed social and
religious reform.
• He was a firm believer in using constitutional
methods and legislation to address social
evils.
• Most significantly, he was the founder and
guiding spirit of the Indian National Social
Conference, which he established in 1887.
• This organization held its annual sessions at
the same venue as the Indian National
Congress, emphasizing the idea that social
reform was an integral part of the national
agenda.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q16. Match List I with List II and select the correct
answer by using the codes given below the lists:
List-I
A. Governor General of Presidency of Fort William in
Bengal (under Regulating Act, 1773)
B. Governor General of India (under Charter Act,

1833)
C. Governor General and Viceroy of India (Under
Indian Councils Act, 1858)
D. Governor General and Crown Representatives
(Under Government of India Act, 1935)
List-II
1. Archibald Percival Wavell, Viscount and Earl
Wavell
2. James Andrew Broun-Ramsay, Earl and
Marquess of Dalhousie
3. Charles Cornwallis 2nd, Earl and First
Marquess of Cornwallis
4. Gilbert John Elliot-Murray-Kynynmound, Earl

5. Louis Mountbatten of Burma
Codes:
ABCD

• B. Governor General of India (under Charter

Q17. Who among the following leaders did not
believe in the drain theory of Dadabhai Naoroji?
(a) B.G. Tilak
(b) R.C. Dutt
(c) M.G. Ranade
(d) Sir Syed Ahmed Khan
The correct option is (d).
• The Drain Theory, which argued that Britain
was systematically draining wealth from
India, was a central tenet of the economic
critique of colonialism put forward by early
Indian nationalists.
• Leaders like B.G. Tilak, R.C. Dutt (who wrote
The Economic History of India), and M.G.
Ranade all accepted and further elaborated
on this theory.
• It became a powerful tool for mobilizing
nationalist sentiment.
• Sir Syed Ahmed Khan, however, took a
different political path. He was a proponent
of Anglo-Muslim cooperation and believed
that the interests of the Muslim community
were best served by remaining loyal to the
British government.
• He did not subscribe to the nationalist
economic critique and therefore did not
believe in the drain theory.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q18. The Anarchical and Revolutionary Crime Act
(1919) was popularly known as the
(a) Rowlatt Act
(b) Pitt’s India Act
(c) Indian Arms Act
(d) Ilbert Bill
The correct option is (a).
• The Anarchical and Revolutionary Crimes Act
of 1919 is the official name of the legislation
that came to be popularly and notoriously
known as the Rowlatt Act.
• The act was based on the recommendations
of the Sedition Committee chaired by Sir
Sidney Rowlatt.
• It gave the British government sweeping
powers to suppress political dissent, including
the power to arrest and imprison any person
on mere suspicion without a trial.
• It was this repressive nature that earned it
the name "Black Act" and led to the
nationwide Satyagraha led by Mahatma
Gandhi.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q19. Who among the following was the first
European to initiate the policy of taking part in the
quarrels of Indian princes with a view to acquire
territories?
(a) Clive
(b) Dupleix
(c) Albuquerque
(d) Warren Hastings
The correct option is (b).
• The policy of European powers intervening in
the internal conflicts of Indian princely states
to further their territorial and commercial
ambitions was first masterfully initiated by
the French Governor-General, Joseph
François Dupleix.
• During the Carnatic Wars in the 1740s and
1750s, Dupleix perfected the strategy of
supporting rival claimants to Indian thrones
with French troops and resources.
• In return for this military support, he would
secure valuable territories, trading rights,

and political influence for the French East
India Company.
• Robert Clive of the British East India
Company later adopted and perfected this
very policy to outmaneuver the French and
establish British dominance.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1997
Q1. Who among the following was associated with
the suppression of Thugs?
(a) General Henry Prendergast
(b) Captain Sleeman
(c) Alexander Burnes
(d) Captain Robert Pemberton
The correct option is (b).
• The "Thugs" (or Thuggee) were organized
gangs of professional robbers and murderers
who terrorized travelers in India for centuries.
• They would typically befriend and then
ritually strangle and rob their victims.
• In the 1830s, the British administration under
Governor-General Lord William Bentinck
launched a major campaign to eradicate this
menace.
• Captain William Henry Sleeman was the
British official who led this highly successful
operation.
• He developed a systematic approach that
involved intelligence gathering, using
informers (approvers), and mass arrests.
• His relentless efforts effectively dismantled
the Thuggee network and brought an end to
their reign of terror, making him famous for
the suppression of Thugs.
Hence option b is the correct answer.
Q2. Match List I with List II and select the correct
answer by using the codes given below the lists:
List-I
A. Butler Committee Report
B. Hartog Committee Report
C. Hunter Inquiry Committee Report
D. Muddiman Committee Report

List-II
1. Jallianwala Bagh Massacre
2. Relationship between the Indian States and
Paramount Power
3. Working of Dyarchy as laid down in the
Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms
4. The growth of education in British India &
potentialities of its further progress
Codes:
ABCD
(a) 3 2 1 4
(b) 1 4 2 3
(c) 2 1 3 4
(d) 2 4 1 3
The correct option is (d).
• Butler Committee Report (1927): This
committee was appointed to investigate and
clarify the relationship between the Indian
Princely States and the Paramount Power
(the British Crown). (A-2)
• Hartog Committee Report (1929): Appointed
as an auxiliary committee to the Simon
Commission, its purpose was to survey the
growth of education in British India and
report on its progress. (B-4)
• Hunter Inquiry Committee Report (1920):
This committee, officially the Disorders
Inquiry Committee, was appointed by the
British government to investigate the
disturbances in Punjab, most notably the
Jallianwala Bagh Massacre. (C-1)
• Muddiman Committee Report (1924): This
committee was set up to inquire into the
working of Dyarchy as laid down in the
Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms (the
Government of India Act, 1919) and to
suggest remedies for any defects. (D-3)
• The correct matching is A-2, B-4, C-1, D-3.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

C. The Chauri-Chaura incident
D. The Dandi March
List-II (Results)
1. Country-wise agitation
2. Withdrawal of a movement
3. Communal Electorates
4. Illegal manufacture of salt
5. Partition of India
Codes:
ABCD
(a) 3 4 5 2
(b) 4 1 2 3
(c) 2 3 4 5
(d) 3 1 2 4
The correct option is (d).
• Morley-Minto Reforms (1909): The most
significant and controversial result of these
reforms was the introduction of Communal
Electorates for Muslims, which
institutionalized separate political identities
based on religion. (A-3)
• Simon Commission (1927-28): The arrival of
this all-white commission was met with a
massive boycott and widespread, countrywise agitation, with protests and slogans of
“Simon Go Back” across India. (B-1)
• The Chauri-Chaura incident (1922): This
violent incident, where protestors burned a
police station, led Mahatma Gandhi to order
the immediate withdrawal of the NonCooperation Movement. (C-2)
• The Dandi March (1930): The march
culminated in Mahatma Gandhi and his
followers breaking the British salt law by the
illegal manufacture of salt from seawater,
which launched the Civil Disobedience
Movement. (D-4)
• The correct matching is A-3, B-1, C-2, D-4.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q3. Match List I with List II and select the correct
answer by using the codes given below the lists:
List-I (Events)
A. Morley Minto Reforms
B. Simon Commission

Q4. “A graduate at 18, professor and associated
editor of the Sudharak at 20, Secretary of the
Sarvajanik Sabha and of the Provincial Conference at
25, Secretary of the National Congress at 29, leading
witness before an important Royal Commission at

31, Provincial legislator at 34, Imperial legislator at
36, President of the Indian National Congress at 39 a
patriot whom Mahatma Gandhi himself regarded as
his master.” This is how a biographer describes
(a) Pandit Madan Mohan Malaviya
(b) Mahadev Govind Ranade
(c) Gopal Krishna Gokhale
(d) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
The correct option is (c).
• This detailed and impressive biographical
timeline perfectly describes the life and
career of Gopal Krishna Gokhale.
• Gokhale was a brilliant intellectual and a
dedicated moderate leader who achieved
remarkable political milestones at a very
young age.
• He was a professor, a social reformer, and a
key figure in organizations like the Poona
Sarvajanik Sabha.
• He became the President of the Indian
National Congress in 1905 at the age of 39.
• Most famously, he was a political mentor to
Mahatma Gandhi, who revered him and
called him his “political guru.”
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q5. The Poona Pact which was signed between the
British Government and Mahatma Gandhi in 1932
provided for
(a) the creation of dominion status for India
(b) separate electorates for the Muslims
(c) separate electorate for the Harijans
(d) joint electorate with reservation for Harijans
The correct option is (d).
• The Poona Pact was an agreement reached in
September 1932 to resolve the crisis created
by the British Communal Award, which had
granted separate electorates to the
Depressed Classes (Harijans).
• Mahatma Gandhi undertook a fast unto
death in protest against this, viewing it as a
move to divide Hindu society.
• The pact, signed between Dr. B. R. Ambedkar
(on behalf of the Depressed Classes) and
leaders of the caste Hindus (with Gandhi's

approval), abandoned the idea of separate
electorates.
• Instead, it provided for a system of joint
electorates with an increased number of
reserved seats for the Harijans in the
provincial and central legislatures.
• The pact was not signed with the British
Government, but between Indian leaders,
and was then accepted by the government.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q6. Match List I with List II and select the correct
answer by using the codes given below the lists:
List-I
A. Surat Split
B. Communal Award
C. All-Party Convention
D. Poorna Swaraj Resolution
List-II
1. 1929
2. 1928
3. 1932
4. 1907
5. 1905
Codes:
ABCD
(a) 4 3 1 5
(b) 4 3 2 1
(c) 2 5 4 1
(d) 1 4 2 3
The correct option is (b).
• Surat Split: The historic split in the Indian
National Congress between the Moderates
and the Extremists occurred at its annual
session in Surat in 1907. (A-4)

•

Communal Award: The controversial award
granting separate electorates to minorities,
including the Depressed Classes, was
announced by British Prime Minister Ramsay
MacDonald in August 1932. (B-3)
• All-Party Convention: An All-Parties
Conference was convened in 1928 to draft a
constitution for India in response to the
challenge posed by the Simon Commission.
This conference produced the Nehru Report.
(C-2)
• Poorna Swaraj Resolution: The landmark
resolution demanding complete
independence (Poorna Swaraj) was passed by
the Indian National Congress at its Lahore
session in December 1929. (D-1)
• The correct matching is A-4, B-3, C-2, D-1.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q7. Match List I with List II and select the correct
answer by using the codes given below the lists:
List-I
A. Chittagong Armoury raid
B. Kakori Conspiracy
C. Lahore Conspiracy
D. Ghadr Party
List-II
1. Lala Hardayal
2. Jatin Das
3. Surya Sen
4. Ram Prasad Bismil
5. Vasudeo Phadke
Codes:
ABCD
(a) 3 4 1 5
(b) 4 3 2 5
(c) 3 4 2 1
(d) 2 4 3 1
The correct option is (c).
• Chittagong Armoury raid (1930): This daring
raid on the British armoury in Chittagong was
masterminded and led by the revolutionary
leader Surya Sen, also known as “Master Da.”
(A-3)

•

Kakori Conspiracy (1925): This was a train
robbery organized by the Hindustan
Republican Association to fund their
activities. Ram Prasad Bismil was one of the

• Lahore Conspiracy: This refers to the case
