was its intellectual guide and a principal
founder. (D-1)
• The correct matching is A-3, B-4, C-2, D-1.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q8. What is the correct chronological sequence of
the following?
1. Wood’s Education Despatch
2. Macaulay’s Minute on Education
3. The Sargeant Education Report
4. Indian Education (Hunter Commission)
Codes
(a) 2, 1, 4, 3
(b) 2, 1, 3, 4
(c) 1, 2, 4, 3
(d) 4, 3, 1, 2
The correct option is (a).
• Macaulay’s Minute on Education (1835): This
influential document, which advocated for
English-medium education, was written in
1835.
• Wood’s Education Despatch (1854): This was
a comprehensive plan for education in India,
often called the “Magna Carta of English
Education”.
• Indian Education (Hunter Commission)
(1882): This commission was appointed to
review the progress of education in India
since the Wood's Despatch.
• The Sargeant Education Report (1944): This
was a post-war plan for educational

development in India, prepared by Sir John
Sargent.
• Therefore, the correct chronological sequence
is 2, 1, 4, 3.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q9. Match List I with List II and select the correct
answer using the codes given below the lists:
A. Moplah revolt : 1. Kerala
B. Pabna revolt : 2. Bihar
C. Eka Movement : 3. Bengal
D. Birsa Munda revolt : 4. Awadh
Codes
ABCD
(a) 1 3 4 2
(b) 2 3 4 1
(c) 1 2 3 4
(d) 3 4 1 2
The correct option is (a).
• Moplah revolt (1921): This was a major
peasant uprising by the Mappila (Moplah)
Muslim tenants against Hindu landlords and
the British administration in the Malabar
region of Kerala. (A-1)
• Pabna revolt (1870s): This was an agrarian
movement by peasants in the Pabna district
of Bengal to resist the oppressive demands of
the zamindars. (B-3)
• Eka Movement (1921-22): This was a peasant
unity movement that emerged in the Awadh
region of present-day Uttar Pradesh,
protesting against high rents and landlord
oppression. (C-4)
• Birsa Munda revolt (1899-1900): This was a
major tribal uprising, also known as the
Ulgulan (Great Tumult), led by Birsa Munda
against the encroachment of outsiders and
the destruction of their traditional way of life
in the Chotanagpur region of Bihar (now
Jharkhand). (D-2)
• The correct matching is A-1, B-3, C-4, D-2.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q10. M.C. Setalvad, B.N. Rau and Alladi
Krishnaswamy Iyer were distinguished members of
the
(a) Swaraj Party
(b) All-India National Liberal Federation
(c) Madras Labour Union
(d) Servants of India Society
The correct option is (d).
• M.C. Setalvad (who became India's first
Attorney General), B.N. Rau (who became the
Constitutional Advisor to the Constituent
Assembly), and Alladi Krishnaswamy Iyer (a
key member of the Drafting Committee) were
all eminent jurists and public figures.
• All three were associated with the liberal
tradition of Indian politics and were
distinguished members of the Servants of
India Society.
• This organization, founded by Gopal Krishna
Gokhale, was dedicated to training national
missionaries for the service of India.
• It attracted many of the country's finest
minds who were committed to public service
and constitutional progress.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 1998
Q1. The educated middle class in India
(a) opposed the revolt of 1857
(b) supported the revolt of 1857
(c) remained neutral to the revolt of 1857
(d) fought against native rulers
The correct option is (c).

•

The Revolt of 1857 was primarily led by
traditional elements of Indian society, such as
feudal chiefs, dispossessed zamindars, and
sepoys.
• The newly emergent educated middle class in
India, which had benefited from Western
education, largely remained neutral or aloof
from the revolt.
• This group believed that British rule, despite
its flaws, was a modernizing force that could
help reform Indian society.
• They were suspicious of the feudal leadership
of the revolt and feared that its success would
lead to a return to a backward, traditional
order.
• Consequently, they did not actively support
the uprising.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q2. Lord Mountbatten came to India as Viceroy
along with specific instructions to
(a) balkanize the Indian sub-continent
(b) keep India united if possible
(c) accept Jinnah’s demand for Pakistan
(d) persuade the Congress to accept partition
The correct option is (b).
• When Lord Mountbatten was appointed as
the last Viceroy of India in March 1947, his
primary mandate from the British
government, led by Prime Minister Clement
Attlee, was to oversee a peaceful and swift
transfer of power.
• His specific instructions were to explore all
possibilities to keep India united if possible.
• The Cabinet Mission Plan of 1946, which
proposed a three-tiered federal structure for
a united India, was still the preferred solution
for the British government.
• Only after Mountbatten concluded, through
his negotiations with Indian leaders like
Nehru, Patel, and Jinnah, that a united India
was no longer viable due to the Muslim
League's stance, did he move towards
partition.

•

His initial goal, however, was to preserve
unity.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q3. Assertion (A): Partition of Bengal in 1905 brought
to an end the Moderates’ role in the Indian freedom
movement.
Reason (R): The Surat session of Indian National
Congress separated the Extremists from the
Moderates.
(a) Both A and R are true, and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true, but R is NOT a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true, but R is false
(d) A is false, but R is true
The correct option is (d).
• Assertion (A) is false. The Partition of Bengal
in 1905 did not bring an end to the
Moderates' role.
• While it did lead to the rise of the Extremists
and exposed the limitations of the
Moderates' methods, the Moderates
continued to be a significant force,
controlling the Congress after the Surat Split.
• Reason (R) is true. The Surat session of the
Indian National Congress (1907) did result in
a formal split, separating the Extremists from
the Moderates.
• The clash over political goals and methods of
agitation led to the expulsion of the
Extremists, leaving the Moderates in control
for several years.
• Since the assertion is false but the reason is
true, the correct option is (d).
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q4. Assertion (A): The Khilafat movement did bring
the urban Muslims into the fold of the National
Movement.
Reason (R): There was a predominant element of
anti-imperialism in both the National and Khilafat
Movements.
(a) Both A and R are true, and R is the correct
explanation of A

(b) Both A and R are true, but R is NOT a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true, but R is false
(d) A is false, but R is true
The correct option is (a).
• Assertion (A) is true. The Khilafat Movement
(1919–1924) successfully mobilized urban
Muslims, including artisans, merchants, and
the clergy, into the anti-British national
movement.
• Reason (R) is also true. Both movements had
a predominant anti-imperialist element
directed against British rule.
• This shared anti-British sentiment provided
the common ground for the two movements
to merge.
• The reason correctly explains the assertion
because this common anti-imperialist
platform allowed Gandhi to unite the Khilafat
issue with the Non-Cooperation Movement,
bringing urban Muslims into a joint struggle.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q5. Assertion (A): The first ever Bill to make primary
education compulsory in India was rejected in 1911.
Reason (R): Discontent would have increased if every
cultivator could read.
(a) Both A and R are true, and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true, but R is NOT a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true, but R is false
(d) A is false, but R is true
The correct option is (a).
• Assertion (A) is true. In 1911, Gopal Krishna
Gokhale introduced a private member's bill in
the Imperial Legislative Council to make
primary education free and compulsory.
• The British government rejected this
pioneering bill despite strong support.
• Reason (R) is also true. The colonial
administration feared that if every cultivator
could read, political awareness and
discontent would rise.

•

This fear of mass education leading to dissent
was a key reason behind their opposition.
• The reason correctly explains the assertion.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q6. Assertion (A): The Congress rejected the Cripps
proposals.
Reason (R): The Cripps Mission consisted solely of
whites.
(a) Both A and R are true, and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true, but R is NOT a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true, but R is false
(d) A is false, but R is true
The correct option is (c).
• Assertion (A) is true. The Indian National
Congress rejected the Cripps proposals of
1942 because it found the offer of Dominion
Status after the war inadequate.
• It was especially opposed to the provision
allowing provinces to opt out of the Indian
Union, which was viewed as a blueprint for
partition.
• Reason (R) is false. The Cripps Mission was a
one-man mission led by Sir Stafford Cripps.
• The issue was not its racial composition. The
Simon Commission was boycotted for being
all-white, but Cripps Mission was rejected for
the content of its proposals, not because of
who composed it.
• Since the assertion is true but the reason is
false, the correct option is (c).
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q7. Assertion (A): Gandhi stopped the NonCooperation Movement in 1922.
Reason (R): Violence at Chauri-Chaura led him to
stop the movement.
(a) Both A and R are true, and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true, but R is NOT a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true, but R is false
(d) A is false, but R is true

The correct option is (a).
• Assertion (A) is true. Mahatma Gandhi
abruptly called off the Non-Cooperation
Movement in February 1922.
• Reason (R) is also true. The immediate cause
was the violence at Chauri-Chaura in Uttar
Pradesh, where a mob burned a police
station, killing 22 policemen.
• The reason correctly explains the assertion
because the movement was founded on nonviolence (Ahimsa).
• Gandhi believed that the people were not
ready for a disciplined non-violent struggle, so
he suspended the movement to prevent
further violence.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q8. When the Indian Muslim League was inducted
into the interim government in 1946, Liyaqat Ali
Khan was assigned the portfolio of
(a) Foreign affairs
(b) Home
(c) Finance
(d) Defence
The correct option is (c).
• The Interim Government was formed in
September 1946, initially with only Congress
members.
• The Muslim League joined in October 1946.
• Liaquat Ali Khan, its senior leader, was
assigned the Finance portfolio.
• The League used its control over Finance to
obstruct the functioning of other
departments headed by Congress ministers.
• This deadlock convinced Congress leaders
that cooperation with the League in a united
India was impossible.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q9. The Indian National Congress agreed in 1947 to
the partition of the country mainly because
(a) the principle of two-Nation theory was then
acceptable to them
(b) it was imposed by the British Government and the
Congress was helpless in this regard

(c) they wanted to avoid large-scale communal riots
(d) India would have otherwise lost the opportunity to
attain freedom
The correct option is (c).
• The Indian National Congress had long
championed a united India and opposed the
Two-Nation Theory.
• By 1947, however, communal violence had
escalated after the Muslim League’s call for
Direct Action in 1946.
• The Great Calcutta Killing and subsequent
riots made civil war seem imminent.
• Faced with this grim reality, the Congress
leadership reluctantly accepted partition as
the only way to avoid larger communal
bloodshed.
• It was seen as a tragic but necessary step for
a peaceful transfer of power.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q10. At the time of India’s Independence, Mahatma
Gandhi was
(a) a member of Congress Working Committee
(b) not a member of the Congress
(c) the President of the Congress
(d) the General Secretary of the Congress
The correct option is (b).
• Although Mahatma Gandhi was the moral
and spiritual leader of the freedom
movement, he held no official position within
the Congress leading up to independence.
• He resigned his primary membership in 1934,
choosing to guide the party from outside its
formal structure.
• Therefore, at the time of India's
independence in 1947, he was not a member
of the Congress nor held any office.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q11. What is the correct sequence of the following
events?
1. Tilak’s Home Rule League
2. Kamagatamaru Incident
3. Mahatma Gandhi’s arrival in India

Select the correct answer using the codes given
below:
(a) 1, 2, 3
(b) 3, 2, 1
(c) 2, 1, 3
(d) 2, 3, 1
The correct option is (d).
• Komagata Maru Incident (1914): A Japanese
ship carrying Indian immigrants was denied
entry into Canada, leading to resentment
against the British, especially in Punjab. (2)
• Mahatma Gandhi’s arrival in India (1915):
Gandhi returned permanently from South
Africa on January 9, 1915. (3)
• Tilak’s Home Rule League (1916): Bal
Gangadhar Tilak founded the Indian Home
Rule League in April 1916 to demand selfgovernment. (1)
• Therefore, the correct chronological
sequence is 2, 3, 1.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q12. Simon Commission of 1927 was boycotted
because
(a) there was no Indian Member in the Commission
(b) it supported the Muslim League
(c) Congress felt that the people of India are entitled
to Swaraj
(d) there were differences among the members
The correct option is (a).
• The Simon Commission (1927) was appointed
to review the Government of India Act, 1919
and suggest reforms.
• It was boycotted by almost all major parties,
including the Congress and a faction of the
Muslim League.
• The main reason: it had no Indian member—
it was an all-white commission.
• Indians saw this as a grave insult to their selfrespect and rejected the idea that foreigners
could decide their future.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q13. The Indian Muslims, in general, were not
attracted to the Extremist movement because of the

(a) influence of Sir Sayyid Ahmed Khan
(b) anti-Muslim attitude of extremist leaders
(c) indifference shown to Muslim aspirations
(d) Extremists’ policy of harping on Hindu past
The correct option is (d).
• The Extremist phase (1905–1915) of
nationalism failed to attract many Muslims.
• The Extremists, led by figures like Bal
Gangadhar Tilak, often used Hindu symbols,
festivals (e.g., Ganapati and Shivaji) to
mobilize people.
• This Hindu revivalist tone alienated Muslims,
who felt their religious and cultural heritage
was excluded.
• This created a sense of unease and distance
among many Muslims toward the movement.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q14. Which one of the following events was
characterised by Montague as ‘Preventive Murder’?
(a) Killing of INA activists
(b) Massacre of Jallianwalla Bagh
(c) Shooting of the Mahatma
(d) Shooting of Curzon-Wyllie
The correct option is (b).
• The Jallianwala Bagh Massacre (April 13,
1919) saw British troops under General Dyer
firing on an unarmed crowd, killing hundreds.
• Edwin Montagu, the Secretary of State for
India, condemned Dyer’s actions.
• In a debate in the British House of Commons,
he called Dyer’s justification for the massacre
— to have a “moral effect” — a doctrine of
“preventive murder.”
• The massacre provoked widespread outrage
in India and Britain.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q15. What is the correct sequence of the following
events?
1. The August Offer
2. The I.N.A. trial
3. The Quit India Movement
4. The Royal Indian Naval Ratings’ Revolt

Select the correct answer using the codes given
below:
(a) 1, 3, 2, 4
(b) 3, 1, 2, 4
(c) 1, 3, 4, 2
(d) 3, 1, 4, 2
The correct option is (a).
• August Offer (1940): Proposals by Lord
Linlithgow to secure Indian cooperation in
WWII; rejected by Congress. (1)
• Quit India Movement (1942): A mass struggle
launched on August 9, calling for immediate
British withdrawal. (3)
• INA Trials (1945): Trials of captured officers
of the Indian National Army at the Red Fort,
starting in November 1945. (2)
• Royal Indian Naval Ratings’ Revolt (1946): A
major mutiny in Bombay, February 1946. (4)
• The chronological order is 1, 3, 2, 4.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q16. Which one of the following defines extremist
ideology during the early phase of Indian freedom
movement?
(a) Stimulating the production of indigenous articles
by giving them preference over imported
commodities
(b) Obtaining self-government by aggressive means in
place of petitions and constitutional ways
(c) Providing national education according to the
requirements of the country
(d) Organising coups against the British empire
through military revolt
The correct option is (b).
• The key difference between Moderates and
Extremists was their methods and goals.
• Extremists rejected the “policy of petitions
and prayers” followed by Moderates.
• They aimed to achieve self-government
(Swaraj) through aggressive methods, such as
mass agitation, boycott of British goods and
institutions, and passive resistance.
• They supported Swadeshi and national
education, but their defining feature was

their assertive, confrontational approach to
self-rule.
• They did not advocate military coups, but
focused on mass mobilization and direct
action.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q17. Which of the following pairs are correctly
matched?
1. Theodore Beck : Mohammadan AngloOriental College, Aligarh
2. Ilbert Bill : Ripon
3. Pherozeshah Mehta : Indian National
Congress
4. Badruddin Tyabji : Muslim League
Select the correct answer using the codes given
below:
(a) 1, 2, 3 and 4
(b) 2 and 4
(c) 1, 3 and 4
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (d).
• Pair 1 is correct: Theodore Beck was the first
principal of the MAO College, Aligarh, and
was close to Sir Syed Ahmed Khan.
• Pair 2 is correct: The Ilbert Bill (1883) was
introduced under Lord Ripon, aiming to end
racial discrimination in the judiciary.
• Pair 3 is correct: Pherozeshah Mehta was a
prominent Congress leader, a Moderate, and
Congress President in 1890.
• Pair 4 is incorrect: Badruddin Tyabji was a
Congress leader and President in 1887, not
associated with the Muslim League (founded
in 1906).
• Therefore, 1, 2, and 3 are correctly matched.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q18. “The Congress is tottering to its fall and one of
my great ambitions while in India, is to assist it to a
peaceful demise.” This statement is attributed to
(a) Lord Dufferin
(b) Lord Curzon
(c) Lord Lytton
(d) None of the above

The correct option is (b).
• This famous statement was made by Lord
Curzon, Viceroy of India (1899–1905).
• In a letter (1900), Curzon called Congress a
weak and dying organization and expressed
his ambition to “assist it to a peaceful
demise.”
• Ironically, his Partition of Bengal (1905) had
the opposite effect — it revitalized the
nationalist movement and strengthened
Congress.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q19. Who was the leader of the Ghaddar Party?
(a) Bhagat Singh
(b) Lala Hardayal
(c) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
(d) V. D. Savarkar
The correct option is (b).
• The Ghadar Party was founded in 1913 in the
USA and Canada by Indian immigrants, mainly
Punjabis.
• Its goal was to liberate India through armed
struggle.
• While Sohan Singh Bhakna was the founding
president, the intellectual and ideological
leader was Lala Hardayal.
• He inspired the movement through writings in
the party’s newspaper “The Ghadar”,
becoming its heart and soul.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 1999
Q1. The first venture of Gandhi in all-India politics
was the
(a) Non-Cooperation Movement
(b) Rowlatt Satyagraha
(c) Champaran Movement
(d) Dandi March
The correct option is (b).
• While the Champaran Movement (1917) was
Gandhi's first major satyagraha in India, it was
a regional struggle focused on the grievances
of local peasants.
• The Rowlatt Satyagraha of 1919 was
Mahatma Gandhi's first attempt to launch a
nationwide, or all-India, political agitation.
• He called for a countrywide hartal (strike)
and civil disobedience against the repressive
Rowlatt Act.
• Although the movement was largely confined
to urban areas and was short-lived, it marked
his first venture into leading a struggle on a
national scale, setting the stage for the larger
Non-Cooperation Movement that followed.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q2. The Congress policy of pray and petition
ultimately came to an end under the guidance of
(a) Aurobindo Ghosh
(b) Bal Gangadhar Tilak
(c) Lala Lajpat Rai
(d) Mahatma Gandhi
The correct option is (b).
• The early phase of the Indian National
Congress was dominated by the Moderates,
whose political method was famously
described as the "3 Ps": Pray, Petition, and
Protest. They believed in appealing to the
British sense of justice through constitutional
means.
• This policy was increasingly seen as ineffective
and was strongly challenged by the rise of the
Extremist faction within the Congress.
• Bal Gangadhar Tilak was the foremost leader
of the Extremists. He vehemently criticized

•

the "policy of mendicancy" (begging) of the
Moderates and advocated for a more
assertive and self-reliant approach based on
mass agitation, boycott, and passive
resistance.
Under his guidance and influence, the
Congress began to shift away from its old
methods towards a more confrontational
political strategy.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q3. Match List I with List II and select the correct
answer using the codes given below the Lists:
List-I (Persons)

List-II (Journals)

A. Shyamji Krishna Varma 1. Bande Mataram
B. Madame Bhikaji Cama 2. Indian Sociologist
C. Annie Besant

3. The Talwar

D. Aurobindo Ghosh

4. Commonwealth

Codes:
ABCD
(a) 2 3 4 1
(b) 3 2 1 4
(c) 2 3 1 4
(d) 3 2 4 1
The correct option is (a).
• A. Shyamji Krishna Varma: An Indian
revolutionary active in London, he founded
the India House and started the journal
Indian Sociologist to promote the cause of
Indian independence. (A-2)
• B. Madame Bhikaji Cama: A prominent
revolutionary figure based in Paris, she was
associated with several revolutionary journals.
She helped publish The Talwar (The Sword)
from Berlin, which was a continuation of an
earlier revolutionary paper. (B-3)
• C. Annie Besant: A leader of the Home Rule
Movement, she started two newspapers to
propagate her ideas: the weekly
Commonwealth and the daily New India. (C4)
• D. Aurobindo Ghosh: During his revolutionary
phase in Bengal, he was the editor of the
nationalist daily Bande Mataram, which

•

became a powerful voice of the Extremist
faction. (D-1)
The correct matching is A-2, B-3, C-4, D-1.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q4. ‘Abhinav Bharat’ a secret society of
revolutionaries was organised by
(a) Khudiram Bose
(b) V. D. Savarkar
(c) Prafulla Chaki
(d) Bhagat Singh
The correct option is (b).
• Abhinav Bharat Society (Young India Society)
was a secret revolutionary organization
founded in 1904.
• It was organized by Vinayak Damodar
Savarkar (V. D. Savarkar) and his brother
Ganesh Damodar Savarkar.
• The society grew out of an earlier
organization called Mitra Mela and was
inspired by Giuseppe Mazzini's "Young Italy".
• It had branches in various parts of India and
was involved in several acts of political
assassination against British officials, aiming
to overthrow British rule through armed
rebellion.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q5. The most short-lived of all of Britain’s
constitutional experiments in India was the
(a) Indian Councils Act of 1861
(b) Indian Councils Act of 1892
(c) Indian Councils Act of 1909
(d) Government of India Act of 1919
The correct option is (c).

•

•

•

•

The Indian Councils Act of 1909, also known
as the Morley-Minto Reforms, proved to be
the most short-lived of the major
constitutional experiments.
It was introduced in 1909, but the
constitutional arrangements it established
were quickly found to be inadequate and
failed to satisfy the growing aspirations of
Indian nationalists.
Within just ten years, it was superseded by
the more substantial Government of India Act
of 1919 (Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms),
which introduced a new system of Dyarchy.
The other acts remained in force for much
longer periods before being replaced.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q6. “It made its proposals in May. It still wanted a
united India. There was to be a Federal Union
composed of British provinces.” The above quotation
is related to
(a) Simon Commission
(b) Gandhi-Irwin Pact
(c) Cripps Mission
(d) Cabinet Mission
The correct option is (d).
• This quotation accurately describes the core
proposals of the Cabinet Mission of 1946.
• The Cabinet Mission arrived in India in March
1946, and after failing to secure an agreement
between the Congress and the Muslim
League, announced its own plan in May 1946.

•

•

•

A key feature of the plan was its rejection of a
sovereign Pakistan; it explicitly stated that it
wanted a united India.
To achieve this, it proposed a three-tiered
structure with a Federal Union at the top,
composed of all British provinces and princely
states.
This Union would handle foreign affairs,
defence, and communications.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q7. The Governor-General who followed a spirited
“Forward” policy towards Afghanistan was
(a) Minto
(b) Dufferin
(c) Elgin
(d) Lytton
The correct option is (d).
• The "Forward Policy" was an aggressive
British imperial strategy in the late 19th
century aimed at countering perceived
Russian expansion towards India by
extending British influence and control over
the frontier regions, particularly Afghanistan.
• Lord Lytton, who was the Viceroy of India
from 1876 to 1880, was the chief proponent
of this policy.
• He abandoned the previous policy of
"masterly inactivity" and pursued an
aggressive and interventionist approach
towards Afghanistan.
• This "spirited Forward policy" directly led to
the Second Anglo-Afghan War (1878–1880),
which proved disastrous for the British in
many respects.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q8. At a time when empires in Europe were
crumbling before the might of Napoleon which one
of the following Governors-General kept the British
flag flying high in India?
(a) Warren Hastings
(b) Lord Cornwallis
(c) Lord Wellesley

(d) Lord Hastings
The correct option is (d).
• The question refers to the period of the
Napoleonic Wars (1803–1815) and its
immediate aftermath, a time of great turmoil
in Europe.
• During this period, the Governor-General who
significantly consolidated and expanded
British power in India, keeping the “British
flag flying high,” was Lord Hastings (formerly
the Earl of Moira), who served from 1813 to
1823.
• Under his leadership, the British decisively
defeated the Marathas in the Third AngloMaratha War (1817–1818), effectively ending
the Maratha challenge and establishing
undisputed British supremacy over most of
India.
• Lord Wellesley’s major expansion occurred
just before and during the early Napoleonic
era, but Lord Hastings finalized British
dominance.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q9. Which Indian nationalist leader looked upon a
war between Germany and Britain as a godsent
opportunity which would enable Indians to exploit
the situation to their advantage?
(a) C. Rajagopalachari
(b) M. A. Jinnah
(c) Subhas Chandra Bose
(d) Jawaharlal Nehru
The correct option is (c).
• Subhas Chandra Bose held the strong
conviction that India's independence could
be won by taking advantage of Britain's
difficulties.
• He famously believed in the principle that
“my enemy's enemy is my friend.”
• When World War II broke out, he saw the war
between Germany and Britain as a golden
opportunity for India.
• He believed that by aligning with the Axis
powers (Germany and Japan), Indians could
militarily oust the British from India.

•

This belief led to his dramatic escape from
India, his journey to Germany, and his
eventual leadership of the Indian National
Army (INA) in Southeast Asia.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q10. Which one of the following leaders of the
Congress was totally in favour of Cabinet Mission
Plan?
(a) Mahatma Gandhi
(b) Jawaharlal Nehru
(c) Sardar Patel
(d) Maulana Abul Kalam Azad
The correct option is (c).
• The Cabinet Mission Plan of 1946 proposed a
complex three-tiered structure for a united
India.
• While the Congress had reservations,
particularly about the compulsory grouping of
provinces, some leaders saw it as the last
chance to avoid partition.
• Among the top Congress leaders, Sardar
Vallabhbhai Patel was one of the most
pragmatic and was strongly in favour of
accepting the Cabinet Mission Plan.
• He believed that rejecting the plan would
lead to chaos and that it offered a workable,
if not ideal, solution to keep India united.
• He was instrumental in persuading the
Congress Working Committee to accept the
long-term proposals of the plan, although the
initiative ultimately failed due to
disagreements over the interim government.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q11. Which one of the following Indian leaders was
dismissed by the British from the Indian Civil
Service?
(a) Satyendranath Tagore
(b) Surendranath Banerji
(c) R. C. Dutt
(d) Subhash Chandra Bose
The correct option is (b).
• Surendranath Banerji, one of the pioneering
leaders of the Indian nationalist movement,

•
•

•
•

was among the first Indians to qualify for the
Indian Civil Service (ICS).
After clearing the examination, he was posted
as an assistant magistrate in Sylhet.
However, he was soon dismissed from the
service in 1874 on flimsy grounds, for what
was considered a minor judicial error.
Banerji believed his dismissal was racially
motivated.
This unjust act ended his career in the
administration but launched his long and
distinguished career as a nationalist leader,
earning him the name “Surrender Not”
Banerji.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q13. Consider the following events:
(1) Indigo Revolt
(2) Santhal Rebellion
(3) Deccan Riot
(d) Mutiny of the Sepoys
The correct chronological sequence of these events
is:
(4) 4, 2, 1, 3
(b) 4, 2, 3, 1
(c) 2, 4, 3, 1
(d) 2, 4, 1, 3
The correct option is (d).
• To arrange these popular uprisings in
chronological order:
o Santhal Rebellion: This was a massive
tribal insurrection against zamindars,
moneylenders, and the British
administration in the region of
present-day Jharkhand. It took place
in 1855–56.
o Mutiny of the Sepoys: This refers to
the great Revolt of 1857, which began
with a mutiny of the sepoys of the
East India Company's army.
o Indigo Revolt: This was a major
peasant uprising in Bengal against
the oppressive system of indigo
cultivation forced upon them by
European planters. It occurred in
1859–60.

o

•

Deccan Riots: These were agrarian
riots that broke out in the Deccan
region of the Bombay Presidency in
1875, where peasants targeted the
bonds and account books of
moneylenders.
Therefore, the correct sequence is Santhal
Rebellion (2), Mutiny of the Sepoys (4),
Indigo Revolt (1), Deccan Riot (3).
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q14. The term “imperial preference” was applied to
the
(a) special privileges on British imports in India
(b) racial discrimination by the Britishers
(c) subordination of Indian interest to that of the
British
(d) preference given to British political agents over
Indian Princes
The correct option is (a).
• "Imperial Preference" was a specific
economic policy adopted by the British
Empire, particularly in the early 20th century.
• It referred to a system of reciprocal tariffs
and trade agreements between Britain and its
colonies and dominions.
• In the context of India, it meant giving special
privileges, such as lower customs duties, to
British imports into India, while Indian goods
faced higher tariffs in Britain and other parts
of the empire.
• This policy was designed to favour British
industries and integrate the colonial
economy more tightly with that of the
metropole, often at the expense of local
industries.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q15. Assertion (A): Lord Linlithgow described the
August Movement of 1942 as the most serious
rebellion since Sepoy Mutiny. Reason (R): There was
massive upsurge of the peasantry in certain areas.
(a) Both A and R are true, and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true, but R is not a correct

explanation of A
(c) A is true, but R is false
(d) A is false, but R is true
The correct option is (a).
• Assertion (A) is true. The Quit India
Movement of 1942, also known as the August
Movement, was a massive and spontaneous
uprising. Viceroy Lord Linlithgow himself
described it in a secret report as “by far the
most serious rebellion since that of 1857.”
• Reason (R) is also true. A key feature of the
movement, especially after the initial urban
phase was suppressed, was the massive
upsurge of the peasantry in several areas like
eastern UP, Bihar, and Midnapur in Bengal.
Peasants attacked symbols of British authority
and even established short-lived “national
governments.”
• The reason correctly explains the assertion
because it was this widespread and militant
participation of the peasantry that gave the
movement its character as a serious rebellion,
comparable in its intensity and geographical
spread to the Revolt of 1857.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q16. There was no independent development of
industries in India during British rule because of the
(a) absence of heavy industries
(b) scarcity of foreign capital
(c) scarcity of natural resources
(d) preference of the rich to invest in land
The correct option is (a).
• The industrial development that occurred in
India during British rule was lopsided and
stunted.
• While some consumer goods industries like
cotton textiles and sugar did develop, there
was a conspicuous absence of heavy
industries.
• The British colonial government’s policies did
not encourage, and often actively
discouraged, the development of key sectors
like heavy machinery, iron and steel, and
chemical industries.

•
•

•

These industries form the bedrock of a
modern, self-reliant industrial economy.
Without a strong capital goods sector, India
remained dependent on Britain for
machinery and technology, which prevented
any real independent development of
industries.
Scarcity of foreign capital was not the issue
(British capital was plentiful but directed
towards extractive industries), nor was
scarcity of natural resources.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

answer using the codes given below the Lists:
List-I (Books)

List-II (Authors)

influential book, which reinterpreted the
1857 revolt as a nationalist war for
independence, was written by Vinayak
Damodar Savarkar. (A-4)
• B. Anandamath: This historical novel, set in
the backdrop of the Sannyasi Rebellion, was
written by Bankim Chandra Chatterjee. It is

Mataram.” (B-3)

philosophical work of Sri Aurobindo, in which

based on the Upanishads, written by

Hence, option (a) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 2000

Q1. While delivering the presidential address, the
Congress President who advocated the introduction
of Roman script for Hindi language was
(a) Mahatma Gandhi
(b) Jawaharlal Nehru
(c) Abul Kalam Azad
(d) Subhas Chandra Bose
The correct option is (d).
• Subhas Chandra Bose, during his tenure as
the President of the Indian National
Congress, was a strong proponent of national
unity and modernization.
• In his presidential address at the Haripura
session of the Congress in 1938, he put
forward several forward-looking ideas for a
free India.
• Among these was the proposal for a common
national language, which he suggested should
be Hindustani (a blend of Hindi and Urdu).
• To make this language accessible to all
Indians, especially those in non-Hindi
speaking regions, he advocated for it to be
written in the Roman script.
• This was a radical idea aimed at simplifying
the language and promoting national
integration.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q2. At the time of partition of India, which one of
the following provinces of British India came forward
with a plan for a united and independent existence?
(a) Punjab
(b) Assam
(c) Bengal
(d) Bihar
The correct option is (a).

•

•

•

•

•

As the partition of India became imminent,
the future of the large, multi-religious
provinces of Punjab and Bengal became a
major point of contention.
In Punjab, there was a significant, though
ultimately unsuccessful, move to avoid
partition and maintain the province's unity.
The Unionist Party, a cross-communal party
of landlords that had long governed the
province, along with some Sikh leaders,
explored the possibility of an independent,
united Punjab, separate from both India and
Pakistan.
This plan, however, failed to gain traction due
to the escalating communal violence and the
firm positions taken by the Congress and the
Muslim League, leading to the tragic partition
of the province.
A similar, less developed plan for a united
Bengal was also floated but did not
materialize.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q3. The Balkan Plan for fragmentation of India was
the brain-child of
(a) W. Churchill
(b) M. A. Jinnah
(c) Lord Mountbatten
(d) V. P. Menon
The correct option is (c).
• The "Balkan Plan" was an early, alternative
plan for the transfer of power formulated by
Lord Mountbatten in April–May 1947.
• This plan, also known as the "Dickie Bird
Plan", envisaged the fragmentation of India.
• It proposed transferring power not to a
central government, but to the individual
provinces, or to confederations of provinces
if they chose to form them.
• The princely states would also become
independent.
• This would have likely led to the
“Balkanization” of India into numerous small,
weak states.

•

The plan was vehemently rejected by
Jawaharlal Nehru, who saw it as a recipe for
chaos and disintegration, leading
Mountbatten to quickly abandon it in favor of
the final partition plan.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q4. “In this instance we could not play off the
Mohammedans against the Hindus”. To which one of
the following events did this remark of Aitchison
relate?
(a) Revolt of 1857
(b) Champaran Satyagraha (1917)
(c) Khilafat and Non-Cooperation Movement (1919–
22)
(d) August Movement of 1942
The correct option is (c).
• This remark was made by Charles Aitchison, a
British official, reflecting on the nature of the
Khilafat and Non-Cooperation Movement
(1919–22).
• A cornerstone of British imperial policy was
"divide and rule", which often involved
“playing off” Hindus and Muslims against
each other to weaken the nationalist
movement.
• However, the Khilafat and Non-Cooperation
Movement witnessed an unprecedented
level of Hindu–Muslim unity.
• Mahatma Gandhi strategically merged the
Congress's Non-Cooperation campaign with
the Muslims' Khilafat agitation, creating a
powerful, united front against the British.
• Aitchison's comment expresses the British
administration's frustration that their usual
tactic of division had failed in the face of this
remarkable unity.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q5. The Indian National Army (INA) came into
existence in 1943 in
(a) Japan
(b) Burma
(c) Singapore
(d) Malaya

The correct option is (c).
• The Indian National Army (INA) was first
formed in 1942 but was soon disbanded.
• It was revived and reorganized into a
powerful fighting force in 1943.
• This revival took place in Singapore following
the arrival of Subhas Chandra Bose.
• On taking command, Bose proclaimed the
formation of the Arzi Hukumat-e-Azad Hind
(Provisional Government of Free India) in
Singapore on October 21, 1943.
• The INA was declared its official army,
drawing thousands of civilian volunteers and
ex-prisoners of war from the Indian
expatriate population in Southeast Asia.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q6. The last major extension of British Indian
Territory took place during the time of
(a) Dufferin
(b) Dalhousie
(c) Lytton
(d) Curzon
The correct option is (b).
• The period of Lord Dalhousie's GovernorGeneralship (1848–1856) witnessed the final
and most aggressive phase of British
territorial expansion in India.
• He annexed vast territories through both war
and his controversial policy of the Doctrine of
Lapse.
• Key annexations during his time included:
o Punjab (1849) after the Second
Anglo-Sikh War.
o Lower Burma (1852) after the Second
Anglo-Burmese War.
o Several states through the Doctrine of
Lapse, such as Satara (1848), Nagpur
(1854), and Jhansi (1853).
o Awadh (Oudh) (1856) on the pretext
of misgovernance.
• After Dalhousie, and especially after the
Revolt of 1857, the British policy shifted from
outright annexation to indirect control,
making his tenure the last period of major

territorial extension.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q7. As an alternative to the partition of India,
Gandhiji suggested to Mountbatten that he
(a) postpone granting of independence
(b) invite Jinnah to form the government
(c) invite Nehru and Jinnah to form the government
together
(d) invite the army to take over for some time
The correct option is (b).
• In a last-ditch effort to prevent the partition
of India, Mahatma Gandhi made a radical
and surprising proposal to Lord Mountbatten
in April 1947.
• He suggested that the British should bypass
the Congress and invite Muhammad Ali
Jinnah, the leader of the Muslim League, to
form the interim government and become the
Prime Minister.
• Gandhi's logic was that this would satisfy
Jinnah's ambition for power and demonstrate
the Congress's commitment to a united India,
thereby making the demand for Pakistan
redundant.
• However, this proposal was not acceptable to
the Congress Working Committee or to
Mountbatten, and it was quickly dropped.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q8. The native State of Tripura became involved in
the Freedom Movement early in the 20th century
because
(a) the kings of Tripura were always anti-British
(b) the Bengal revolutionaries took shelter in Tripura
(c) the tribes of the State were fiercely freedom loving

(d) there were already some groups fighting against
the kingship and its protector, the British
The correct option is (d).
• The involvement of Tripura in the broader
Indian Freedom Movement was largely
driven by internal political dynamics.
• Within the princely state, there were already
some groups fighting against the autocratic
rule of the local kingship (the Manikya
dynasty) and its ultimate protector, the
British paramount power.
• These internal anti-monarchical and antiimperialist struggles, such as the Reang
uprising, naturally aligned with the larger
goals of the Indian National Movement.
• Nationalist ideas from neighboring Bengal
also influenced these local movements,
integrating Tripura's struggle into the allIndia freedom movement.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q9. After returning from South Africa, Gandhiji
launched his first successful Satyagraha in
(a) Chauri-Chaura
(b) Dandi
(c) Champaran
(d) Bardoli
The correct option is (c).
• After returning to India from South Africa in
1915, Mahatma Gandhi's first major and
successful application of his technique of
Satyagraha was in Champaran, Bihar, in 1917.
• He went there at the request of local
peasants to investigate and protest against
the oppressive tinkathia system, under which
they were forced by European planters to
cultivate indigo on a portion of their land.
• Gandhi's method of non-violent civil
disobedience, which involved a detailed
inquiry and a readiness to court arrest,
forced the British authorities to appoint a
commission of inquiry and ultimately led to
the abolition of the exploitative system.

•

This victory established Gandhi's leadership
in India.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q10. Assertion (A): Lord Linlithgo described the
August Movement of 1942 as the most serious revolt
after the Sepoy mutiny. Reason (R): Peasants joined
the movement in large number in some places.
(a) Both A and R are true, and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true, but R is not a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true, but R is false
(d) A is false, but R is true
The correct option is (a).
• Assertion (A) is true. The Quit India
Movement of 1942, also known as the August
Movement, was a massive and spontaneous
uprising.
• Viceroy Lord Linlithgow himself described it
in a secret report as "by far the most serious
rebellion since that of 1857."
• Reason (R) is also true. A key feature of the
movement, especially after the initial urban
phase was suppressed, was the massive
upsurge of the peasantry in several areas like
eastern UP, Bihar, and Midnapur in Bengal.
• Peasants attacked symbols of British
authority and even established short-lived
“national governments.”
• The reason correctly explains the assertion
because it was this widespread and militant
participation of the peasantry that gave the
movement its character as a serious rebellion,
comparable in its intensity and geographical
spread to the Revolt of 1857.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q11. Assertion (A): The basic weakness of the early
nationalist movement lay in its narrow social base.
Reason (R): It fought for the narrow interests of the
social groups which joined it.
(a) Both A and R are true, and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true, but R is not a correct

explanation of A
(c) A is true, but R is false
(d) A is false, but R is true
The correct option is (c).
• Assertion (A) is true. A widely accepted
critique of the early nationalist movement
(the Moderate phase, 1885–1905) is that its
social base was narrow.
• It was largely confined to the Englisheducated urban middle class, including
lawyers, doctors, journalists, and
government servants, and it failed to
penetrate the vast rural masses.
• Reason (R) is false. Despite its narrow social
base, the early nationalist movement did not
fight for the narrow interests of its own class.
• On the contrary, it raised broad national
issues that affected all sections of society.
• It demanded constitutional reforms,
administrative changes, and most
importantly, put forward a powerful
economic critique of colonialism (like the
Drain Theory) that highlighted the
exploitation of the Indian masses, including
the peasantry.
• Therefore, the assertion is true, but the
reason is false.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q12. That the per capita income in India was Rs. 20
in 1867–68, was ascertained for the first time by
(a) M. G. Ranade
(b) Sir W. Hunter
(c) R. C. Dutta
(d) Dadabhai Naoroji
The correct option is (d).
• Dadabhai Naoroji, known as the “Grand Old
Man of India”, was the first person to make a
systematic attempt to estimate India's
national and per capita income.
• In his seminal work, Poverty and Un-British
Rule in India, he presented his pioneering
calculations.
• Based on his analysis of agricultural and nonagricultural production data for the year

•

1867–68, he ascertained that the per capita
income in India was a meagre Rs. 20.
This figure became a powerful tool for the
early nationalists to highlight the poverty and
economic exploitation of India under British
rule.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q13. Match List I with List II and select the correct
answer using the codes given below the Lists:
List I
A. Chittagong Armoury Raid
B. Abhinav Bharat
C. Anushilan Samiti
D. Kuka Movement
List II
1. Kalpana Dutt
2. Guru Ram Singh
3. Vinayak Damodar Savarkar
4. Aurobindo Ghosh

ABCD

(b) 1 3 2 4

The correct option is (a).
• A. Chittagong Armoury Raid (1930): Kalpana
Dutt was a prominent woman revolutionary
and a key member of Surya Sen's group that
carried out the Chittagong Armoury Raid. She
was arrested and tried for her role in the
uprising. (A-1)
• B. Abhinav Bharat: This secret revolutionary
society was founded by Vinayak Damodar
Savarkar and his brother in 1904. (B-3)
• C. Anushilan Samiti: This was a major
revolutionary organization in Bengal.
Aurobindo Ghosh was one of its key
ideological inspirations and early leaders
before he turned to spiritualism. (C-4)
• D. Kuka Movement: This was a socio-religious
and political movement among the Sikhs in
Punjab in the 19th century. It was founded by

•

Bhagat Jawahar Mal, and its most prominent
leader was Guru Ram Singh. (D-2)
The correct matching is A-1, B-3, C-4, D-2.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q14. Match List I with List II and select the correct
answer using the codes given below the Lists:
List-I
A. Land allotted to big feudal landlords
B. Land allotted to revenue farmers or rent collectors
C. Land allotted to each peasant with the right to
sublet, mortgage, gift or sell
D. Revenue settlements made at village level
List-II
1. Jagirdari System
2. Ryotwari System
3. Mahalwari System
4. Zamindari System
Codes
ABCD
(a) 1 3 2 4
(b) 1 4 2 3
(c) 3 4 1 2
(d) 2 1 3 4
The correct option is (b).
• A. Land allotted to big feudal landlords: This
describes the Jagirdari System, a pre-British
system where the right to collect revenue
from a piece of land (a jagir) was granted to
an official (jagirdar) in lieu of a salary. (A-1)
• B. Land allotted to revenue farmers or rent
collectors: This describes the Zamindari
System (or Permanent Settlement)
introduced by the British. The Zamindars
were recognized as proprietors of the land,
responsible for collecting rent from peasants
and paying a fixed revenue to the state. (B-4)
• C. Land allotted to each peasant with the
right to sublet, mortgage, gift or sell: This is
the defining feature of the Ryotwari System,
where the individual cultivator (ryot) was
recognized as the owner of the land and paid
revenue directly to the state. (C-2)
• D. Revenue settlements made at village
level: This describes the Mahalwari System,

where the revenue settlement was made
with the village community or estate (mahal)
as a whole, which was collectively
responsible for paying the revenue. (D-3)
The correct matching is A-1, B-4, C-2, D-3.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q15. Which one of the following is not a feature of
the Government of India Act of 1935?
(a) Diarchy at the Centre as well as in the provinces
(b) A bicameral legislature
(c) Provincial autonomy
(d) An All-India Federation
The correct option is (a).
• The Government of India Act of 1935 was a
long and complex piece of legislation with
several key features.
• It proposed an All-India Federation (d),
introduced provincial autonomy by abolishing
diarchy in the provinces (c), and provided for
a bicameral federal legislature (b).
• However, the statement that it introduced
Diarchy at the Centre as well as in the
provinces is incorrect.
• The Act abolished diarchy in the provinces
and instead introduced it at the Centre. The
federal subjects were to be divided into
"Reserved" and "Transferred" subjects.
• Therefore, this feature makes the statement
incorrect.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 2001
Q1. Who among the following organised the famous
Chittagong armoury raid?
(a) Laxmi Sehgal
(b) Surya Sen
(c) Batukeshwar Datta
(d) J.M. Sengupta
The correct option is (b).
• The Chittagong Armoury Raid of April 1930
was one of the most daring and well-planned
revolutionary actions during the Indian
freedom struggle.

•

•

•

The raid was masterminded and organized by
Surya Sen, a schoolteacher who was
popularly and respectfully known as "Master
Da."
He led a group of revolutionaries from the
Indian Republican Army, Chittagong Branch,
in an attempt to capture the two main
armouries in Chittagong, disrupt
communications, and establish a provisional
revolutionary government.
While the raid itself was not entirely
successful in its objectives, it was a major
blow to British prestige and inspired
revolutionaries across India.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q2. A London branch of the All-India Muslim League
was established in 1908 under the presidency of
(a) Agha Khan
(b) Ameer Ali
(c) Liaquat Ali Khan
(d) M.A. Jinnah
The correct option is (b).
• While the All-India Muslim League was
founded in Dhaka in 1906, a branch was
established in London to promote its
objectives and influence British public
opinion and policymakers.
• This London branch was established in 1908
under the presidency of Syed Ameer Ali.
• Ameer Ali was a prominent jurist, political
leader, and scholar who had settled in Britain.
• He played a crucial role in advocating for the
political rights of Indian Muslims, including
the demand for separate electorates, which
was granted in the Morley–Minto Reforms of

1909.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q3. Who among the following, was the President of
the All-India States’ Peoples’ Conference in 1939?
(a) Jaya Prakash Narayan
(b) Jawaharlal Nehru
(c) Sheikh Abdullah
(d) Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel
The correct option is (b).
• The All-India States' Peoples' Conference
(AISPC) was an organization of political
movements in the princely states of British
India, demanding democratic rights and
responsible government.
• Initially, the Indian National Congress
maintained a policy of non-interference in
the affairs of the princely states.
• By the late 1930s, it began to actively support
the states' peoples' movements.
• Signifying this shift, Jawaharlal Nehru was
elected as the President of the All-India
States’ Peoples’ Conference for its session
held in Ludhiana in 1939.
• This act symbolized the integration of the
struggles in British India and the princely
states into a single national movement.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q4. The Hunter Commission was appointed after the
(a) Black hole incident
(b) Jallianwala Bagh massacre
(c) Uprising of 1857
(d) Partition of Bengal
The correct option is (b).
• The Hunter Commission, officially known as
the Disorders Inquiry Committee, was
appointed by the British government in
October 1919.
• Its specific purpose was to investigate the
recent disturbances in India, most notably
the Jallianwala Bagh massacre that had
occurred in Amritsar on April 13, 1919.
• The commission, chaired by Lord Hunter, was
tasked with inquiring into the causes of the

•

unrest and the measures taken by the
government to deal with it.
Its report, which was seen as a whitewash of
General Dyer's actions, was widely
condemned by Indian nationalists.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q5. Who among the following leaders proposed to
adopt Complete Independence as the goal of the
Congress in the Ahmedabad session of 1921?
(a) Abul Kalam Azad
(b) Hasrat Mohani
(c) Jawaharlal Nehru
(d) Mohandas Karamchand Gandhi
The correct option is (b).
• At the Ahmedabad session of the Indian
National Congress in 1921, the prevailing goal
was still Swaraj within the British Empire.
• The poet and freedom fighter Maulana Hasrat
Mohani made a radical proposal.
• He moved a resolution to define Swaraj as
"Complete Independence, free from all
foreign control."
• This was one of the first times that the
demand for complete independence was
formally proposed from a major Congress
platform.
• Although the motion was considered too
radical at the time and was opposed by
Mahatma Gandhi, it signaled the growing
sentiment for full independence that would
be officially adopted by the Congress in 1929.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q6. Under the Permanent Settlement, 1793, the
zamindars were required to issue pattas to the

farmers which were not issued by many of the
zamindars. The reason was
(a) the zamindars were trusted by the farmers
(b) there was no official check upon the zamindars
(c) it was the responsibility of the British Government
(d) the farmers were not interested in getting pattas
The correct option is (b).
• The Permanent Settlement of 1793,
introduced by Lord Cornwallis, recognized the
zamindars as proprietors of the land.
• The settlement required the zamindars to
issue pattas (deeds) to the ryots (farmers),
specifying the rent to be paid, intended to
protect the tenants from arbitrary rent hikes.
• However, in practice, this provision was
widely ignored.
• The primary reason was that there was no
effective official check or enforcement
mechanism to compel the zamindars to issue
these pattas.
• The colonial administration was primarily
concerned with the timely collection of its
fixed revenue from the zamindar and did not
create an administrative machinery to
oversee the zamindar–tenant relationship,
leaving the tenants at the mercy of the
landlords.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q7. Consider the following statements:
1. Arya Samaj was founded in 1835.
2. Lala Lajpat Rai opposed the appeal of Arya
Samaj to the authority of Vedas in support of
its social reform programmes.
3. Under Keshab Chandra Sen, the Brahmo
Samaj campaigned for women’s education.
4. Vinoba Bhave founded the Sarvodaya Samaj
to work among refugees.
Which of these statements are correct?
(a) 1 and 2
(b) 2 and 3
(c) 2 and 4
(d) 3 and 4
The correct option is (d).

•

•

•

•

•

Statement 1 is incorrect. The Arya Samaj was
founded by Swami Dayanand Saraswati in
1875, not 1835.
Statement 2 is incorrect. Lala Lajpat Rai was a
prominent leader of the Arya Samaj in
Punjab. He did not oppose its appeal to the
authority of the Vedas; in fact, he was a
strong supporter of its principles.
Statement 3 is correct. Keshab Chandra Sen,
as a leader of the Brahmo Samaj, was a
radical social reformer who actively
campaigned for women's education, widow
remarriage, and the abolition of child
marriage.
Statement 4 is correct. After India's
independence, Vinoba Bhave founded the
Sarvodaya Samaj (Society for the Welfare of
All) to carry forward Gandhian ideals. A
significant part of its early work involved
providing relief and rehabilitation for
refugees affected by the partition of India.
Therefore, only statements 3 and 4 are
correct.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 2002

Q1. The last opportunity to avoid the partition of
India was lost with the rejection of
(a) Cripps Mission
(b) Rajagopalachari Formula
(c) Cabinet Mission
(d) Wavell Plan
The correct option is (c).
• The Cabinet Mission of 1946 proposed a
complex, three-tiered federal structure for
India that explicitly rejected the creation of a
sovereign Pakistan.
• It aimed to keep India united while providing
significant autonomy to Muslim-majority
provinces through a system of provincial
groupings.
• Both the Congress and the Muslim League
initially accepted the long-term plan, albeit
with different interpretations.

•

•

However, disagreements over the formation
of the interim government and the nature of
the provincial groupings led to the plan's
collapse.
The failure of the Cabinet Mission Plan is
widely seen by historians as the last real
opportunity to avoid the partition of India.
After its rejection, the political discourse
shifted decisively towards partition as the
only viable solution.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q2. Which one of the following submitted in 1875 a
petition to the House of Commons demanding
India’s direct representation in the British
Parliament?
(a) The Deccan Association
(b) The Indian Association
(c) The Madras Mahajan Sabha
(d) The Poona Sarvajanik Sabha
The correct option is (d).
• The Poona Sarvajanik Sabha, founded in 1870
by M.G. Ranade and others, was one of the
most important pre-Congress nationalist
organizations in India.
• It was known for its meticulous work in
articulating popular grievances through
constitutional means.
• In 1875, the Sabha took the bold and
unprecedented step of submitting a petition
to the British House of Commons.
• This petition famously demanded that India
be granted direct representation in the
British Parliament, arguing for the principle of
"no taxation without representation."
• This was a significant step in the evolution of
Indian political demands.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q3. The real intention of the British to include the
princely states in the Federal Union proposed by the
India Act of 1935 was to
(a) exercise more and direct political and
administrative control over the princely states
(b) involve the princes actively in the administration of

the colony
(c) finally effect the complete political and
administrative take-over of all the princely states by
the British
(d) use the princes to counter-balance the antiimperialist doctrines of the nationalist leaders
The correct option is (d).
• The Government of India Act of 1935
proposed an All-India Federation comprising
both British Indian provinces and the princely
states.
• The real political intention behind including
the princely states was strategic.
• The British saw the princes, who were
autocratic rulers dependent on British
support, as a conservative and loyalist force.
• By bringing them into the federal legislature,
where they would be given significant
weightage (and their representatives would
be nominated, not elected), the British hoped
to use the princes to counter-balance the
growing power of the elected nationalist
leaders from British India.
• The princes were expected to vote with the
government and act as a brake on the antiimperialist and radical demands of the Indian
National Congress.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q4. Which one of the following Acts of British India
strengthened the Viceroy’s authority over his
executive council by substituting “portfolio” or
departmental system for corporate functioning?
(a) Indian Councils Act, 1861
(b) Government of India Act, 1858
(c) Indian Councils Act, 1892
(d) Indian Councils Act, 1909
The correct option is (a).
• The Indian Councils Act of 1861 introduced
several important changes to the structure of
the Viceroy's Executive Council.
• A key innovation was the introduction of the
"portfolio" system.
• Prior to this, the council functioned
collectively.

•

•

Under the new system, each member of the
council was placed in charge of a specific
department (or portfolio), such as Home,
Revenue, or Military.
This system, introduced by Viceroy Lord
Canning, streamlined administration and
significantly strengthened the Viceroy's
authority, as he could now deal with
individual departments directly and had the
final say, while the council as a whole was
consulted only on major policy matters.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q5. The President of the Indian National Congress at
the time of partition of India was
(a) C. Rajagopalachari
(b) J. B. Kripalani
(c) Jawaharlal Nehru
(d) Maulana Abul Kalam Azad
The correct option is (b).
• The partition of India and the transfer of
power occurred in August 1947.
• The President of the Indian National
Congress during this crucial and turbulent
period was Acharya J. B. Kripalani.
• He was elected as the Congress President for
the Meerut session in November 1946 and
held the post throughout the final
negotiations, the partition, and the dawn of
independence.
• He resigned from the presidency in
November 1947, shortly after independence.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

villages.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

o (d) Rabindranath Tagore, while
critical of some aspects of the
Swadeshi movement, preached the
concept of Atmasakti (self-reliance),
emphasizing constructive work like

Q7. With which one of the following mountain tribes
did the British first come into contact with after the
grant of Diwani in the year 1765?
(a) Garo
(b) Khasis
(c) Kukis
(d) Tipperahs
The correct option is (b).
• After the British East India Company acquired
the Diwani (the right to collect revenue) of
Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa in 1765, their
territory expanded to include the district of
Sylhet (now in Bangladesh).
• Sylhet bordered the hill territories of the
Khasis.
• The British soon sought to establish trade
links and control over the resources,
particularly limestone, found in the Khasi
Hills.
• This led to their first significant and direct
contact with the Khasi tribes, which
eventually resulted in conflicts and the
gradual extension of British control over the
region.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q8. With reference to colonial period of Indian
history, match List I (Person) with List II (Event) and
select the correct answer using the codes given
below the lists :
List I (Person)
A. MacDonald
B. Linlithgow
C. Dalhousie
D. Chelmsford
List II (Event)
1. Doctrine of Lapse
2. Communal Award
3. August Offer
4. Dyarchy
Codes:
ABCD

(a) 3 2 1 4
(b) 3 2 4 1
(c) 2 3 1 4
(d) 2 3 4 1
The correct option is (c).
• A. MacDonald: Ramsay MacDonald, the
British Prime Minister, announced the
Communal Award in 1932, which granted
separate electorates to various minority
communities. (A-2)
• B. Linlithgow: Lord Linlithgow, the Viceroy of
India, made the August Offer in 1940 in an
attempt to secure Indian cooperation in
World War II. (B-3)
• C. Dalhousie: Lord Dalhousie, GovernorGeneral from 1848–1856, aggressively
implemented the Doctrine of Lapse to annex
several Indian states. (C-1)
• D. Chelmsford: Lord Chelmsford was the

Q9. With reference to the Indian freedom struggle,
which one of the following statements is not correct?
(a) Hakim Ajmal Khan was one of the leaders to start a
nationalist and militant Ahrar movement
(b) When the Indian National Congress was formed,
Sayyid Ahmad Khan opposed it
(c) The All-India Muslim League which was formed in
1906 vehemently opposed the partition of Bengal and
separate electorates
(d) Maulana Barkatullah and Maulana Obeidullah
Sindhi were among those who formed a Provisional
Government of India in Kabul
The correct option is (c).
• This statement is not correct. The All-India
Muslim League, formed in 1906, did not
oppose the partition of Bengal or separate
electorates.

•

•

•

On the contrary, the League was formed with
the encouragement of the British and was
largely in support of the Partition of Bengal,
as it created a new Muslim-majority
province.
Furthermore, one of the League's primary
early demands was for separate electorates
for Muslims, which they saw as essential for
protecting their political interests.
The other statements are correct.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q10. During the Indian freedom struggle, the Khudai
Khidmatgars, also known as Red Shirts, called for
(a) the Union of Pashtun tribal areas in north-west
with the Afghanistan
(b) the adoption of terrorist tactics and methods for
terrorising and finally ousting the colonial rulers
(c) the adoption of communist revolutionary ideology
for political and social reform
(d) the Pathan regional nationalist unity and a struggle
against colonialism
The correct option is (d).
• The Khudai Khidmatgars (Servants of God),
popularly known as the "Red Shirts" because
of the colour of their uniform, was a nonviolent resistance movement among the
Pashtuns of the North-West Frontier
Province.
• Led by the charismatic Khan Abdul Ghaffar
Khan (also known as the "Frontier Gandhi"),
the movement's primary aims were Pathan
regional nationalist unity and a struggle
against colonialism.
• Deeply influenced by Mahatma Gandhi, they
were committed to non-violence and worked
for social reform and independence from
British rule.
• They were closely allied with the Indian
National Congress.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q11. Match List I (Acts of Colonial Government of
India) with List II (Provisions) and select the correct
answer using the codes given below the lists:

List I (Acts of Colonial Government of India)
A. Charter Act, 1813
B. Regulating Act
C. Act of 1858
D. Pitt’s India Act
List II (Provisions)
1. Set up a Board of Control in Britain to fully
regulate the East India Company’s affairs in
India
2. Company’s trade monopoly in India was
ended
3. The power to govern was transferred from
East India Company to British Crown
4. The Company’s Directors were asked to
present to the British Government all
correspondence and documents pertaining to
the administration of the company
Codes:
A BCD
(a) 2 4 3 1
(b) 1 3 4 2
(c) 2 3 4 1
(d) 1 4 3 2
The correct option is (a).
• A. Charter Act, 1813: A key provision of this
act was that the East India Company's trade
monopoly in India was ended, opening up
Indian trade to all British merchants (though
the monopoly on tea trade and trade with
China was retained). (A-2)
• B. Regulating Act (1773): This was the first
step by the British Parliament to control the
Company's affairs. It required that the
Company's Directors present all
correspondence regarding civil, military, and
revenue affairs to the British Government.
(B-4)
• C. Act of 1858 (Government of India Act,
1858): Following the Revolt of 1857, this act
abolished the East India Company and
transferred the power to govern India
directly to the British Crown. (C-3)
• D. Pitt’s India Act (1784): This act established
a system of dual control by creating a Board
of Control in Britain to supervise the civil,

•

military, and revenue affairs of the Company
in India. (D-1)
The correct matching is A-2, B-4, C-3, D-1.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q12. Assertion (A): The effect of labour participation
in the Indian nationalist upsurge of the early 1930s
was weak. Reason (R): The labour leaders considered
the ideology of Indian National Congress as
bourgeois and reactionary.
(a) Both A and R are individually true and R is the
correct explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are individually true but R is not a
correct explanation of A
(c) A is true but R is false
(d) A is false but R is true
The correct option is (a).
• Assertion (A) is true. During the Civil
Disobedience Movement of the early 1930s,
the participation of the industrial working
class (labour) was noticeably weak compared
to their involvement in the late 1920s. While
there were some strikes, they were not as
widespread or integrated into the main
nationalist campaign.
• Reason (R) is also true. By the early 1930s,
the labour movement was heavily influenced
by communists and other left-wing leaders.
These leaders were often critical of the Indian
National Congress, which they considered to
be a bourgeois organization that did not truly
represent the interests of the working class.
• They viewed its ideology as reformist and
reactionary from a revolutionary perspective.
• The reason correctly explains the assertion
because this ideological divide and suspicion
of the Congress's motives led many labour
leaders to keep their unions and workers
aloof from the Congress-led Civil
Disobedience Movement.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 2003
Q1. The leader of the Bardoli Satyagraha (1928) was:
(a) Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel
(b) Mahatma Gandhi
(c) Vithalbhai J. Patel
(d) Mahadev Desai
The correct option is (a).
• The Bardoli Satyagraha of 1928 was a major
peasant movement in the Bardoli taluka of
Gujarat against an arbitrary and excessive
hike in land revenue (by 30%) by the British
government.
• The local Congress leaders invited Sardar
Vallabhbhai Patel to lead the agitation.
• Patel organized the peasants with remarkable
efficiency, dividing the region into camps and
using methods of social boycott and nonviolent resistance to defy the government.
His masterful leadership earned him the title
"Sardar" (leader) from the women of Bardoli.
The movement was a resounding success,
forcing the government to appoint an inquiry
and reduce the revenue enhancement.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q2. Which one of the following provisions was NOT
made in the Charter Act of 1833?
(a) The trading activities of the East India Company
were to be abolished
(b) The designation of the supreme authority was to
be changed as the Governor-General of India in
Council
(c) All law-making powers to be conferred on
Governor-General in Council
(d) An Indian was to be appointed as a Law Member in
the Governor-General’s Council
The correct option is (d).
• The Charter Act of 1833 was a significant step
in the centralization of British administration
in India.
• It abolished the East India Company's trading
activities (a), making it a purely
administrative body.

•

•

•

It changed the designation of the GovernorGeneral of Bengal to the Governor-General of
India (b), with Lord William Bentinck being
the first to hold this title.
It centralized legislative authority by
conferring all law-making powers on the
Governor-General in Council (c).
However, the provision that an Indian was to
be appointed as a Law Member is incorrect.
The Act did provide for the appointment of a
Law Member to the council, but the first
person appointed to this post was an
Englishman, Lord Macaulay, not an Indian.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q3. An important aspect of the Cripps Mission of
1942 was :
(a) That all Indian States should join the Indian Union
as a condition to consider any degree of autonomy for
India
(b) The creation of an Indian Union with Dominion
status very soon after the Second World War
(c) The active participation and cooperation of the
Indian people, communities and political parties in the
British war effort as a condition for granting
independence with full sovereign status to India after
war.
(d) The framing of a constitution for the entire Indian
Union, with no separate constitution for any province,
and a Union Constitution to be accepted by all
provinces
The correct option is (b).
• The Cripps Mission was sent to India in March
1942 to secure Indian cooperation for the
British war effort during World War II.
• The most important proposal of the mission
was the creation of an Indian Union with
Dominion status that would be set up
immediately after the war.
• This Indian Union would be free to decide its
relationship with the Commonwealth and
other international bodies. The mission also
proposed a constitution-making body to be
set up after the war. However, it was rejected
by the Congress because it did not offer

immediate transfer of real power and
because its provision for provinces to secede
was seen as a blueprint for partition.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q4. With reference to colonial rule in India, what
was sought by the Ilbert Bill in 1883?
(a) To bring Indians and Europeans on par as far as the
criminal jurisdiction of courts was concerned
(b) To impose severe restrictions on the freedom of
the native press as it was perceived to be hostile to
colonial rulers
(c) To encourage the native Indians to appear for civil
service examinations by conducting them in India
(d) To allow native Indians to possess arms by
amending the Arms Act.
The correct option is (a).
• The Ilbert Bill, introduced in 1883 during the
viceroyalty of Lord Ripon, was a legislative
measure aimed at ending judicial
discrimination based on race.
• At the time, Indian judges, even senior ones,
were not allowed to preside over criminal
cases involving European subjects.
• The bill sought to amend the law to bring
Indian and European magistrates on par,
allowing qualified Indian judges to try
Europeans in criminal cases.
• The bill faced ferocious opposition from the
European community in India, who saw it as
an attack on their racial privilege. This "white
mutiny" forced the government to heavily
amend the bill, but the controversy deeply
embittered Indian nationalists and exposed
the deep-seated racism of colonial rule.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q5. When Congress leaders condemned the
Montagu-Chelmsford Report, many moderates left
the party to form the:
(a) Swarajya Party
(b) Indian Freedom Party
(c) Independence Federation of India
(d) Indian Liberal Federation
The correct option is (d).

•

•

•

The Montagu-Chelmsford Report, which
formed the basis of the Government of India
Act of 1919, was met with a mixed reaction.
While the Congress, under the growing
influence of its nationalist wing, condemned
it as "disappointing and unsatisfactory," the
older Moderate leaders were inclined to
accept the reforms.
This difference in opinion led to a formal split
in the Congress. The Moderate leaders, who
wanted to work with the new reforms, left
the Congress and formed their own political
party in 1919.
This new party was called the Indian Liberal
Federation. Prominent leaders who joined
this new organization included Surendranath
Banerjea, Tej Bahadur Sapru, and V.S.
Srinivasa Sastri.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q6. With reference to the entry of European powers
into India, which one of the following statements is
NOT correct?
(a) The Portuguese captured Goa in 1499
(b) The English opened their first factory in South
India at Masulipatam
(c) In Eastern India, the English Company opened its
first factory in Orissa in 1633
(d) Under the leadership of Dupleix, the French
occupied Madras in 1746
The correct option is (a).
• This statement is NOT correct. The
Portuguese, under the command of Afonso
de Albuquerque, captured Goa from the
Sultan of Bijapur in 1510, not 1499. This
became the capital of the Portuguese empire
in the East.
• The other statements are correct:
o (b) The English East India Company
established its first factory in South
India at Masulipatnam in 1611.
o (c) The first English factory in Eastern
India was established at Hariharpur in
Orissa in 1633.

o

(d) During the First Carnatic War, the
French forces led by Governor
Dupleix successfully captured Madras
from the British in 1746.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q7. With reference to Indian freedom struggle,
which one of the following statements is NOT
correct?
(a) The Rowlatt Act aroused a wave of popular
indignation and led to the Jallianwala Bagh massacre
(b) Subhas Chandra Bose formed the Forward Bloc
(c) Bhagat Singh was one of the founders of Hindustan
Republican Socialist Association
(d) In 1931, the Congress Session at Karachi opposed
Gandhi-Irwin Pact
The correct option is (d).
• This statement is NOT correct. The Karachi
session of the Congress in March 1931,
presided over by Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel,
formally endorsed the Gandhi-Irwin Pact,
despite strong opposition from the younger,
radical wing of the party who were angered
by the failure to secure commutation of the
death sentences for Bhagat Singh, Rajguru,
and Sukhdev.
• The other statements are correct:
o (a) The repressive Rowlatt Act led to
the nationwide Rowlatt Satyagraha,
and the protests in Punjab culminated
in the tragic Jallianwala Bagh
massacre.
o (b) After resigning from the Congress
presidency in 1939, Subhas Chandra
Bose formed the Forward Bloc as a
left-wing faction within the Congress.
o (c) Bhagat Singh, along with
Chandrasekhar Azad and others,
transformed the Hindustan
Republican Association into the
Hindustan Socialist Republican
Association (HSRA) in 1928.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q8. Who headed the Interim Cabinet formed in the
year 1946?
(a) Rajendra Prasad
(b) Jawaharlal Nehru
(c) Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel
(d) Rajagopalachari
The correct option is (b).
• The Interim Government of India was formed
on September 2, 1946.
• While the Viceroy of India was the formal
head of the Executive Council, Jawaharlal
Nehru was appointed as the Vice-President of
the Council.
• In this capacity, he effectively headed the
cabinet and functioned as the de facto Prime
Minister, leading the government during the
crucial transition to independence.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q9. During the colonial period in India, what was the
purpose of the Whitley Commission?
(a) To review the fitness of India for further political
reforms
(b) To report on the existing conditions of labour and
to make recommendations
(c) To draw up a plan for financial reforms for India
(d) To develop a comprehensive scheme for Civil
Services in India
The correct option is (b).
• The Whitley Commission, officially the Royal
Commission on Labour in India, was
appointed in 1929.
• Its primary purpose was to investigate and
report on the existing conditions of labour in
industrial undertakings and plantations
across India.
• The commission conducted an extensive
survey covering issues like wages, working
hours, housing, health, and industrial
relations. Its comprehensive report,
submitted in 1931, made numerous
recommendations for improving the welfare
of industrial workers and became a landmark
document in the history of labour legislation

in India.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q10. The aim of education as stated by the Wood’s
dispatch of 1854 was:
(a) the creation of employment opportunities for
native Indians
(b) the spread of western culture in India
