of the All-Parties Conference, chaired by
Motilal Nehru.
• Statement 1 is incorrect. The report did not
recommend "complete independence."
Instead, it recommended Dominion Status for
India, similar to Canada and Australia within
the British Empire. This became a point of
contention with the younger, more radical
nationalists like Jawaharlal Nehru and Subhas
Chandra Bose.
• Statement 2 is correct. The report firmly
rejected the principle of separate
electorates. It recommended joint
electorates for all communities, but with
provision of reservation of seats for
minorities (like Muslims) in legislatures in
proportion to their population.
• Statement 3 is correct. The Nehru Report was
one of the first Indian documents to propose
a detailed provision of fundamental rights for
the citizens of India, including freedom of
conscience, speech, and association.

•

Therefore, only statements 2 and 3 are
correct.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q4. Mahatma Gandhi said that some of his deepest
convictions were reflected in a book titled, “Unto
This Last” and the book transformed his life. What
was the message from the book that transformed
Mahatma Gandhi?
(a) Uplifting the oppressed and poor is the moral
responsibility of an educated man.
(b) The good of an individual is contained in the good
of all.
(c) The life of celibacy and spiritual pursuit are
essential for a noble life.
(d) All the statements (a), (b) and (c) are correct in this
context.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
The correct option is (b).
• Mahatma Gandhi was profoundly influenced
by John Ruskin's book, Unto This Last. He
read it on a train journey in South Africa and
stated that it "brought about an
instantaneous and practical transformation
in my life."
• Gandhi summarized the teachings of the book
into three main principles.
• The most central and transformative of these
was the idea that the good of the individual is
contained in the good of all.
• This principle, which Gandhi called Sarvodaya
(the welfare of all), became a cornerstone of
his social and economic philosophy.
• It rejected the utilitarian idea of "the greatest
good of the greatest number" and instead
emphasized that a society's progress should
be measured by the well-being of its most
vulnerable members.
• The other principles he derived were the
equal value of all labor and the nobility of a
life of labor.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q5. The tendency for increased litigation was visible
after the introduction of the land settlement system

of Lord Cornwallis in 1793. The reason for this is
normally traced to which of the following provisions?
(a) Making Zamindar’s position stronger vis-a-vis the
ryot.
(b) Making East India Company an overlord of
zamindars.
(c) Making the judicial system more efficient.
(d) None of the (a), (b) and (c) above.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
The correct option is (d).
• The land settlement system of Lord
Cornwallis (the Permanent Settlement) and
his judicial reforms did lead to a significant
increase in litigation, but the reasons
provided in options (a), (b), and (c) are not the
primary cause.
• The actual reasons were more complex and
included:
o The replacement of traditional,
informal methods of dispute
resolution with a formal, complex,
and expensive legal system. This
encouraged people to go to court.
o The clear definition and registration
of property rights, which made these
rights contestable in a court of law.
o The abolition of court fees by
Cornwallis, which, while wellintentioned, removed a financial
barrier and made it easier for people
to file lawsuits, often for frivolous
reasons.
• Since the core reasons are not accurately
captured in options (a), (b), or (c), the most
appropriate answer is (d).
• The increased power of the Zamindar (a) led
to oppression, not necessarily litigation by the
ryots who could ill-afford it.
• The efficiency of the judicial system (c) is
debatable and not the root cause.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q6. Which one of the following observations is not
true about the Quit India Movement of 1942?
(a) It was a non-violent movement.

(b) It was led by Mahatma Gandhi.
(c) It was a spontaneous movement.
(d) It did not attract the labour class in general.
The correct option is (a).
• The Quit India Movement, launched in
August 1942, was a massive and widespread
uprising.
• Statement (a) is not true. Although Mahatma
Gandhi gave the call for a non-violent
struggle ("Do or Die"), the immediate arrest
of all top leaders left the movement
leaderless.
• As a result, it did not remain entirely nonviolent. There were widespread acts of
sabotage, attacks on government property
like railway stations and post offices, and
violent clashes with the police and army.
• The other statements are true:
o (b) The movement was ideologically
led by Mahatma Gandhi, who gave
the famous "Do or Die" call.
o (c) After the arrest of the leadership,
the movement became largely
spontaneous, with local leaders and
the general populace taking the
initiative.
o (d) For various reasons, including the
influence of the Communist Party
which opposed the movement, it did
not attract the organized labour class
in general, unlike previous
movements.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q7. With reference to the period of colonial rule in
India, “Home Charges” formed an important part of
the drain of wealth from India. Which of the
following funds constituted “Home Charges”?
1. Funds used to support the India Office in
London.
2. Funds used to pay salaries and pensions of
British personnel engaged in India.
3. Funds used for waging wars outside India by
the British.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?

(a) 1 only.
(b) 1 and 2 only.
(c) 2 and 3 only.
(d) 1, 2, and 3.
The correct option is (d).
• "Home Charges" were a key component of
the "Drain of Wealth" theory articulated by
Dadabhai Naoroji. They referred to the
expenditures incurred in Britain by the
Secretary of State for India, which were
charged to the Indian exchequer.
• Statement 1 is correct. The administrative
costs of the India Office in London, which was
the headquarters of the British administration
for India, were paid for out of Indian
revenues.
• Statement 2 is correct. The salaries,
pensions, and other allowances for British
civil and military personnel who had served
or were serving in India were paid from Indian
funds.
• Statement 3 is correct. The costs of waging
wars outside India by the British, where
Indian troops were used to further British
imperial interests (e.g., in Burma,
Afghanistan, China), were often charged to
the Indian budget.
• All three were significant components of the
Home Charges, representing a unilateral
transfer of wealth from India to Britain.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q8. What was the reason for Mahatma Gandhi to
organize a satyagraha on behalf of the peasants of
Kheda?
1. The administration did not suspend the land
revenue collection in spite of a drought.
2. The administration proposed to introduce
permanent settlement in Gujarat.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
(a) 1 only.
(b) 2 only.
(c) Both 1 and 2.
(d) Neither 1 nor 2.
The correct option is (a).

•

•

•

•

The Kheda Satyagraha of 1918 was one of
Gandhi's early and significant peasant
movements in India.
Statement 1 is correct. The immediate reason
for the satyagraha was that the peasants of
the Kheda district in Gujarat were suffering
from crop failure due to a drought. According
to the government's own revenue code, if the
crop yield was less than one-fourth of the
normal, the peasants were entitled to a full
remission of the land revenue.
However, the administration refused to
acknowledge the crop failure and insisted on
collecting the full revenue. Gandhi advised
the peasants to withhold payment, leading to
the satyagraha.
Statement 2 is incorrect. The issue in Kheda
was not about the introduction of a
permanent settlement. The conflict was
specifically about the remission of land
revenue for a single year due to crop failure
under the existing Ryotwari system.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 2012

Q1. The Rowlatt Act aimed at
(a) compulsory economic support to war efforts
(b) imprisonment without trial and summary
procedures for trial
(c) suppression of the Khilafat movement
(d) imposition of restrictions on freedom of the press
The correct option is (b).
• The Rowlatt Act, officially the Anarchical and
Revolutionary Crimes Act of 1919, was a
highly repressive piece of legislation passed
by the British Indian government.
• Its primary aim was to give the government
extraordinary powers to suppress political
dissent and revolutionary activities.
• The most notorious provision of the Act was
that it authorized the government to arrest
and detain any person suspected of seditious
activities for up to two years without trial.

•

•
•

It also provided for in-camera trials with no
right of appeal, which are essentially
summary procedures for trial.
This blatant denial of basic civil liberties and
legal rights is what made the Act so infamous.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q2. Consider the following statements: The most
effective contribution made by Dadabhai Naoroji to
the cause of the Indian National Movement was that
he
1. Exposed the economic exploitation of India by
the British
2. Interpreted the ancient Indian texts and
restored self-confidence
3. Stressed the need for the eradication of all
social evils before anything else.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
(a) 1 only
(b) 2 and 3 only
(c) 1 and 3 only
(d) 1, 2, and 3
The correct option is (a).
• Statement 1 is correct. Dadabhai Naoroji's
most significant and effective contribution to
the Indian National Movement was his
pioneering economic critique of British rule.
• Through his famous “Drain Theory”, detailed
in his book Poverty and Un-British Rule in
India, he meticulously exposed the
systematic economic exploitation of India by
the British, showing how India's wealth was
being drained to Britain.
• This provided a powerful and concrete basis
for nationalist agitation.
• Statement 2 is incorrect. While leaders like
Swami Vivekananda and Dayanand Saraswati
focused on interpreting ancient texts to
restore self-confidence, this was not Naoroji's
primary contribution.
• Statement 3 is incorrect. Naoroji was a
political leader focused on political and
economic issues. While he supported social
reform, he did not stress its precedence over

•

political goals in the way that some social
reformers did.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q3. Which of the following statements is/are correct
regarding Brahmo Samaj?
1. It opposed idolatry.
2. It denied the need for a priestly class for
interpreting the religious texts.
3. It popularized the doctrine that the Vedas are
infallible.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
(a) 1 only
(b) 1 and 2 only
(c) 3 only
(d) 1, 2, and 3
The correct option is (b).
• The Brahmo Samaj, founded by Raja Ram
Mohan Roy in 1828, was a monotheistic
reform movement within Hinduism.
• Statement 1 is correct. A central tenet of the
Brahmo Samaj was its strong opposition to
idolatry (image worship) and polytheism,
advocating the worship of one formless God.
• Statement 2 is correct. The Samaj
emphasized a rational and individual
approach to religion. It denied the need for a
hereditary priestly class (like Brahmins) as
intermediaries for interpreting religious texts,
encouraging individuals to read and interpret
the scriptures for themselves.
• Statement 3 is incorrect. The doctrine that
the Vedas are infallible was popularized by
the Arya Samaj, founded by Swami Dayanand
Saraswati. The Brahmo Samaj, while
respecting the Upanishads, did not hold the
Vedas to be infallible and was open to truths
from all religions.
• Therefore, only statements 1 and 2 are
correct.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q4. The Lahore Session of the Indian National
Congress (1929) is very important in history because

1. The Congress passed a resolution demanding
complete independence.
2. The rift between the extremists and
moderates was resolved in that session.
3. A resolution was passed rejecting the twonation theory in that session.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
(a) 1 only
(b) 2 and 3
(c) 1 and 3
(d) None of the above
The correct option is (a).
• The Lahore Session of 1929, presided over by
Jawaharlal Nehru, was a pivotal moment in
the freedom struggle.
• Statement 1 is correct. The most important
outcome of this session was that the Congress
officially changed its goal from Dominion
Status to “Poorna Swaraj” or complete
independence.
• A resolution demanding this was passed, and
it was decided to celebrate January 26, 1930,
as Independence Day.
• Statement 2 is incorrect. The rift between
the Moderates and Extremists was resolved
much earlier, at the Lucknow Session in 1916.
• Statement 3 is incorrect. The two-nation
theory was not a major point of debate at the
Lahore Session in 1929. The Congress
consistently rejected this theory, but a specific
resolution against it was not the defining
feature of this particular session.
• Therefore, only statement 1 is correct.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q5. Consider the following:
1. Assessment of land revenue on the basis of
the nature of the soil and the quality of crops.
2. Use of mobile cannons in warfare.
3. Cultivation of tobacco and red chilies.
Which of the above was/were introduced into India by
the English?
(a) 1 only
(b) 1 and 2

(c) 2 and 3
(d) None
The correct option is (d).
• None of these were introduced to India by
the English.
• Statement 1 is incorrect. The system of
assessing land revenue based on soil quality
was a well-established practice in India long
before the British. It was systematized by
rulers like Sher Shah Suri and further refined
during the Mughal emperor Akbar’s reign.
• Statement 2 is incorrect. The use of mobile
cannons and artillery in warfare was
introduced to India by the Mughals, most
notably by Babur at the First Battle of Panipat
in 1526.
• Statement 3 is incorrect. The cultivation of
tobacco and red chilies was introduced to
India by the Portuguese in the 16th century.
• Therefore, none of the items were introduced
by the English.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q6. The distribution of powers between the Centre
and the States in the Indian Constitution is based on
the scheme provided in the
(a) Morley-Minto Reforms, 1909
(b) Montagu-Chelmsford Act, 1919
(c) Government of India Act, 1935
(d) Indian Independence Act, 1947
The correct option is (c).
• The constitutional framework of modern
India draws heavily from the Government of
India Act, 1935.
• A key feature of the 1935 Act was the
detailed enumeration of legislative powers,
dividing them between the central and
provincial governments.
• It introduced three legislative lists: the
Federal List (for the Centre), the Provincial
List (for the provinces), and the Concurrent
List (for both).
• This scheme of distributing powers was
adopted, with modifications, into the
Constitution of independent India.

•

Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q7. The Congress ministries resigned in the seven
provinces in 1939 because
(a) The Congress could not form ministries in the
other four provinces.
(b) Emergence of a ‘left wing’ in the Congress made
the working of the ministries impossible.
(c) There were widespread communal disturbances in
their provinces.
(d) None of the statements (a), (b), and (c) given
above is correct.
The correct option is (d).
• The reason for the resignation of the
Congress ministries in 1939 is not listed in
options (a), (b), or (c).
• The actual reason was that the Viceroy, Lord
Linlithgow, declared India to be at war with
Germany at the outbreak of World War II
without consulting any Indian political leaders
or the elected provincial governments.
• The Congress demanded a clear statement of
Britain’s war aims and a promise of
independence in return for cooperation.
• When the British government refused to
meet these demands, the Congress ministries
resigned in protest in October–November
1939.
• Since the correct reason is not provided, the
answer is (d).
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q8. Which of the following is/are the principal
feature(s) of the Government of India Act, 1919?
1. Introduction of dyarchy in the executive
government of the provinces.

2. Introduction of separate communal
electorates for Muslims.
3. Devolution of legislative authority by the
centre to the provinces.
Select the correct answer using the codes given
below:
(a) 1 only
(b) 2 and 3 only
(c) 1 and 3 only
(d) 1, 2, and 3
The correct option is (c).
• The Government of India Act, 1919, also
known as the Montagu–Chelmsford Reforms,
introduced significant changes.
• Statement 1 is correct. The most distinctive
feature of the Act was the introduction of
dyarchy (a system of dual government) in the
provinces. Provincial subjects were divided
into Reserved subjects (administered by the
Governor and his council) and Transferred
subjects (administered by the Governor with
the help of ministers responsible to the
legislative council).
• Statement 2 is incorrect. The introduction of
separate communal electorates for Muslims
was done earlier, by the Morley–Minto
Reforms of 1909. The 1919 Act did not
introduce them but extended the principle to
other communities like Sikhs, Indian
Christians, and Anglo-Indians.
• Statement 3 is correct. The Act marked the
first clear step towards federalism by
demarcating central and provincial subjects.
This devolution of legislative authority gave
the provinces the power to legislate on
subjects in the “provincial list.”
• Therefore, statements 1 and 3 are correct.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q9. During the Indian freedom struggle, the National
Social Conference was formed. What was the reason
for its formation?
(a) Different social reform groups or organizations of
Bengal region united to form a single body to discuss
the issues of larger interest and to prepare

appropriate petitions/representations to the
government.
(b) Indian National Congress did not want to include
social reforms in its deliberations and decided to form
a separate body for such a purpose.
(c) Behramji Malabari and M.G. Ranade decided to
bring together all the social reform groups of the
country under one organization.
(d) None of the statements (a), (b), and (c) given
above is correct in this context.
The correct option is (b).
• The National Social Conference was founded
in 1887 by M.G. Ranade and Raghunath Rao.
• The primary reason for its formation was that
the Indian National Congress, in its early
years, deliberately decided not to include
social reform issues in its official
deliberations.
• The early Congress leaders wanted to focus
exclusively on political issues to avoid
creating divisions within the nationalist ranks
over sensitive social questions.
• To provide a national platform for social
reform without embroiling the Congress in
controversy, the National Social Conference
was formed.
• It used to hold its annual sessions in the same
venue as the Congress, immediately after the
Congress session, acting as the social reform
wing of the nationalist movement.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q10. Which of the following parties were established
by Dr. B. R. Ambedkar?
1. The Peasants and Workers Party of India
2. All India Scheduled Castes Federation
3. The Independent Labour Party
Select the correct answer using the codes given
below:
(a) 1 and 2 only
(b) 2 and 3 only
(c) 1 and 3 only
(d) 1, 2, and 3
The correct option is (b).

•

•

•

•

•
•

Dr. B.R. Ambedkar founded several political
parties to champion the rights of the
Depressed Classes and the working class.
Statement 1 is incorrect. The Peasants and
Workers Party of India was a Marxist political
party founded in Maharashtra in 1947 by
leaders like Keshavrao Jedhe and Shankarrao
More. Dr. Ambedkar was not associated with
its formation.
Statement 2 is correct. Dr. Ambedkar
founded the All India Scheduled Castes
Federation (SCF) in 1942 as an all-India
political party exclusively for the Scheduled
Castes.
Statement 3 is correct. Dr. Ambedkar
founded the Independent Labour Party (ILP)
in 1936 to contest the 1937 provincial
elections. The party aimed to represent the
interests of the labouring classes, including
industrial workers and poor peasants, while
also fighting against the caste system.
Therefore, only statements 2 and 3 are
correct.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q11. Mahatma Gandhi undertook a fast unto death
in 1932 mainly because
(a) The Round Table Conference failed to satisfy
Indian political aspirations.
(b) Congress and the Muslim League had differences
of opinion.
(c) Ramsay MacDonald announced the Communal
Award.
(d) None of the statements (a), (b), and (c) given
above is correct in this context.
The correct option is (c).
• In August 1932, the British Prime Minister,
Ramsay MacDonald, announced the
Communal Award.
• This award granted separate electorates not
only to Muslims, Sikhs, and Christians but
also extended them to the Depressed Classes
(Dalits).

•

•

•

•

•

Mahatma Gandhi viewed this as a sinister
move to divide the Hindu community and
perpetuate untouchability.
He believed it would permanently segregate
the Depressed Classes from the rest of Hindu
society.
In protest against this provision of separate
electorates for the Depressed Classes,
Gandhi, who was imprisoned in Yerwada Jail
in Poona, began a fast unto death on
September 20, 1932.
This led to the signing of the Poona Pact
between Gandhi and Dr. Ambedkar, which
replaced separate electorates with a system
of reserved seats.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q12. With reference to the Ryotwari Settlement,
consider the following statements:
1. The rent was paid directly by the peasants to
the Government.
2. The Government gave Pattas to the Ryots.
3. The lands were surveyed and assessed before
being taxed.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
(a) 1 only
(b) 1 and 2 only
(c) 1, 2, and 3
(d) None
The correct option is (c).
• The Ryotwari Settlement was a system of
land revenue introduced by the British,
primarily in parts of the Madras and Bombay
presidencies.
• Statement 1 is correct. A key feature of this
system was the elimination of intermediaries
like zamindars. The individual cultivator, or
ryot, was recognized as the proprietor of the
land, and the land revenue (rent) was paid
directly by the peasant to the Government.
• Statement 2 is correct. Under this system, the
government issued a formal deed or title,
known as a Patta, to the ryot, which specified
the extent of their landholding and the
revenue to be paid.

•

•
•

Statement 3 is correct. A crucial part of the
Ryotwari system was that the lands were
supposed to be carefully surveyed and
assessed based on soil quality and
productivity before the revenue demand was
fixed. Although the initial assessments were
often harsh and arbitrary, the principle of
survey and assessment was central to the
system.
Since all three statements are correct, the
correct option is (c).
Hence, option (c) is correct
UPSC PYQs - 2013

Q1. With reference to Indian History, the Members
of the Constituent Assembly from the Provinces
were
(a) directly elected by the people of those Provinces
(b) nominated by the Indian National Congress and
the Muslim League
(c) elected by the Provincial Legislative Assemblies
(d) selected by the Government for their expertise in
constitutional matters
The correct option is (c).
• The members of the Constituent Assembly,
which was tasked with framing the
Constitution of India, were not chosen
through universal adult franchise.
• Instead, they were chosen through a system
of indirect election.
• The members representing the British Indian
provinces were elected by the members of
the newly formed Provincial Legislative
Assemblies.
• The voting was conducted using the method
of proportional representation by means of a
single transferable vote.
• The members representing the princely states
were to be nominated by the heads of those
states.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q2. The demand for the Tebhaga Peasant Movement
in Bengal was for
(a) the reduction of the share of the landlords from
one-half of the crop to one-third
(b) the grant of ownership of land to peasants as they
were the actual cultivators of the land
(c) the uprooting of Zamindari system and the end of
serfdom
(d) writing off all peasant debts
The correct option is (a).
• The Tebhaga Movement was a powerful
peasant agitation that occurred in Bengal in
1946–47.
• It was led by sharecroppers, known as
bargadars.
• The central demand of the movement was
encapsulated in its name, "Tebhaga", which
means "one-third".
• The sharecroppers demanded the reduction
of the share of the crop given to the
landlords (jotedars) from the traditional onehalf to a more just one-third.
• They also demanded that the harvested crop
be stored in their own granaries (khamars)
and not in the landlord's, to prevent
manipulation.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q3. The people of India agitated against the arrival of
the Simon Commission because
(a) Indians never wanted the review of the working of
the Act of 1919
(b) Simon Commission recommended the abolition of
Dyarchy (Diarchy) in the Provinces
(c) there was no Indian member in the Simon
Commission
(d) the Simon Commission suggested the partition of
the country
The correct option is (c).
• The Simon Commission was appointed by the
British government in 1927 to review the
working of the Government of India Act,
1919, and to suggest further constitutional
reforms.

•

•

•
•

•

The commission faced immediate and
widespread agitation and boycott from the
people of India upon its arrival.
The primary reason for this universal
opposition was that there was no Indian
member in the Simon Commission.
It was an all-white, seven-member
commission.
This exclusion was seen as a grave insult to
the self-respect of Indians and a denial of
their right to participate in determining their
own future.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q4. Quit India Movement was launched in response
to
(a) Cabinet Mission Plan
(b) Cripps Proposals
(c) Simon Commission Report
(d) Wavell Plan
The correct option is (b).
• The Quit India Movement was launched in
August 1942.
• Its immediate context was the failure of the
Cripps Mission.
• The Cripps Mission of March 1942 had
offered Dominion Status after the war but
failed to provide for any immediate transfer
of real power to Indians.
• The failure of the Cripps Proposals, combined
with the growing threat of a Japanese
invasion and the general frustration with
British intransigence, led Mahatma Gandhi
and the Indian National Congress to conclude

•

•

that the time had come for a final, decisive
struggle.
This led to the launch of the Quit India
Movement, demanding an immediate end to
British rule.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q5. The Ilbert Bill controversy was related to the
(a) imposition of restrictions on the Indians of certain
to carry arms by Indians
(b) imposition of restrictions on newspapers and
magazines published in Indian languages
(c) removal of disqualifications imposed on the Indian
magistrates with regard to the trial of the Europeans
(d) removal of a duty on imported cotton cloth
The correct option is (c).
• The Ilbert Bill controversy of 1883–84 was a
major political storm during the viceroyalty of
Lord Ripon.
• The controversy was related to a bill that
sought to remove the disqualifications
imposed on Indian magistrates and judges.
• At the time, Indian judges were not
permitted to preside over the criminal trials
of European subjects.
• The Ilbert Bill proposed to end this racial
discrimination in the judiciary.
• It was met with ferocious opposition from
the European community in India, who saw it
as an attack on their racial superiority.
• This "white mutiny" forced the government
to compromise, but the episode exposed the
deep-seated racism of colonial rule and
galvanized Indian nationalist sentiment.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q6. Annie Besant was
1. responsible for starting the Home Rule
Movement.
2. the founder of the Theosophical Society.
3. once the President of the Indian National
Congress.
Select the correct statement/statements using the
codes given below.
(a) 1 only

(b) 2 and 3 only
(c) 1 and 3 only
(d) 1, 2, and 3
The correct option is (c).
• Statement 1 is correct. Annie Besant was a
key figure in the Indian freedom struggle and
was responsible for starting the All-India
Home Rule League in September 1916. She
modeled it on the Irish Home Rule movement
to demand self-government for India within
the British Empire.
• Statement 2 is incorrect. Annie Besant was a
prominent leader and later the international
President of the Theosophical Society, but
she was not its founder. The Theosophical
Society was founded in New York in 1875 by
Madame H.P. Blavatsky and Colonel H.S.
Olcott.
• Statement 3 is correct. In recognition of her
immense contribution to the nationalist
cause and in protest against her internment
by the British government, Annie Besant was
elected as the President of the Indian
National Congress for its Calcutta session in
1917. She was the first woman to hold this
position.
• Therefore, statements 1 and 3 are correct.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 2014
Q1. The 1929 Session of the Indian National Congress
is of significance in the history of the Freedom
Movement because the
(a) attainment of Self-Government was declared as
the objective of the Congress
(b) attainment of Poorna Swaraj was adopted as the
goal of the Congress
(c) Non-Cooperation Movement was launched
(d) decision to participate in the Round Table
Conference in London was taken
The correct option is (b).
• The Lahore session of the Indian National
Congress in December 1929, presided over by

•

•

•

•

Jawaharlal Nehru, is a landmark event in the
freedom struggle.
Its primary significance lies in the fact that the
Congress officially changed its goal from
Dominion Status to “Poorna Swaraj”
(Complete Independence).
A resolution to this effect was passed, and it
was decided to celebrate January 26, 1930, as
the first Independence Day.
This session marked a radical shift in the
Congress's political objective and set the
stage for the Civil Disobedience Movement.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q2. The Partition of Bengal made by Lord Curzon in
1905 lasted until
(a) The First World War when Indian troops were
needed by the British and the partition was ended.
(b) King George V abrogated Curzon’s Act at the Royal
Darbar in Delhi in 1911.
(c) Gandhiji launched his Civil Disobedience
Movement.
(d) the Partition of India in 1947 when East Bengal
became East Pakistan.
The correct option is (b).
• The Partition of Bengal in 1905 by Lord
Curzon sparked a massive and sustained
protest movement, known as the Swadeshi
Movement.
• The intense and continuous agitation against
the partition forced the British government to
reconsider its decision.
• The partition was finally annulled in 1911.
• The announcement was made by King
George V at the grand Delhi Durbar held in
December 1911.
• At the same event, the decision to shift the
capital of British India from Calcutta to Delhi
was also announced.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q3. The Ghadr (Ghadar) was a
(a) revolutionary association of Indians with
headquarters at San Francisco.
(b) nationalist organization operating from Singapore.

(c) militant organization with headquarters at Berlin.
(d) communist movement for India’s freedom with
headquarters at Tashkent.
The correct option is (a).
• The Ghadar Party was a pivotal organization
in the history of the Indian revolutionary
movement abroad.
• It was a revolutionary association of Indians,
primarily Sikhs from Punjab, that was
founded in 1913.
• Its specific aim was to overthrow British rule
in India through an armed rebellion.
• The party's headquarters, known as the
Yugantar Ashram, was located in San
Francisco, USA.
• It published a weekly newspaper called “The
Ghadar”, which was circulated among Indian
expatriates worldwide to inspire
revolutionary sentiment.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q4. What was/were the object/objects of Queen
Victoria’s Proclamation (1858)?
1. To disclaim any intention to annex Indian
States
2. To place the Indian administration under the
British Crown
3. To regulate the East India Company’s trade
with India
Select the correct answer using the code given
below.
(a) 1 and 2 only
(b) 2 only
(c) 1 and 3 only
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (a).
• Queen Victoria's Proclamation of 1858 was
issued following the suppression of the
Revolt of 1857 and marked a major shift in
British policy towards India.
• Statement 1 is correct. To reassure the Indian
princes who had been alarmed by Dalhousie's
annexationist policies, the Proclamation
explicitly disclaimed any desire for further
territorial expansion and promised to respect

•

•

•

the rights, dignity, and honor of the native
princes.
Statement 2 is correct. The primary object of
the Proclamation was to announce the end of
the East India Company's rule and place the
Indian administration directly under the
authority of the British Crown. The GovernorGeneral was henceforth to be known as the
Viceroy.
Statement 3 is incorrect. The Proclamation
was about the transfer of governance, not
trade regulation. The East India Company's
trading rights had already been curtailed,
and the Act of 1858 abolished the Company
entirely.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q5. The Radcliffe Committee was appointed to
(a) solve the problem of minorities in India
(b) give effect to the Independence Bill
(c) delimit the boundaries between India and Pakistan
(d) enquire into the riots in East Bengal
The correct option is (c).
• As the partition of India became a reality in
1947, the task of dividing the provinces of
Punjab and Bengal between the two new
dominions, India and Pakistan, was
undertaken.
• The Radcliffe Committee, officially known as
the Boundary Commission, was appointed for
this purpose.
• Chaired by Sir Cyril Radcliffe, a British lawyer
with no prior experience with India, the
committee's mandate was to delimit the
boundaries based on the contiguous majority
areas of Muslims and non-Muslims.
• The lines drawn by this committee, known as
the Radcliffe Line, became the international
borders between India and Pakistan (both
West and East).
• Hence, option (c) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 2015
Q1. With reference to the Cabinet Mission, which of
the following statements is/are correct?
1. It recommended a federal government.
2. It enlarged the powers of the Indian courts.
3. It provided for more Indians in the ICS.
Select the correct answer using the code given
below.
(a) 1 only
(b) 2 and 3
(c) 1 and 3
(d) None
The correct option is (a).
• The Cabinet Mission came to India in 1946
with the primary objective of devising a
constitutional framework for the transfer of
power.
• Statement 1 is correct. The central
recommendation of the Cabinet Mission Plan
was the formation of a Union of India,
comprising both British India and the Princely
States. This Union would have a federal
government responsible for Foreign Affairs,
Defence, and Communications.
• Statement 2 is incorrect. The Plan was
concerned with the future constitution and
interim government, not with enlarging the
powers of the Indian courts.
• Statement 3 is incorrect. The issue of
increasing Indians in the ICS was a longstanding demand but not a specific provision
of the Cabinet Mission Plan.
• Therefore, only statement 1 is correct.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q2. Who of the following was/were economic
critic/critics of colonialism in India?
1. Dadabhai Naoroji
2. G. Subramania Iyer
3. R. C. Dutt
Select the correct answer using the code given
below.
(a) 1 only
(b) 1 and 2 only

(c) 2 and 3 only
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (d).
• A key feature of the early phase of Indian
nationalism was the development of a
powerful economic critique of British
colonialism.
• Dadabhai Naoroji: His Drain of Wealth
theory, in Poverty and Un-British Rule in India,
showed how India’s resources were siphoned
off to Britain.
• G. Subramania Iyer: A prominent journalist
and founder of The Hindu, he criticized British
economic policies, highlighting poverty,
taxation, and the drain of wealth.
• R. C. Dutt: A retired ICS officer and economic
historian, in The Economic History of India,
gave a comprehensive critique of British
policies on agriculture and industry.
• All three were pioneers in developing and
popularizing the economic critique of
colonialism.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q3. Which one of the following movements has
contributed to a split in the Indian National Congress
resulting in the emergence of ‘moderates’ and
‘extremists’?
(a) Swadeshi Movement
(b) Quit India Movement
(c) Non-Cooperation Movement
(d) Civil Disobedience Movement
The correct option is (a).
• The ideological differences between the
Moderates and Extremists had been growing,
but they came to a head during the Swadeshi
Movement (1905–1908).
• The movement was launched in response to
the Partition of Bengal.
• The Extremists, led by Tilak, Lajpat Rai, and
Bipin Chandra Pal, wanted to extend the
movement beyond Bengal and expand
boycott to all association with the
government.

•
•
•

The Moderates opposed these radical
methods.
This conflict escalated, culminating in the
historic split at the Surat Session (1907).
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q4. With reference to Rowlatt Satyagraha, which of
the following statements is/are correct?
1. The Rowlatt Act was based on the
recommendations of the ‘Sedition
Committee’.
2. In Rowlatt Satyagraha, Gandhiji tried to use
the Home Rule League.
3. Demonstrations against the Simon
Commission coincided with the Rowlatt
Satyagraha.
Select the correct answer using the code given
below.
(a) 1 only
(b) 1 and 2 only
(c) 2 and 3 only
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (b).
• Statement 1 is correct. The Rowlatt Act
(Anarchical and Revolutionary Crimes Act,
1919) was based on the Sedition Committee
Report chaired by Sir Sidney Rowlatt.
• Statement 2 is correct. Gandhi utilized Home
Rule Leagues (founded by Annie Besant and
Tilak) as a network to organize the
nationwide protest.
• Statement 3 is incorrect. The Rowlatt
Satyagraha (1919) and Simon Commission
protests (1928) were nearly a decade apart.
• Therefore, only statements 1 and 2 are
correct.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q5. With reference to the Congress Socialist Party,
consider the following statements:
1. It advocated the boycott of British goods and
evasion of taxes.
2. It wanted to establish the dictatorship of the
proletariat.

3. It advocated separate electorates for
minorities and oppressed classes.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
(a) 1 and 2 only
(b) 3 only
(c) 1, 2 and 3
(d) None
The correct option is (d).
• The Congress Socialist Party (CSP) was
formed in 1934 as a socialist caucus within
Congress.
• Statement 1 is incorrect. CSP participated in
boycott/tax evasion as part of Congress
programmes, not as its distinct agenda.
• Statement 2 is incorrect. The CSP was
influenced by Marxism but did not advocate
dictatorship of the proletariat; that was the
CPI’s line.
• Statement 3 is incorrect. CSP and Congress
stood for joint electorates and opposed
separate electorates.
• Since all three statements are incorrect,
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q6. The Government of India Act of 1919 clearly
defined
(a) the separation of power between the judiciary and
the legislature
(b) the jurisdiction of the central and provincial
governments
(c) the powers of the Secretary of State for India and
the Viceroy
(d) None of the above
The correct option is (b).
• A key feature of the Government of India Act,
1919 was the devolution of power.
• It clearly defined the jurisdiction of the
central and provincial governments by
demarcating legislative subjects.
• It introduced two lists: Central List and
Provincial List, each with distinct subjects.
• This was a significant constitutional
development, laying the foundation of
federalism.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q7. Who of the following organized a march on the
Tanjore coast to break the Salt Law in April 1930?
(a) V. O. Chidambaram Pillai
(b) C. Rajagopalachari
(c) K. Kamaraj
(d) Annie Besant
The correct option is (b).
• As part of the Civil Disobedience Movement,
similar salt marches took place nationwide.
• In South India, C. Rajagopalachari (Rajaji) led
a march from Trichinopoly to Vedaranyam on
the Tanjore coast in April 1930.
• At Vedaranyam, they broke the Salt Law by
making salt from seawater.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q8. Consider the following statements:
1. The first woman President of the Indian

National Congress was Badruddin Tyabji.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
(a) 1 only
(b) 2 only
(c) Both 1 and 2
(d) Neither 1 nor 2
The correct option is (b).
• Statement 1 is incorrect. The first woman
President of INC was Annie Besant (1917).
Sarojini Naidu was the first Indian woman, in
1925.
• Statement 2 is correct. Badruddin Tyabji was
the first Muslim President of INC, presiding
over the Madras Session (1887).
• Therefore, only statement 2 is correct.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 2016
Q1. The ‘Swadeshi’ and ‘Boycott’ were adopted as
methods of struggle for the first time during the
(a) Agitation against the Partition of Bengal
(b) Home Rule Movement
(c) Non-Cooperation Movement
(d) visit of the Simon Commission to India

The correct option is (a).
• The methods of ‘Swadeshi’ (promotion of
indigenous goods) and ‘Boycott’ (of foreign,
particularly British goods) were adopted as
the principal strategies of mass protest for
the first time during the agitation against the
Partition of Bengal.
• When the British government announced the
partition in July 1905, Indian nationalist
leaders, particularly in Bengal, responded by
calling for a boycott of British products and
the promotion of Indian-made goods.
• The formal proclamation of the Swadeshi
Movement was made on August 7, 1905, in
Calcutta Townhall.
• These methods were later adopted and
expanded in subsequent nationalist
movements, but their first large-scale
application was during the anti-partition
agitation.
• Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q2. Satya Shodhak Samaj organized
(a) a movement for the upliftment of tribals in Bihar
(b) a temple-entry movement in Gujarat
(c) an anti-caste movement in Maharashtra
(d) a peasant movement in Punjab
The correct option is (c).
• Satya Shodhak Samaj (Truth-Seekers'
Society) was founded by Jyotiba Phule in
Pune, Maharashtra, in 1873.
• Its primary objective was to fight against
social injustice and inequality.
• It organized a powerful anti-caste movement,
aimed at liberating the lower castes (Shudras
and Ati-Shudras) from Brahminical
oppression.
• The movement promoted education for
lower castes and women, challenged the
authority of Brahmin priests, and advocated
social equality and rationalism.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q3. The Montague-Chelmsford Proposals were
related to
(a) social reforms
(b) educational reforms
(c) reforms in police administration
(d) constitutional reforms
The correct option is (d).
• The Montagu-Chelmsford Proposals
(Montford Report) were published in 1918,
named after Edwin Montagu (Secretary of
State for India) and Lord Chelmsford
(Viceroy).
• These proposals were related to
constitutional reforms for British India.
• They outlined a new framework for
governance with the goal of “gradual
development of self-governing institutions”.
• Key recommendations included the
introduction of Dyarchy in the provinces and
bicameral legislature at the Centre.
• These formed the basis of the Government of
India Act, 1919.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q4. Consider the following:
1. Calcutta Unitarian Committee
2. Tabernacle of New Dispensation
3. Indian Reform Association
Keshab Chandra Sen is associated with the
establishment of which of the above?
(a) 1 and 3 only
(b) 2 and 3 only
(c) 3 only
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (b).

•

•

•

•
•

1. Calcutta Unitarian Committee: Founded in
1821 by Raja Ram Mohan Roy, William
Adam, and Dwarkanath Tagore. Keshab
Chandra Sen was not associated with it.
2. Tabernacle of New Dispensation: After
splitting from the Brahmo Samaj, Keshab
Chandra Sen founded the Brahmo Samaj of
India and later proclaimed a new universal
religion (Nava Vidhan). In 1868, he laid the
foundation of the Tabernacle of New
Dispensation.
3. Indian Reform Association: Founded by
Keshab Chandra Sen in 1870, to implement
social reform ideas such as women’s
education, temperance, and abolition of child
marriage.
Therefore, he is associated with 2 and 3.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q5. What was the main reason for the split in the
Indian National Congress at Surat in 1907?
(a) Introduction of communalism into Indian politics
by Lord Minto.
(b) Extremists’ lack of faith in the capacity of the
moderates to negotiate with the British Government.
(c) Foundation of Muslim League.
(d) Aurobindo Ghosh’s inability to be elected as the
President of the Indian National Congress.
The correct option is (b).
• The Surat Split (1907) resulted from
ideological differences between Moderates
and Extremists in the INC.
• The main reason was the Extremists’ lack of
faith in the Moderates’ methods (petitioning
and constitutional agitation) to achieve
meaningful concessions.
• The Extremists, led by Bal Gangadhar Tilak,
advocated radical methods like boycott and
passive resistance, viewing the Moderates’
approach as weak.
• This fundamental disagreement over strategy
and goals led to the split at Surat.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q6. The plan of Sir Stafford Cripps envisaged that
after the Second World War
(a) India should be granted complete independence.
(b) India should be partitioned into two before
granting independence.
(c) India should be made a republic with the condition
that she will join the Commonwealth.
(d) India should be given Dominion status.
The correct option is (d).
• The Cripps Mission (1942) came to India to
secure Indian cooperation in the war effort.
• It proposed that after the war, India would
be given Dominion status.
• This Dominion would be free to decide its
relationship with the British Commonwealth.
• It did not offer complete independence, one
of the reasons why Congress rejected it.
• It also allowed for the possibility of partition,
as provinces could choose not to join the new
union.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 2017
Q1. With reference to the Indian freedom struggle,
consider the following events:
1. Mutiny in Royal Indian Navy
2. Quit India Movement launched
3. Second Round Table Conference
What is the correct chronological sequence of the
above events?
(a) 1-2-3
(b) 2-1-3
(c) 3-2-1
(d) 3-1-2
The correct option is (c).
• To arrange these significant events in
chronological order:
o 3. Second Round Table Conference:
Held in London, September–
December 1931, attended by
Mahatma Gandhi as the sole
Congress representative.

o

•
•

2. Quit India Movement launched:
Launched on August 8, 1942 by the
Indian National Congress.
o 1. Mutiny in Royal Indian Navy:
Began in Bombay on February 18,
1946.
Therefore, the correct chronological sequence

Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q2. In the context of Indian history, the principle of
‘Dyarchy (diarchy)’ refers to
(a) Division of the central legislature into two houses.
(b) Introduction of double government i.e., Central
and State governments.
(c) Having two sets of rulers; one in London and
another in Delhi.
(d) Division of the subjects delegated to the provinces
into two categories.
The correct option is (d).
• The principle of ‘Dyarchy’ (rule by two) was a
key feature of the Government of India Act,
1919.
• It referred to the division of subjects
delegated to the provinces into two
categories:
o Reserved Subjects: Finance, law and
order, irrigation — administered by
the Governor and his executive
council.
o Transferred Subjects: Education,
public health, local self-government
— administered by Indian ministers
responsible to the legislature.
• This dual governance system was complex
and unworkable at the provincial level.
• Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q3. Consider the following pairs:
1. Radhakanta Deb — First President of the
British Indian Association
2. Gazulu Lakshminarasu Chetty — Founder of
the Madras Mahajana Sabha
3. Surendranath Banerjee — Founder of the
Indian Association

Which of the above pairs is/are correctly matched?
(a) 1 only
(b) 1 and 3 only
(c) 2 and 3 only
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (b).
• Pair 1 is correct: The British Indian
Association was formed in 1851, and Raja
Radhakanta Deb was its first President.
• Pair 2 is incorrect: The Madras Mahajana
Sabha was founded in 1884 by M.
Veeraraghavachariar, G. Subramania Iyer,
and P. Ananda Charlu, not Gazulu
Lakshminarasu Chetty.
• Pair 3 is correct: The Indian Association was
founded in 1876 in Calcutta by Surendranath
Banerjee and Ananda Mohan Bose.
• Therefore, pairs 1 and 3 are correctly
matched.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q4. The object of the Butler Committee of 1927 was
to
(a) Define the jurisdiction of the Central and Provincial
Governments.
(b) Define the powers of the Secretary of State for
India.
(c) Impose censorship on the national press.
(d) Improve the relationship between the
Government of India and the Indian States.
The correct option is (d).
• The Butler Committee (Indian States
Committee) was appointed in 1927, chaired
by Sir Harcourt Butler.
• Its primary object was to investigate and
clarify the relationship between the British
Crown (paramount power) and the Indian
Princely States.
• It examined financial and economic relations
and made recommendations to improve
relations between the Government of India
and the Indian States.
• Its report reaffirmed paramountcy — the
relationship was between the States and the
Crown, not the Government of India.

•

Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q5. Who among the following was/were associated
with the introduction of the Ryotwari Settlement in
India during British rule?
1. Lord Cornwallis
2. Alexander Read
3. Thomas Munro
Select the correct answer using the code given
below:
(a) 1 only
(b) 1 and 3 only
(c) 2 and 3 only
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (c).
• 1. Lord Cornwallis: Associated with
Permanent Settlement, not Ryotwari.
• 2. Alexander Read: Pioneered the Ryotwari
system in Baramahal district in 1792,
collecting revenue directly from ryots.
• 3. Thomas Munro: Worked with Read, later as
Governor of Madras, extended the Ryotwari
system widely.
• Therefore, Alexander Read and Thomas
Munro were key figures.
• Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q6. Consider the following statements:
1. The Factories Act, 1881 was passed with a
view to fix the wages of industrial workers
and to allow the workers to form trade
unions.
2. N.M. Lokhande was a pioneer in organizing
the labour movement in British India.
Which of the above statements is/are correct?
(a) 1 only
(b) 2 only
(c) Both 1 and 2
(d) Neither 1 nor 2
The correct option is (b).
• Statement 1 is incorrect: The Factories Act,
1881 only regulated child labour — it did not
fix wages or allow trade unions. Trade Union
Act came in 1926.

•

•
•

Statement 2 is correct: Narayan Meghaji
Lokhande is known as the “Father of the
Trade Union Movement in India.”
o In the 1880s, he organized mill
workers in Bombay.
o Founded the Bombay Mill-Hands'
Association (1890), the first labour
union in India.
Therefore, only statement 2 is correct.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 2018

Q1. Which one of the following statements does not
apply to the system of Subsidiary Alliance introduced
by Lord Wellesley?
(a) To maintain a large standing army at others
expense
(b) To keep India safe from Napoleonic danger
(c) To secure a fixed income for the Company
(d) To establish British paramountcy over the Indian
States
The correct option is (c).
• The Subsidiary Alliance system aimed to
establish British paramountcy and ensure
military and political subservience of Indian
States to the East India Company.
• Securing a fixed income for the Company was
not its objective.
• Indian states had to maintain British troops at
their expense and post British residents in
their courts.
• They were forbidden to employ Europeans
without permission and to ally with other
powers.
• If rulers failed to pay maintenance, territory
was ceded.
• Awadh was the first to come under it.
• It was a ‘Non-Intervention Policy’ tool to
extend British control indirectly.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q2. Economically, one of the results of the British
rule in India in the 19th century was the:

(a) Increase in the export of Indian handicrafts
(b) Growth in the number of Indian owned factories
(c) Commercialization of Indian agriculture
(d) Rapid increase in the urban population
The correct option is (c).
• British rule led to the commercialization of
Indian agriculture, introducing cash crops
such as opium, indigo, cotton, and jute.
• Indian industries and handicrafts declined
due to influx of British goods.
• There was no industrial growth or rapid
urbanization.
• The economy was reoriented to serve British
industrial needs, exporting raw materials and
importing finished goods.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q3. The staple commodities of export by the English
East Indian Company from Bengal in the middle of
the 18th century were
(a) Raw cotton, oil-seeds and opium
(b) Sugar, salt, zinc and lead
(c) Copper, silver, gold, spices and tea
(d) Cotton, silk, saltpeter and opium
The correct option is (d).
• The East India Company traded mainly in
cotton, silk, opium, and saltpetre from
Bengal.
• These were high-demand export
commodities to Europe and China.
• The trade in tea and saltpetre later became
especially significant.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q4. In the Federation established by the Government
of India Act of 1935, residuary powers were given to
the:
(a) Federal Legislature
(b) Governor General
(c) Provincial Legislature
(d) Provincial Governors
The correct option is (b).
• The Government of India Act, 1935 divided
subjects into Federal (59 items), Provincial
(54), and Concurrent (36) lists.
• Residuary powers were not vested in either
legislature.
• They were given to the Governor-General,
who could authorize either legislature to
legislate on such matters.
• The Act also:
o Abolished dyarchy in provinces,
introduced provincial autonomy.
o Extended communal representation
to depressed classes and women.
o Created bicameral legislatures in six
provinces.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q5. After the Santhal Uprising subsided, what
was/were the measure/measures taken by the
colonial government?
1. The territories called ‘Santhal Paraganas’ were
created.

2. It became illegal for a Santhal to transfer land
to a non-Santhal.
Select the correct answer using the code given below:
(a) 1 only
(b) 2 only
(c) Both 1 and 2
(d) Neither 1 nor 2
The correct option is (c).
• The Santhal Uprising (1854–56) led to key
reforms.
• The British created a separate district, Santhal
Parganas, to pacify the Santhals.
• Laws were enacted making it illegal for a
Santhal to transfer land to non-Santhals.
• These measures aimed to protect tribal land
and reduce exploitation by outsiders (dikus).
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q6. Which one of the following is a very significant
aspect of the Champaran Satyagraha?
(a) Active all-India participation of lawyers, students
and women in the National Movement
(b) Active involvement of Dalit and Tribal communities
of India in the National Movement
(c) Joining of peasant unrest to India’s National
Movement
(d) Drastic decrease in the cultivation of plantation
crops and commercial crops
The correct option is (c).
• Champaran Satyagraha (1917) was Gandhi’s
first experiment with Satyagraha in India.
• It linked peasant struggles with the Indian
National Movement.
• Farmers were forced under the Tinkathia
system to grow indigo on part of their land.
• Gandhi investigated their grievances and led
non-violent resistance.
• Associated leaders: Rajendra Prasad, Mazharul-Haq, J.B. Kripalani, Mahadeo Desai.
• It marked the beginning of the Gandhian era
in India’s freedom struggle.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q7. In 1920, which of the following changed its name
to “Swarajya Sabha”?

(a) All India Home Rule League
(b) Hindu Mahasabha
(c) South Indian Liberal Federation
(d) The Servants of India Society
The correct option is (a).
• The Home Rule League Movement (1916)
sought self-rule through education and
mobilization.
• Tilak founded his league in Belgaum (April
1916) and Annie Besant in Madras
(September 1916).
• The All India Home Rule League was renamed
Swarajya Sabha in 1920.
• It prepared the ground for mass participation
under Gandhi’s leadership.
• Key leaders: Motilal Nehru, C.R. Das, Lala
Lajpat Rai, Jinnah, Malaviya.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q8. Regarding Wood’s Dispatch, which of the
following statements are true?
1. Grants-in-Aid system was introduced.
2. Establishment of universities was
recommended.
3. English as a medium of instruction at all levels
of education was recommended.
Select the correct answer using the code given below:
(a) 1 and 2 only
(b) 2 and 3 only
(c) 1 and 3 only
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (a).
• Wood’s Dispatch (1854) is called the “Magna
Carta of English Education in India.”
• It introduced the Grants-in-Aid system to
encourage private education.
• It recommended the establishment of
universities in Calcutta, Bombay, and
Madras.
• It proposed English for higher education and
vernacular languages at school level.
• Therefore, only statements 1 and 2 are
correct.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q9. Which of the following led to the introduction of
English Education in India?
1. Charter Act of 1813
2. General Committee of Public Instruction, 1823
3. Orientalist and Anglicist controversy
Select the correct answer using the code given below:
(a) 1 and 2 only
(b) 2 only
(c) 1 and 3 only
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (d).
• Charter Act of 1813: Allowed Christian
missionaries to propagate English education
and preach Christianity.
• General Committee of Public Instruction
(1823): Established to manage education
funds and later led to Macaulay’s Minute
(1835) advocating English education.
• Orientalist–Anglicist controversy: Debate
over medium of instruction — English vs
vernacular languages.
• Together, these developments introduced
English education in India.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q11. He wrote biographies of Mazzini, Garibaldi,

(c) Lala Lajpat Rai
(d) Motilal Nehru
The correct option is (c).

• He was INC President (1920) and founded
Servants of the People Society (1921).
• Stayed in the United States (1917); wrote
several books including “Unhappy India”

UPSC PYQs - 2019
Q1. Which one of the following groups of plants was
domesticated in the ‘New World’ and introduced
into the ‘Old World’?
(a) Tobacco, cocoa and rubber
(b) Tobacco, cotton and rubber
(c) Cotton, coffee and sugarcane
(d) Rubber, coffee and wheat
The correct option is (a).
• Tobacco, cocoa, and rubber are the groups of
plants that were domesticated in the ‘New
World’ and introduced into the ‘Old World’
through the European explorers in the late
medieval or early modern era.
• The Columbian Exchange:
o The Columbian exchange, also known
as the Columbian interchange,
named for Christopher Columbus,
was the widespread transfer of
plants, animals, culture, human
populations, technology, diseases,
and ideas between the Americas,
West Africa, and the Old World in the
15th and 16th centuries.
o It also relates to European
colonization and trade following
Christopher Columbus’s 1492 voyage.
Invasive species, including
communicable diseases, were a byproduct of the Exchange.

o

Old World to New World: Coffee,
wheat, cotton, barley, rice, sugarcane
and sugar beet, etc.
o New World to Old World: avocado,
cashew, cocoa bean, potato, maize,
rubber, tobacco, etc.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q2. Consider the following statements about ‘the
Charter Act of 1813’:
• It ended the trade monopoly of the East India
Company in India except for trade in tea and
trade with China.
• It asserted the sovereignty of the British
Crown over the Indian territories held by the
company.
• The revenues of India were now controlled by
the British Parliament.
Which of the statements given above are correct?
(a) 1 and 2 only
(b) 2 and 3 only
(c) 1 and 3 only
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (a).
• CHARTER ACT OF 1813:
o Company’s territories in India came
under the Sovereignty of the British
Government.
o Taxes could be levied against citizens
by the local They could punish people
for not paying taxes.
o It ended the East India Company’s
monopoly on trading with India.
o The company’s rule was extended for
another 20 years. The trade
monopoly of their country was ended
except for the trade in tea, opium,
and with China.
o Giving missionaries permission to
travel to India was one of the key
components of this law.
• PITT’S INDIA ACT OF 1784:
o Pitt’s India Act, 1784 extended the
control of the British Government

over the Company’s affairs and its
administration in India.
o The act was significant for two
reasons:
▪ The Company’s possessions in
India were officially referred
to as “British possessions in
India” for the first time.
▪ The Company’s activities and
administration in India were
placed under the total control
of the British Government.
• Statement 1 is correct: The Charter Act 1813
ended the trade monopoly of the East India
Company except for the trade in tea, opium,
and with China.
• Statement 2 is correct: Company’s territories
in India came under the Sovereignty of the
British Government through this Act.
• Statement 3 is incorrect: The revenue of
India was controlled by the British parliament
through Pitt’s India Act 1784.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q3. With reference to Swadeshi Movement consider
the following statements:
• It contributed to the revival of the indigenous
artisan crafts and industries.
• The National Council of Education was
established as a part of the Swadeshi
Movement.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
(a) 1 only
(b) 2 only
(c) Both 1 and 2
(d) Neither 1 nor 2
The correct option is (c).
• Swadeshi and Boycott Movements:
o There were large-scale protests
against the partition of Bengal. Later,
the movement spread to other
regions of the nation:
o The Boycott Resolution was passed at
the Calcutta Town Hall on August 7,

1905, formally launching the
Swadeshi Movement.
o Partition came into force: October
16, 1905.
o There were also new means of protest
like processions, public meetings, and
boycotts of imported goods.
o Imaginative use of Traditional
Festivals, Melas: Tilak’s Ganapati and
Shivaji festivals became a medium of
swadeshi.
• Statement 1 is correct: Importance to SelfReliance (Atma Shakti): Emphasis was placed
on honour, social and economic regeneration
of the villages. Swadeshi enterprises:
Swadeshi textile mills, banks, etc, were set up.

Ghosh as its Principal – inspired by
Tagore’s
o The National Council of Education
(1906) was set up to organise

medium.

training.

Amar Sonar Bangla, Subramania Bharati
wrote Swadesha Geetham.
• Corps of Volunteers or ‘Samitis’: they
generated political consciousness among the
masses.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q4. With reference to the British colonial rule in
India, consider the following statements:
• Mahatma Gandhi was instrumental in the

• In Lord Chelmsford’s War Conference’,

War.
• Consequent upon the breaking of the Salt Law

abolished in the British Empire. He also

resolution passed in the INC meeting
condemning the indentured labour system in

of salt law. But this did not deter the
satyagrahis who continued the movement.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 2020
Q1. With reference to the history of India, consider
the following pairs:
1.Aurang – In-charge of treasury of the State
2.Banian – Indian agent of the East India Company
3.Mirasidar – Designated revenue payer to the State
Which of the pairs given above is/are correctly
matched?
(a) 1 and 2 only
(b) 2 and 3 only
(c) 3 only
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (b).
• Pair 1 is incorrect: Aurang is a Persian term
referring to a warehouse where goods are
collected before being sold.
• Pair 2 is correct: The word Bania (also Vania)
is derived from the Sanskrit vanij, meaning ‘a
merchant’. The banian acted as an agent for
individual East India Company managers and
performed a range of functions including,
bookkeeping, interpreting, and brokerage, as
well as managing his household and personal
business activities. Banians served as
middlemen for European merchants in Bengal
during the British era.
• Pair 3 is correct: Mirasdar in Urdu means
hereditary landowner, co-proprietor and in
Persian, miras is inheritance, estate. Under
the Ryotwari settlement system in Southern
India, the East India Company recognized
mirasdars as the only proprietors of land,
dismissing tenants’ rights completely. They
were prohibited by law from selling their land
but it can be leased to tenants for a nominal
fee in return for its cultivation. They acted as
a designated revenue payer of the state.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q2. Which of the following statements correctly
explain the impact of the Industrial Revolution on
India during the first half of the nineteenth century?
(a) Indian handicrafts were ruined.
(b) Machines were introduced in the Indian textile
industry in large numbers.
(c) Railway lines were laid in many parts of the
country.
(d) Heavy duties were imposed on the imports of
British manufactures.
The correct option is (a).
• Declining and deindustrialisation of the
Indian traditional cottage and handicraft
industry was a direct effect of the industrial
revolution in England along with the
colonisation of India.
• The Industrial Revolution brought severe
consequences to Indian society.
• The fall and destruction of the urban and
rural handicraft industry was one of the most
significant effects of British Rule.
• The Industrial Revolution in England also
affected the textile industries in India in
many ways, such as: English textiles were
now posing a serious threat to Indian textiles
in the European and American markets.
• Textiles from India were subject to high taxes
in Britain.
• The African markets were successfully taken
over by cheap, machine-made textiles from
Britain.
• By the 1830s, inexpensive British
manufactured products had inundated the
Indian marketplaces. The Indian textile
industries suffered as a result of them being
less expensive than Indian textiles.
• Following the Industrial Revolution, Britain
began making textiles by a machine that were
considerably more affordable and of higher
quality than conventional Indian textile.
• The start of the industrial revolution had a
negative impact on the Indian handicraft in
India.
• Additionally, the decline of the kings,
chieftains, and zamindars who served as the

employers of these masters of handicrafts led
to the steady expansion of British dominion in
India through outright battles with the rulers,
which resulted in the suffering of handicrafts
in India.
• India became both a raw material supplier
and a finished goods market. India, a country
based on agriculture, became an economic
colony.
• The British items were inexpensive and only
required a little amount of duty, while the
Indian goods were subject to heavy import
duties.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q3. Indigo cultivation in India declined by the
beginning of the 20th century because of
(a) peasant resistance to the oppressive conduct of
planters
(b) its unprofitability in the world market because of
new inventions
(c) national leaders’ opposition to the cultivation of
indigo
(d) Government control over the planters
The correct option is (b).
• Indigo cultivation in India declined by the
beginning of the 20th century because of new
inventions of improved versions of chemical
colours that substitute indigo, thus the
production of indigo became unprofitable in
the world market.
• Indigo Revolt 1856-57:
o Area: Bengal
o Leaders: Bishnu Charan Biswas and
Diagambar Biswas
o The great worth of indigo as a
commerce commodity led to its
frequent usage as the nickname
“blue”. It gives the materials a blue
tint.
o They started losing their land due to
poor output and they started fall into
debt trap.
o India’s first peasant strike resulted in
the compelled planting of indigo since

the trade in it was profitable because
blue dye was in high demand.
o During their strike, peasants refused
to produce indigo or sign. The strike
expanded to other parts of Bengal,
and other zamindars supported the
uprisings. A commission was
established in their favour.
o Intelligentsia helped to make it a
powerful movement.
o Harsh Chandra Mukherjee (editor
Hindu Patriot), Dinabandhu Mitra
play: Neel Darpan.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q4. With reference to the history of India, “Ulgulan”
or the Great Tumult is the description of which of the
following events?
(a) The Revolt of 1857
(b) The Mappila Rebellion of 1921
(c) The Indigo Revolt of 1859 – 60
(d) Birsa Munda’s Revolt of 1899-1900
The correct option is (d).
• Munda Rebellion:
o It is one of the prominent 19th
century tribal rebellions in the
subcontinent. In the years 1899–
1900, Birsa Munda served as the
movement’s leader in the south of the
Chotanagpur region.
o The Ulgulan, also known as “the
Great Tumult,” was an insurrection
that aimed to establish Munda Raj or
Munda authority in this area.
• Reason of revolt:
o In 1874, the British replaced the
traditional Khuntkari system of
Munda tribals with the zamindari
system.
o The introduction of the zamindari
system created the classes of
zamindars (landlords) and ryots
(tenants).
o This led to the monetisation of the
economy, the tribals had to depend

on cash for paying the rent and for
meeting their daily needs.
o This situation left them with no other
option but to depend on the moneylenders, who used to lend money at
an exorbitant rate.
• Birsa Munda:
o A tribal liberation warrior from the
Munda tribe, Birsa Munda is revered
as a folk hero.
o He was a spearhead behind the
Ulgulan movement that arose in the
Chotanagpur region of Bihar and
Jharkhand belt in the 19th century
under the British.
o He is often referred to as the ‘Dharti
Abba’ or the Earth Father.
o Birsa Munda organised masses to
stop paying debts/interest to
moneylenders and taxes to the
British. By this, he led a revolt to bring
an end to Victorian rule and the
establishment of Munda Rule in
Jharkhand (Chotanagpur region).
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q5. In the context of Indian history, the Rakhmabai
case of 1884 revolved around:
1. women’s right to gain education
2. age of consent
3. restitution of conjugal rights
Select the correct answer using the code given below:
(a) 1 and 2 only
(b) 2 and 3 only
(c) 1 and 3 only
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (b).
• Dr. Rakhmabai (1864–1955):
o Rakhmabai was an Indian physician
and a feminist. Apart from being one
of the first practising women doctors
in colonial India, she was also involved
in a landmark legal case involving her
marriage as a child bride between
1884 and 1888.

o

At the young age of eleven, she was
married to Dadaji Bhikaji, who was
then nineteen. Bhikaji petitioned the
court in 1885, 12 years after they had
been married, asking for the
“restitution of conjugal rights.”
o Behramji Malabari and Pandita
Ramabai established the Rakhmabai
Defense Committee to stand up for
her.
o The case lasted for 4 years until Dadji
was compensated in 1888 out of
court.
o The case was instrumental in the
development of the Age of Consent
Act in stipulating minimum age of
consent to be 12 years however no
minimum age of marriage was
introduced.
• Statement 1 is incorrect: The Rakhmabai case
did not involve the women’s right to gain
education.
• Statement 2 is correct: The Rakhmabai case
of 1884 was instrumental in the drafting of
the Age of Consent Act in 1891.
• Statement 3 is correct: Dadaji filed a petition
seeking restitution of conjugal rights.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q6. The Gandhi-Irwin Pact included which of the
following?
1. Invitation to Congress to participate in the
Round Table Conference
2. Withdrawal of Ordinances promulgated in
connection with the Civil Disobedience
Movement
3. Acceptance of Gandhi’s suggestion for enquiry
into police excesses.
4. Release of only those prisoners who were not
charged with violence.
Select the correct answer using the code given below:
(a) 1 only
(b) 1, 2 and 4 only
(c) 3 only

(d) 2, 3 and 4 only
The correct option is (b).
• Statement 1 is correct: The Gandhi–Irwin
Pact was a political agreement signed by
Mahatma Gandhi and Lord Irwin, Viceroy of
India, on 5 March 1931 before the Second
Round Table Conference in London, where
the Indian National Congress was invited to
participate.
• Statement 2 is correct: Withdrawal of all
ordinances issued by the Government of India
imposing curbs on the activities of the Indian
National Congress was agreed by the Viceroy.
• Statement 3 is incorrect: The demand for a
public enquiry of excesses committed by
police during the civil disobedience
movement was rejected by the Viceroy.
• Statement 4 is correct: Release of all political
prisoners, except those guilty of violence was
also agreed.
• Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q7. Wellesley established the Fort William College at
Calcutta because:
(a) he was asked by the Board of Directors at London
to do so
(b) he wanted to revive interest in oriental learning in
India
(c) he wanted to provide William Carey and his
associates with employment
(d) he wanted to train British civilians for
administrative purpose in India
The correct option is (d).
• Fort William College (1800):
o Fort William College was founded on
10 July 1800 in Calcutta, British India.
o Fort William College was set up by
Lord Richard Wellesley in 1800.
o It was set up for the training of civil
servants of the Company in languages
and customs of Indians.
o The court of directors of the British
East India Company was not in
support of a training college in
Kolkata.

o

Then it got closed in 1802 and a
separate College was established in
1807 in England.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q8. With reference to the book “Desher Katha”
written by Sakharam Ganesh Deuskar during the
freedom struggle, consider the following statement:
1. It warned against the Colonial States hypnotic
conquest of the mind.
2. It inspired the performance of swadeshi street
plays and folk songs.
3. The use of ‘desh’ by Deuskar was in the
specific context of the region of Bengal.
Which of the statements given above are correct?
(a) 1 and 2 only
(b) 2 and 3 only
(c) 1 and 3 only
(d) 1, 2 and 3
The correct option is (a).
• Statement 1 and 2 are correct: In
overwhelming detail, the book “Desher
Katha” summarised the work of M. G. Ranade
and Naoroji in a popular idiom and warned in
its concluding chapter against the colonial
state’s “hypnotic conquest of the mind”.
“Desher Katha” had an immense repercussion
in Bengal, captured the mind of young Bengal
and assisted more than anything else in the
preparation of the Swadeshi Movement.
• Statement 3 is incorrect: Deuskar uses the
word ‘desh’ in the context of the whole
country, not just Bengal.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q9. The Vital-Vidhvansak, the first monthly journal
to have the untouchable people as its target
audience was published by:
(a) Gopal Baba Walangkar
(b) Jyotiba Phule
(c) Mohandas Karamchand Gandhi
(d) Bhimrao Ramji Ambedkar
The correct option is (a).
• Gopal Baba Walangkar (1840-1900):

o

He was also known as Gopal Krishna,
born into a family of the untouchable
Mahar caste in Maharashtra.
o Gopal Baba Walangkar is an early
example of a social activist working to
untrammel the untouchable people
of India from their historic socioeconomic oppression and he is
generally considered to be the
pioneer of that movement.
o He also developed a racial theory to
explain the oppression and also
published the first journal targeted at
the untouchable people.
o The first publication to have the
untouchable people as its target
readership was Walangkar’s VitalVidhvansak (Destroyer of
Brahmanical or Ceremonial
Pollution), which he started
publishing in 1888.
o He also wrote articles for Marathilanguage newspapers such as
Sudharak and Deenbandhu, as well as
composed couplets in Marathi that
were intended to inspire the people.
o In 1889, he published Vital
Viduvansan (Annihilation of
Ceremonial Pollution), which
protested the position of
untouchables in society and raised
consciousness regarding what those
people should expect.
o He addressed this pamphlet, which
was crafted as a collection of 26
questions, to the elites of
Maharashtrian society.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q1. In the first quarter of the seventeenth century, in
which of the following was/were the
factory/factories of the English East India Company
located?

Broach
Chicacole
3. Trichinopoly
Select the correct answer using the code given
below.
(a) 1 only
(b) 1 and 2
(c) 3 only
(d) 2 and 3
The correct option is (a).
• The English East India Company had a factory
at Broach (present-day Bharuch) in Gujarat,
India during the seventeenth century.
• Chicacole (present-day Srikakulam) and
Trichinopoly (present-day Tiruchirappalli)
were not among the locations of the
Company’s factories during this period.
• Establishment of Factories by English East
India Company:
o West Coast: The English India
Company established their factories
at Agra, Ahmadabad, Baroda and
Broach by 1619, under the control of
the president and council of the Surat
factory which was established in the
year 1612.
o South-eastern Coast: EIC established
their factories at Masulipatam in
1611 and Armagaon near Pulicat in
1626.
o Eastern India: In eastern India EIC
established their factories at
Hariharpur and Balasore (now
Odisha) in 1633, at Hugli in 1651,
followed by setting up factories at
Patna, Dacca, Kasimbazar in Bengal
and Bihar respectively.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
